---

## 1. Purpose

The software factory (binary name `sf`) is a long-running Rust program that turns approved GitHub issues into merged pull requests. For each issue it prepares an isolated workspace, runs a coding agent to plan and implement the change, validates the result with checks the agent does not control, publishes a pull request, and merges it once the required human and automated gates have passed.

Humans steer the factory by writing issues and specs, approving work, and reviewing pull requests. They do not write the code.

### 1.1 Goals

- **G1.** Take an approved issue to a mergeable pull request without human involvement between approval and review.
- **G2.** Keep GitHub as the only database of record. Losing the factory host loses no work item, decision or approval.
- **G3.** Make wrong or malicious output hard to merge: the agent that builds a change never judges it, never holds a GitHub credential, and cannot alter the checks that gate it.
- **G4.** Make cost and quality visible per issue and per merged pull request.
- **G5.** Build the factory with itself from milestone M1 (attended) and M2 (unattended) onward (section 21).
- **G6.** Reach lights-out operation by evidence. Every class of work starts with the operator reviewing each pull request, and is promoted to merging without review only on its measured record (13.6). The operator always approves each issue.

### 1.2 Non-goals

- A second issue tracker, or any store that holds work items GitHub does not.
- A general workflow engine. The pipeline is fixed in code; only stage settings are data.
- A multi-user or hosted service. This is a personal factory: one person runs one daemon on their own machine, signed in to the agent CLI with their own account. The factory never handles anyone else's model credentials.
- Several agents writing to one branch. Parallelism comes from independent issues.
- Merging without review before it is earned (G6, 13.6).
- Opening issues or pull requests on repositories the operator has not added to the factory.
- Working on issues written by anyone but the operator (SEC-2).
- Releasing or deploying. The factory's job ends at merge; it never holds a publishing or deployment credential.
- Running on macOS or Windows. The host is a Linux server (4.4).

### 1.3 Design principles

These come from the practice of teams that run such systems (report 01) and each one constrains the design below.

1. **The control plane is deterministic code.** An LLM never chooses the next lifecycle stage. Agents run inside stages; code sequences the stages and enforces the gates.
2. **One issue, one workspace, one writer, one small pull request.**
3. **A session is a cache, never state.** Every stage is restartable from the issue, the branch and handoff files alone.
4. **The builder is not the judge.** Verification runs on a clean copy of the committed tree; review is done by a separate agent with a clean context; acceptance is a human decision by default.
5. **Propose, validate, apply.** The agent produces files and a structured result. The control plane validates them and performs every external write.
6. **Assume prompt injection succeeds.** Safety comes from what the agent process cannot reach, not from what the model is told.
7. **Budgets are enforced outside the model.**
8. **Every stage is switchable and measured**, so a stage that stops catching problems can be removed.

The terms this specification uses are defined once, in `GLOSSARY.md` at the root of the factory repository, and are used here as defined there (D-21). The file cites the ids and section numbers of this specification.

## 3. System overview

```
                         GitHub (system of record)
        issues · labels · dependencies · branches · PRs · check runs
                 ▲                                   ▲
   read (poll, ETag)                      every write (rate-governed)
                 │                                   │
┌────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────┴─────────────────┐
│ CONTROL PLANE (trusted)                                    sf daemon │
│                                                                      │
│  GitHub gateway ── Reconciler ── Engine (pure state machine)         │
│                                    │                                 │
│  Scheduler · Budgets · Journal (SQLite) · Policy loader              │
│  Git publisher · Integrity checker · Merge coordinator               │
│  Egress proxy · Model gateway · Control API (Unix socket)            │
└───────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                │ spawns and supervises; reads the event stream and results
┌───────────────▼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ WORKER (untrusted, one sandbox instance per stage execution)         │
│  agent CLI ─ shell, build, tests ─ workspace or candidate export     │
│  no GitHub credential · scrubbed environment · default-deny network  │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

### 3.1 Invariants

These hold in every mode and milestone. A change that breaks one is a defect.

- **INV-1.** No GitHub credential ever enters a worker, and none appears in the arguments, environment or files of any child process.
- **INV-2.** The control plane never executes anything from a workspace and never reads configuration from one: no hooks, no workspace `.git/config`, no agent settings, no `.cargo/config.toml` or `rust-toolchain.toml`. It does not open paths inside a workspace. Its only access is an `lstat`-only walk and git commands run inside a sandbox (9.4).
- **INV-3.** Every command that builds or runs repository code, and every git command whose work tree is a workspace, runs inside a sandbox.
- **INV-4.** A run starts only on an action by an authorised actor whose permission was checked through the GitHub API at that moment. There is no exception: every issue the factory works on was approved individually.
- **INV-5.** The factory identity never approves a pull request and never bypasses a repository rule.
- **INV-6.** At most one non-terminal run exists per (repository, issue), and at most one stage execution per run at a time.
- **INV-7.** All external side effects are idempotent and keyed (8.5). Repeating any step after a crash produces no duplicate branch, pull request, comment, check run or issue.
- **INV-8.** The local store is a rebuildable cache. If it disagrees with GitHub, GitHub wins.
- **INV-9.** Text from issues, pull requests, comments, logs and agents is data. It is never interpolated into a shell command or a control-plane decision, and it is encoded before being written back to GitHub (GH-5a).
- **INV-10.** The policy and agent instructions that govern a run are read from a default-branch commit, never from the workspace or the work branch.
- **INV-11.** `verify` and `review` never see the workspace. They run on a clean export of the candidate commit, in a fresh sandbox instance with a fresh home directory. Each stage execution of either has an export of its own (WS-14). Nothing one stage execution writes is visible to a later one except the workspace and the implementer target directory (both visible to implementer rounds only), the candidate commit and the structured result.

---

## 4. Deployment prerequisites and modes

### 4.1 Enforcement mode

GitHub rulesets are what stop a faulty or compromised control plane from pushing straight to the default branch. They are not available on private repositories owned by a Free personal account (verified on the operator's account; report 07). The factory detects one of two modes per repository at startup, daily, and when a ruleset changes:

| Mode | Condition | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| `enforced` | `GET /repos/{o}/{r}/rules/branches/{default}` returns active `pull_request`, `required_status_checks`, `non_fast_forward` and `deletion` rules whose parameters match section 20.3, with the factory checks bound to the factory App. DEP-5 says how the rules of more than one ruleset are read | GitHub enforces pull requests, approvals and required checks. |
| `factory-enforced` | The call returns 403, or the rules are missing or weaker | Only the factory enforces the gates. This is a weaker guarantee. |

- **DEP-1.** The mode MUST be shown in `sf status`, in the status comment and in every pull request body. M1a builds no detection of the mode (22), so before M1b a pull request body shows it only where the run holds it (VAL-14a).
- **DEP-2.** In `factory-enforced` mode the factory MUST itself verify, immediately before merging, every condition the ruleset would have enforced (12.3), and autonomy is capped at A2 (13.6).
- **DEP-3.** `enforced` mode requires a public repository, or a private repository on GitHub Pro, Team or Enterprise. The spec recommends `enforced` for any repository the factory runs on unattended. The operator's repositories are public (decision D-1), so they run in `enforced` mode, which auto-merge requires (13.6).
- **DEP-4.** The App has no Administration permission, so it cannot read a ruleset's bypass list. `sf repo check` reads it once with the operator's own credential (not stored) and records the result; it repeats when the ruleset's `updated_at` changes. Until then the mode is shown as `enforced (bypass list unverified)`. The accepted bypass list is fixed by 20.3, and a ruleset with no bypass actor is accepted too (DEP-5); the factory App is never on it. If a list read is neither of the two, or contains the factory App, the mode is `factory-enforced` until a later `sf repo check` passes. At every mode detection the daemon reads `updated_at` of each contributing ruleset (ids come from the branch-rules response); when it changes, the mode reverts to `enforced (bypass list unverified)` until the operator runs `sf repo check` again. If probe P-14 shows `current_user_can_bypass` is populated for installation tokens, any value other than `never` on a contributing ruleset makes the mode `factory-enforced`.
- **DEP-5.** More than one ruleset may apply to the default branch (D-41). The call lists a rule once for each ruleset that holds it, with that ruleset's `ruleset_id`, and merges nothing. A bypass reaches the rules of its own ruleset and no rule of another. On the scratch repository a pull request of the bypass actor's merged without the approval and the checks of the ruleset that named the actor; that ruleset's rules against a force push and a deletion were not tried. With a second ruleset that named no bypass actor, the merge was refused, over REST and over GraphQL, until that ruleset's check was green (P-38). A further ruleset therefore adds to what GitHub asks and takes nothing away, and it is no reason for `factory-enforced`. For each rule of 20.3 the factory looks for an entry that matches it, in a ruleset whose bypass list is the one that 20.3 fixes or is empty. Such a ruleset is a contributing ruleset of DEP-4. A second entry of the same type with other parameters, as the pull-request rule of DEV-8 with its zero approvals, is not a weaker rule and does not change the mode. Neither does a rule that 20.3 does not name. Such a rule can ask more than the factory gives: a required check that `required_checks` does not list (VAL-8), or signed commits (5.6), stops the factory's merge, and the mode does not show it. What the factory reports of such a rule is left open. That GitHub holds an account without a bypass to the rules of both rulesets was not observed: the scratch repository has one human account.

### 4.2 Approval

The factory has one human. The approver of an issue also reviews and approves its pull request, and review is requested from that approver. There is no separation between approving the work and approving the code (23.3).

### 4.3 Capability probe

At startup and daily, the factory probes each repository and records what is available: rulesets, native merge queue, issue types, issue fields, sub-issues, issue dependencies. Features are used only when the probe finds them; each has a fallback (section 5). The label read of GH-3a is part of the probe and has no fallback.

### 4.4 Host requirements

- A Linux server on the operator's own hardware (x86-64 or arm64) with KVM available to the factory's user. macOS and Windows are not supported hosts. The workspace still compiles on macOS for development, with the sandbox backends compiled out; there only sandbox level `none` with the fake agent works. Linux-only code is developed in a local Linux VM (DEV-3).
- Unprivileged user namespaces enabled, and `bubblewrap` 0.9.0 or later installed. That is the version of the hosted runners (P-16), the older of the two whose manual holds every option that 13.3 names (D-40).
- `git` ≥ 2.47.3, the oldest version that the hostile-tree suite ran on (P-7). The data directory on its own volume if possible.
- An agent CLI (v1: Claude Code; 10.3), from M1a in a version of the tested range (AG-5).
- The daemon runs as a dedicated system user under systemd, by default `sf`. Its home directory is `/home/sf` by default and can be any other path (CFG-4). The operator controls it through GitHub and through `sf` over SSH; there is no remote client.
- A run does not start when the sandbox backend for its required level is unavailable (13.3).

**The operator's server.** Observed on 2026-11-20 by a script that the operator ran over SSH as their own account, without root (`docs/research/14-server-facts.md`). The server is not named here, and no address is given. The operator then installed `bubblewrap` and Claude Code and ran the script again the same day; what that run added is marked "second run" below. A third run that day was made as the factory's system user, which the operator had created as a member of group `kvm`. Both are recorded under P-24 in `docs/research/probes.md`. An observation made later is added with its own date.

|---|---|---|

| `/dev/kvm` | Present as `crw-rw---- root kvm`, with `kvm_intel` loaded. The operator's account is not in group `kvm` and could not open the device. Third run: the factory's user opened it, and `KVM_GET_API_VERSION` and `KVM_CREATE_VM` succeeded; no guest was booted (P-24) | Met |

Provisioning the server (22, M0) therefore still includes an agent CLI and a toolchains directory with a toolchain in it (WS-7), both of which the factory's user can read, and the data directory, which `/srv` has room for. For M0's exit check (BOOT-3) it also includes the operator configuration (16.4) and the file of the subscription token (13.5), both written by hand until `sf init` exists (CLI-7); the directory `/opt/sf` that an installed build lies in (DEV-13); and, for the operator's own account, the toolchain and the check tools that `mise.toml` pins, so that the check set runs there on a tagged commit (DEV-7), with the git setting of WS-15 in that account's global configuration. By the onboarding of the factory repository (BOOT-6) it also includes the tools directory, with the check tools that the repository's policy names (WS-7a).

## 5. GitHub data model

### 5.1 What lives where

| Information | Location | Written by |
|---|---|---|
| Work item, acceptance criteria | Issue title and body | Humans (the factory never rewrites an issue body) |
| Lifecycle state | One `state:*` label | Humans and the factory per 7.2 |
| Classification | Issue type; fallback `type:*` label | Humans |
| Priority | `priority:p0`–`p3` label; the `Priority` issue field once probe P-17 shows field changes are attributable | Humans |
| Decomposition | Native sub-issues | The factory from an approved plan, or humans |
| Ordering | Native blocked-by dependencies | The factory from an approved plan, or humans |
| Run summary and durable machine state | One status comment per issue (5.4) | Factory |
| Code | Branch `factory/issue-<n>` and its pull request | Factory |
| Gate verdicts | Check runs on the pull request head SHA (5.5) | Factory and CI |
| Provenance | Commit trailers, squash message and pull request body (5.6) | Factory |
| Journal, deadlines, transcripts | Local store and run directories (section 18) | Factory |

Projects v2 and GitHub's native granular issue status are not used. Four label states correspond to native status values (triage→Backlog, ready→Todo, in-progress→In progress, in-review→In review); `state:needs-human` has no native equivalent and would remain a label if native status were adopted.

### 5.2 Labels

**State labels.** An issue is a work item if and only if it is open and carries exactly one of these:

| Label | Meaning |
|---|---|
| `state:triage` | Not yet approved for work. |
| `state:ready` | Approved (or resumed). The factory may claim or resume it. |
| `state:in-progress` | Claimed by a run. |
| `state:in-review` | A pull request is ready for human review or is merging. |
| `state:needs-human` | The run stopped and waits for a human. |

Any closed issue is terminal: closed as completed, as not planned, or as duplicate. Every close other than through a merged factory pull request is a cancellation (7.7).

**Human inputs** (honoured only under SEC-1):

| Label | Effect |
|---|---|
| `state:ready` | Start (5.3) or resume (7.5.1). A human need only add it; the factory removes the previous state label. |
| `factory:hold` | Freezes the item in any state. No new stage starts; a running stage execution is interrupted at the next tick. A held run releases its concurrency slot, and time on hold counts toward no timer or failure counter. It stays in force until an authorised actor removes it. |
| `factory:restart` | Resets the run (7.6). Removed by the factory once acted on. |
| `factory:plan-approved` | Approves the plan posted for this issue (7.4). |
| `factory:stop` | On a designated open issue: stops the repository (13.7). |
| `tier:0|1|2` | Work tier (6.1). No tier label means tier 1. |
| `risk:low|medium|high` | Risk. No risk label means `risk:medium`. |
| `priority:p0`–`p3` | Dispatch order. |
| `type:ci`, `type:spec-delta` | Change classes with their own integrity handling (11.3): `type:spec-delta` permits changes under `docs/specs/**`; `type:ci` turns a workflow change into a patch for a human to commit. |

Through M1 `sf run` reads these labels when ORCH-9 says, and ORCH-9 says which of them it honours without the check of SEC-1.

**Factory-set labels:** `needs:clarification`, `needs:decision`, `factory:origin` (on factory pull requests and factory-filed issues), `factory:protected-change` (11.3). These are indicators only and are never read back as authority (SEC-1b).

- **GH-1.** Labels MUST be changed with the add-label and remove-label endpoints, never by replacing the whole label set. A swap adds the new label before removing the old one.
- **GH-2.** If the reconciler finds zero or several `state:*` labels on an issue it knows, it MUST normalise to a single state derived from GitHub evidence and the journal, and record the correction in the status comment. Normalisation never produces `state:ready`. A human-applied `state:ready` next to another state label is not a conflict: it is the start or resume signal.
- **GH-3.** `state:ready` MUST NOT appear in the `labels` key of any issue form, so that opening an issue can never approve it.
- **GH-3a.** Before its first label write on a repository, the factory lists the repository's labels (`GET /repos/{o}/{r}/labels`, which the App's Issues permission covers: the scratch App's token read it in probe P-25) and compares their names with the labels of this section (D-26): the five state labels, the human inputs, with every name of `tier:*`, `risk:*` and `priority:*` and the two `type:*` labels, and the factory-set labels. Where one is missing the factory claims no issue on that repository, and it names every missing label when it refuses. The factory MUST NOT add a label that the read did not find. The REST API creates a label that is added by name, grey and without a description, and answers with its ordinary success, for the App's token as for the operator's (P-25), so a skipped step 5 of 20.1 would otherwise show as grey labels and never as a failed call. A name is compared exactly as this section spells it. A label that differs from a label of this section only in letter case counts as missing, and the refusal names both spellings: each of the four calls that take label names applied the existing label under its own name when it was sent the other spelling (P-25), and SEC-1 reads the events of the exact label. The names compared are those of the list, since a read of one label by its name returns it under either spelling. Colour and description are not compared. `sf run` makes the read at its start, from M1a. From M2 the read is part of the capability probe (4.3). `sf repo check` reports the same comparison.

### 5.3 Readiness

An issue is dispatchable when all of these hold:

1. It is open and carries `state:ready`, valid under SEC-1.
2. It has no `factory:hold` label.
3. Every issue it is blocked by is closed. (Without native dependencies, the fallback is a `Blocked by: #n` line in the issue body.)
4. It passes the readiness lint (6.4).
5. The repository's review queue is below its limit (8.3).
6. Its title and body have not been edited since the `labeled` event that applied the current `state:ready`. An edit counts as since the event when its time is the event's own second or a later one. The times are the body's `lastEditedAt`, which a title change does not move, and that of the title's last rename event. GitHub reports whole seconds, and a body edit in the event's second cannot be placed before it or after it; the title is held to the same rule. One command that edits an issue and applies the label landed both in one second in every trial of P-8, and the issue then fails this rule. The title and the body of the snapshot and `lastEditedAt` come from one GraphQL query, the first read of GH-21, so the check and the snapshot cannot diverge. The last rename event and the `labeled` event are the most recent of their kind among all the events of the issue, which the claim reads to their end. Each is on the first read unless 100 later events follow it, and what is added to the issue after the first read is on no older page (GH-21). On failure the factory removes `state:ready`, sets `state:triage` and says so once.
7. SEC-2 admits its author and every editor of its title and body. The claim reads who they are as GH-21 says. On failure the factory does what it does for rule 6, and what it says names the account and where it stands: as the author, as the editor of a body edit, with the time of that edit, or as the actor of a rename. Where the rule failed because the edit history is not known to be whole, it says that (GH-21).

Through M1 a rule that fails writes nothing to GitHub: the claim ends, and `sf run` says which rule failed and why (ORCH-11).

Dispatch order is priority, then creation date, oldest first.

- **GH-21. What the claim reads of an issue (D-45).** Rule 6, rule 7, SEC-1 and the snapshot read three lists of an issue that have no fixed length: its edit history, its events and its comments. The claim reads each from its newest end.
  - **The first read.** One GraphQL query takes the issue's state, title, body, `lastEditedAt`, author and labels, and with them the newest 100 entries of each list: `userContentEdits(first: 100)`, which lists the newest entry first; `timelineItems(last: 100)` for the rename events and the `labeled` and `unlabeled` events; and `comments(last: 100)`. The events and the comments come oldest first, so a query that asks for the first 100 of either gets the oldest: of 111 events it returned the first 100, without the last rename and the last `labeled` event (P-8). The query asks each list for `pageInfo`, and the comments also for `totalCount`. A query for the three lists, with the title, the body and the author of the issue, cost one point of the GraphQL rate limit (P-8).
  - **Older pages.** Where the page of the events or of the comments reports a page before it (`hasPreviousPage`), the claim asks for that page with `last: 100` and, as `before`, the `startCursor` of the page it holds, and it goes on so from page to page. It reads the events to their end. SEC-2 asks for the actor of every rename, and SEC-1 for the most recent `labeled` event of a label, which is on an older page where 100 later events follow it. It reads the comments as far back as the walk of SEC-4 goes, and for at most 10 pages *(tunable)*, the first read among them. Comments before those pages are left out, and SEC-4 says how the snapshot counts them.
  - **An older page does not depend on when it is asked.** The page before a cursor was the same after the issue had gained an edit, a rename, a comment and a label event, and after the comment that the cursor names was deleted (P-8). So what is added to the issue after the first read is on no older page, and it is a change during a run, which 7.7 is for. An item of an older page that is itself changed between two reads of one claim, a comment that is edited, minimised or deleted, was not observed. The claim takes such an item as it is when its page is read.
  - **The edit history ends at 100 entries.** The API ended the list there: the text as created and the newest 99 edits. After 105 body edits the six oldest were in no response, an edit by the scratch App among them, while `totalCount` was 100 and no further page was offered. One more edit took the next one out (P-8). A history of 100 entries is therefore not known to be whole, and nothing in the response tells it from one that is.
  - **When a list is whole.** The claim holds every event when a page reports no page before it. It holds the whole edit history when the list has fewer than 100 entries and reports no further page, and SEC-2 says what follows where it does not. `totalCount` is no test for a whole list of events. It counts the list as it is when the page is asked, and it moved between two pages of one read (P-8). A page that cannot be fetched is a failure of the request (8.6) and no failure of a rule.
  - **Who reads so.** The claim. Through M1 the later reads of `sf run` take none of the three lists (ORCH-9). How far back the resume of 7.5.1 and the reconciler of 7.7 read, which ask who edited since the last snapshot, is said when M2 builds them.
  - **Not taken.** One page of each list, and no claim of an issue that has more. M1a would then build no paging. Every cancel and new approval of M1 adds about six label events (ORCH-9), so an issue is past 100 after some 16 of them, and the scratch issue that M1a is developed against would be. Checking the editors that a full edit history still names, and running the issue, was not taken either: an edit by another account that 99 later edits pushed out of the list would never be seen.

### 5.4 Status comment

Each run has exactly one status comment on its issue, authored by the factory's App identity, created at claim and edited in place afterwards. It starts with a hidden marker block, followed by a human-readable part (current stage, attempt, round counts, spend, links to the pull request and checks, last error, enforcement mode, the number of comments left out of the snapshot (SEC-4)).

```
<!-- factory:state v1
{"run_id":"…","attempt":1,"stage":"implement","branch":"factory/issue-42","pr":57,
 "base_sha":"…","head_sha":"…","policy_sha":"…",
 "snapshot_sha256":"…","title_body_sha256":"…","tier":1,"risk":"medium",
 "plan_sha256":null,"plan_approved_by":null,"locked_paths_sha256":null,
 "escalation":null,
 "spend_usd":3.41,"budget_grants":0,
 "rounds":{"plan":0,"verify":1,"ci":0,"review":0,"human":0,"merge_conflict":0},
 "size_strikes":0,"last_failure":"…fingerprint…","children":[],
 "factory_version":"0.3.1"}
-->
```

This field list is normative for `v1`. `escalation` is `{"category","seq"}` while the run is in `needs-human`. `children` (tier 2 parents only) is the list of sub-issue numbers. `head_sha` is written on the transition from `publish` to `ci`. `snapshot_sha256` is taken over the snapshot as sanitised, and `title_body_sha256` over the title and the body as read from GitHub (SEC-4).

- **GH-4.** The marker is written only on stage transitions, on escalations and when the run reaches a terminal state (`"stage":"done"` or `"cancelled"`). Heartbeats and deadlines MUST NOT be written to GitHub.
- **GH-5.** The status comment is the comment whose id is in the journal or, on rebuild, the most recently created App-authored comment whose body starts with the marker block; earlier ones belong to terminal runs of the same issue. A marker whose stage is `done` or `cancelled` reconstructs no run. A marker anywhere else, including other App-authored comments, issue bodies and pull request bodies, is ignored. Dedupe markers (8.5) are recognised only as the first line of a comment or issue body authored by the App.
- **GH-5a.** All text that originates from an agent, an issue, a review or a log and is written to GitHub passes through one outbound encoder. It caps length, renders the text inside a fenced block labelled as agent output (except sub-issue bodies, PLAN-5), and neutralises `<!--`, `-->`, @mentions and issue references. Agent text never starts a body. The fence is longer than every run of backticks in the text, because text that ends its fence is live from there on. A reference is any of:
  - a `#` that a digit follows, which covers `#<digits>` and `owner/repo#<digits>`;
  - `GH-<digits>`, compared without regard to case;
  - `<owner>/<repo>/issues/<digits>` or `<owner>/<repo>/pull/<digits>`, with or without a scheme and a host before it, since GitHub takes the issue path alone for a reference.

  The encoder neutralises by inserting a zero-width space (U+200B), in fenced and unfenced text alike: after every `@`; after the `#` of the first form; after the hyphen of the second; before the number of the third, and after the colon of its scheme, so that no link to a wrong address is left; and after every `&` that a `#` or an ASCII letter follows. The last is because GitHub decodes a character reference before it looks for a mention or a reference, so that `&#64;`, `&#x40;` and `&commat;` are each a live `@`. A backslash escape or a character reference MUST NOT be used instead of the zero-width space: on a comment, an issue body and a pull request body neither stops a notification, a cross-reference or a closing (P-15). A commit message is not Markdown: a fence stops nothing there, and `&#<digits>;` is a reference to issue `<digits>`, under the first form. Nothing invisible is put into a commit subject, so a subject that contains a reference or a skip token (9.4) is rejected instead. A closing keyword is left as it is everywhere. It acts only through a reference directly after it, so a subject such as `fix: handle empty input` passes.

  The encoder is a pure function of `factory-core` (19.1, D-33): text in, text out, with nothing read from GitHub. `factory-github` passes every such text through it before a write. On the same way `factory-github` makes the searches of the literal-token scan, one of them on such a text before the encoder changes it (SEC-13). Capture, which cannot depend on `factory-github`, takes the forms of a reference from the same module for the subjects it rejects (9.4), so the list above is written once in code as it is here. M1a builds the encoder whole (22), because its draft pull request already carries such text (VAL-14a). The human-readable part of the status comment (5.4) and the output of a check run (5.5), which M1b writes, pass through it as every other surface does. Probe P-15 wrote to no check run, as its record says, so whether a mention or a reference is live in that output was not observed. The rule does not wait for the answer. GitHub documents none of what the encoder rests on, and the forms that the probe did not write, which its record lists, are encoded on the strength of the surface next to them. A test therefore holds the encoder against GitHub itself, and fails when a neutralised mention or reference renders as a link (19.4, D-46).
- **GH-5b.** On recovery and rebuild, `policy_sha` and `base_sha` must be ancestors of the default-branch head; counters and spend from a marker are lower bounds; the resumed stage is the earlier of the marker's stage and what GitHub evidence supports (8.4).
- **GH-5c.** How a path is written to GitHub (D-33). A path of a diff is text that an agent chose, and git allows any byte in one but NUL. Wherever the host writes such a path to GitHub as a path, each takes one line inside the fence of GH-5a: in the flag listing of VAL-3, in the pull request body and in the summary of a check run (GH-8), and among the files of item 5 of 11.6. A line holds the path. For a symbolic link an arrow, `->`, and the link's target follow it (VAL-5f), and where the listing names the pattern that a path matched (VAL-3), the pattern follows in parentheses. Where it counts what a file was flagged for, as it does for the dependency rule (VAL-5g), the number follows in parentheses. A line of the summary of VAL-3b holds a pattern in the place of the path, and the number of paths that it matched follows in parentheses. One space stands between the parts.
  - A path, a target and a pattern are each written in one form. It is the text as it is, unless the text is empty, is not well-formed UTF-8, or holds a space, a backslash, a double quote or a character of the escaped set. Then it is written between double quotes, with the escapes of the table. A space between the quotes stands for itself.
  - The escaped set is named by Unicode properties and not by a list: every code point of the general categories Cc and Cf, which are the control and the format characters; of Zl and Zp, the line and the paragraph separator; of Zs other than the space U+0020; and every code point with the property Default_Ignorable_Code_Point. Among them are the characters that take no width and those that set the direction of text. The tables are those of the crate `icu_properties`, at the pin and the Unicode version of VAL-5d (19.2).

    | In the text | Written as |
    |---|---|
    | A backslash | `\\` |
    | A double quote | `\"` |
    | A line feed, a tab, a carriage return | `\n`, `\t`, `\r` |
    | Any other character of the escaped set | `\u{…}`: the code point in hexadecimal, in capital digits and without a leading zero |
    | A byte that is no part of well-formed UTF-8 | `\x` and two hexadecimal digits, in capitals |

    | Path | Line of the listing |
    |---|---|
    | `src/lib.rs`, `docs/élan.md` | As it is |
    | `a`, a line feed, `b.rs` | `"a\nb.rs"` |
    | `x`, U+202E, `gpj.exe` | `"x\u{202E}gpj.exe"` |
    | `a`, the bell character U+0007 | `"a\u{7}"` |
    | `bad`, the byte FF, `name` | `"bad\xFFname"` |
    | `my notes.md` | `"my notes.md"` |
    | `.cargo`, a link to `tools/cfg` | `.cargo -> tools/cfg` |
    | `clippy.toml`, a link with an empty target, matched by the pattern `clippy.toml` | `clippy.toml -> "" (clippy.toml)` |
    | In the summary of VAL-3b, the pattern `my notes/**`, which matched three paths | `"my notes/**" (3)` |
    | `AGENTS.md`, as a file of the computed instruction set | `AGENTS.md ([instruction-set])` |

  - A written path is therefore one line, and it holds no character that Unicode counts as a control, a format character, a separator other than the space, or one to ignore by default. Without the escape a line break in a path would forge a second entry, and a character that reverses the direction of text would show one path as another. No part of a line holds a space outside quotes, so the parts cannot run into each other: a path that ends in an arrow is in quotes, and the arrow of a link is not. A capture that refuses such a path was not taken in place of the escape: a diff can still delete one that is in the base tree, and the listing has to name it.
  - A backtick stands for itself. The fence of GH-5a is longer than every run of backticks in what it holds, so a path cannot end it.
  - A letter outside ASCII is written as it is, so a path stays readable. A letter of another script that looks like a Latin one is not told apart, and that is accepted.
  - The escape is applied first and the encoder of GH-5a after it. The encoder inserts its zero-width space into the written line as into any text, after an `@` for example. A path's own zero-width space is `\u{200B}` by then, so one that a reader finds in a listing is the encoder's. A path copied out of a listing can carry it.
  - Where the encoder's cap on length shortens a listing, it cuts between two lines and never inside one. The lines it drops are counted among those the listing does not name (VAL-3).
  - The form is a pure function of `factory-core`, beside the encoder (19.1).
- **GH-6.** Readers MUST preserve unknown fields. The `v1` version tag changes only on incompatible changes.
- **GH-7.** Apart from the status comment, a run may create a bounded number of comments (default 6 *(tunable)*): the plan, one per escalation, and one when work completes. Progress never goes into new comments.

### 5.5 Check runs

The factory reports three check runs on every head SHA it publishes, with `external_id` set to the run id:

| Check | Content |
|---|---|
| `factory/integrity` | Protected-path and tampering policy (11.3). |
| `factory/verify` | Result of the repository's verification commands on the candidate export (11.2). |
| `factory/review` | Per-criterion verdict of the reviewer agent (11.5). |

- **GH-8.** Conclusions: gate passed → `success`; integrity `flag` → `neutral` with the flagged rules in the summary; stage disabled by policy, not applicable to the tier, or not yet implemented → `skipped` with the reason; gate failed → `failure`. GitHub treats `success`, `neutral` and `skipped` as satisfying a required check, and so does 12.3.
- **GH-9.** A factory check is reported for a SHA only when the gate ran on exactly that commit's tree. 12.3 and 8.4 accept a factory check only when its `external_id` is the run id.
- **GH-10.** In `enforced` mode the ruleset requires the three factory checks, bound to the factory App as their source, plus the repository's CI checks. Because required checks apply to every pull request, the handling of human-authored pull requests is defined in 20.3.

Repository CI reports its own checks through GitHub Actions (11.4).

### 5.6 Branch, pull request and commits

- Branch: `factory/issue-<n>`, one per issue, created by the factory and owned by it. It only ever fast-forwards, except on a reset (7.6), which force-updates it with a lease.
- Pull request: at most one open pull request per branch, opened as a draft at first publish, targeting the default branch, with `Closes #<n>` in the body and the label `factory:origin`. A reset closes the old pull request and opens a new one that links it.
- Commit author and committer: the App's bot identity (`<id>+<slug>[bot]@users.noreply.github.com`). Dates come from the engine's injected clock and are journalled.
- Commit message: a one-line subject from the agent's result, validated (9.4); the body and trailers are written by the host. Trailers: `Factory-Issue: <n>`, `Factory-Run: <run id>`, `Factory-Agent: <cli>/<version> <model>`, and `Co-authored-by:` naming the approver.
- Merge method: squash. The factory passes an explicit, host-generated squash title and message (with the trailers) in the merge call, so the default squash text never pulls in pull request body text. With merge strategy `human` or `native_queue` the trailers on the default branch are not guaranteed and the pull request body is the provenance record.

v1 publishes with `git push` over HTTPS, so commits are unsigned. A ruleset that requires signed commits is incompatible with v1. Publishing through GraphQL `createCommitOnBranch` (GitHub-signed) is a later option limited to change sets it can express.

- **GH-22. No rule on the work branch (D-51).** The factory's App is on no bypass list (GH-11), so a rule that GitHub applies to the name `factory/issue-<n>` binds every write that the factory makes to that branch. A repository is fit for the factory only where GitHub lists no rule for the name of a work branch. On the scratch repository each rule that a ruleset on `factory/**` was given stopped at least one of those writes, for the scratch App (P-45):

  | Rule | The create of the ref (8.2) | The push of a candidate (9.4) | The push of a merge commit (12.4) | A force-update of the ref (7.6, 8.2) | The delete of the ref (12.3, 18.2) |
  |---|---|---|---|---|---|
  | `creation` | Refused | Accepted | Accepted | Accepted | Accepted |
  | `update` | Accepted | Refused | Refused | Refused | Accepted |
  | `deletion` | Accepted | Accepted | Accepted | Accepted | Refused |
  | `non_fast_forward` | Accepted | Accepted | Accepted | Refused | Accepted |
  | `required_linear_history` | Refused at the head of the default branch, which has merge commits behind it. Accepted at a commit that has none | Accepted | Refused | Accepted | Accepted |
  | `required_signatures` | Accepted, at a commit that is not signed | Refused | Refused | Refused as the push of a new commit. Accepted as an update through the REST API to a commit that the default branch holds | Accepted |
  | `pull_request` | Accepted | Refused | Refused | Refused | Accepted |
  | `required_status_checks` | Refused. Accepted where the rule sets `do_not_enforce_on_create` | Refused | Refused | Refused | Accepted |

  A force-update was sent in two ways in each row: as a push with a lease of a commit that the ref's head is no ancestor of, and as `PATCH /git/refs` with `force`.
  - **What GitHub says of a refusal.** A refused create answers `422 Reference update failed` under each of the three rules that refuse it, `creation`, `required_linear_history` and `required_status_checks`, and names none. It is the message of a branch named `factory` in the way (8.2). A refused push names its rule in the lines that git prints from the remote: `GH013: Repository rule violations found for refs/heads/factory/issue-<n>`, and below it a line such as `Cannot update this protected ref.` A refused update or delete of the ref through the REST API names it in the `message` of its 422.
  - **The read.** `GET /repos/{o}/{r}/rules/branches/factory/issue-<n>`, which the App's Metadata permission covers. GitHub's documentation says that the branch need not exist and that the rules of every active ruleset are returned, and the scratch App's token read each rule of the table for a name that no ref had. An entry holds the rule's `type` and, where the rule has them, its `parameters`, and it names its ruleset, as `ruleset_id`, `ruleset_source_type` and `ruleset_source`. The read listed the rule of a ruleset whose condition names every branch (`~ALL`) and the rule of one that names a bypass actor. It listed nothing for a ruleset that is disabled, under which the create went through. The documentation gives the response pages of 30 entries, which was not observed: the factory asks for 100 and reads the pages to their end. The operator's own token read the same list as the App's, entry for entry, also where the operator's role may bypass the ruleset, so the read can be made by hand (20.1). What a claim makes of the 403 that 4.1 names for a repository without rulesets is left open: the operator's repositories are public (D-1), and no such repository was read. Until it is decided that answer is a failure of the request (8.6).
  - **Which rules count.** Every entry of the read, whatever its type: a type that the table does not hold, and one that the build does not know, count as the others do. Not taken: the six types that stopped the create or a push that is no force-update, with `non_fast_forward` and `deletion` as advisory items. A ruleset that keeps force pushes and deletions from every branch would then leave the repository fit, and on it the ref of a cancelled run could not be used again (8.2) and the branch of a merged run would stay (18.2). `creation` alone was not taken either, nor an advisory item alone: a run would then meet the refusal at publish, after a whole implementer session.
  - **When the factory reads.** Each claim makes the read for the name of its own work branch, before the run row (8.2), from M1a and in the daemon alike. It is one request for each claim. A ruleset can be added after onboarding, and a pattern can cover one work branch and not another. Onboarding makes the read for the name `factory/issue-1`, and a rule there is a blocking item of the readiness report (20.1). `sf repo check` reports the same read. Not taken: the times of the label read (GH-3a), `sf run` at its start and from M2 the capability probe, which would leave a ruleset that is added between two probes to the 422 of the create; and onboarding alone.
  - **A claim whose read lists a rule ends there.** It leaves no run row and no ref, and it writes nothing to GitHub, as for a missing label (GH-3a). The issue keeps `state:ready`: the fault is the repository's and not the issue's. `sf run` names each entry by its type, the id of its ruleset and where that ruleset comes from, and says what the operator does about it, which is to exclude `refs/heads/factory/**` from the target of the ruleset. With that exclusion a ruleset for every branch listed nothing for a work branch, and the App's create, force-update and delete of one went through (P-45). From M2 the daemon then claims no issue on that repository, as for a missing label, also where the pattern covers that one work branch alone: a rule on any work branch is the operator's to remove. When the daemon makes the read again for such a repository is left open, to be said when M2 builds the dispatch (D-51). Not taken: an escalation of each such issue from M2, which the operator would resume one by one for a fault that is not the issue's.
  - **A rule that the read did not show.** A ruleset can be added between the read and a write, and the message of a refused create does not tell a rule from another cause. Every create that is refused while the ref is absent (8.2) is therefore followed by a second read of the rules. What is reported names each entry that it lists, or says that it listed none, beside GitHub's message. The failure stays the Deterministic one of 8.2: the run row exists by then, so through M1 the run stops (ORCH-11) and from M2 it is escalated, where a rule in the first read ends the claim with nothing written. A push, an update or a delete that GitHub refuses is a Deterministic failure too (8.6), and what is reported carries GitHub's lines, which name the rule. git reports a refused push as `[remote rejected]` on the line of the ref, which tells it from a push that did not reach GitHub. The remote's lines of a rule's refusal hold `GH013` under a ruleset and `GH006` under a classic rule (P-45). The refusal of a workflow held neither (P-5), and 12.4 keeps its own answer for the one push that it names, the update commit that is rejected for a workflow of the default branch.
  - **Classic branch protection is not read.** A classic branch protection rule is in no response of the read above. The scratch App was refused the reads that list classic rules, the GraphQL field `branchProtectionRules` and `GET /branches/{branch}/protection`. Of a branch that exists it read `protected` and, over GraphQL, the `refUpdateRule` of the ref with its pattern. For a name that no ref has there is nothing to read (P-45). On the scratch repository no classic rule on `factory/*` stopped the create. One that restricts who may push, and with it who may create, was not tried: GitHub refused to make it there, with `Only organization repositories can have users and team restrictions`. As created, with nothing chosen, such a rule stopped a force-update and the delete. With required reviews, or with a lock, it also stopped every push. Each refusal named its cause, a push under `GH006: Protected branch update failed`. So the readiness report holds an advisory item that says classic rules are not read (20.1), and the operator looks at them by hand. A run that meets one stops at the write that GitHub refuses, as a run does that meets a rule the read did not show. Not taken: a read of the `refUpdateRule` once the claim has created the ref, which would stop such a run before its session with the ref and the run row already made; and a read by `sf repo check` with the operator's own credential, as for a bypass list (DEP-4).
  - **Not observed.** A ruleset of an organisation: GitHub's documentation has the read return a rule at whichever level it is configured, and the operator's repositories belong to a personal account (D-1), which has no such ruleset. Every other rule type of GitHub's reference, which lists 25: three of them GitHub refused to create on the scratch repository, the rule for required deployments and the patterns for a commit message and for a branch name, and the others were not asked for, the merge queue, code scanning and the rules of a push ruleset among them (P-45). A ruleset in evaluate mode, which GitHub offers on another plan and which the documentation leaves out of the read. Since every entry counts, the factory needs to know none of these types.

### 5.7 Identity and permissions

The factory authenticates as a GitHub App. An installed build of `sf` authenticates as the factory's App, and a development build (BOOT-2) and the tests of 19.4 that write to the scratch repository as the scratch App (GH-11a). Both Apps hold the permissions below and no other.

| Permission | Level | Use |
|---|---|---|
| Metadata | read | Required |
| Contents | write | Push the work branch |
| Issues | write | Labels, status comment, follow-up issues, dependencies |
| Pull requests | write | Open, update, mark ready, merge |
| Checks | write | Report factory checks |
| Actions | read | Read CI results and logs |
| Commit statuses | read | Read legacy statuses |

- **GH-11.** The App MUST NOT hold the Workflows or Administration permission and MUST NOT be on any ruleset bypass list. Without Workflows, GitHub refuses the App a push that adds or changes a file under `.github/workflows/` at the repository root, and it refused the scratch App the addition of one through the REST API. Its refusal is narrower than the workflow rule of 11.3: it accepted a push that only removes a workflow or changes its mode, and it let the scratch App put a ref on a commit, and merge a branch, in which the operator had pushed a workflow (P-5). The integrity check does not rely on GitHub's refusal, and onboarding accounts for it (20.1).
- **GH-11a.** There are two Apps (D-38). The factory's App is installed on the target repositories, and its private key is on the server and nowhere else (13.5). The scratch App is installed on the scratch repository and on no other, and its private key is the only GitHub credential of the development VM (DEV-4). A private key belongs to an App and not to an installation: whoever holds it can have a token for every repository the App is installed on. With one App, the key in the development VM could write to the factory repository from M1b on, which BOOT-2 rules out for a development build. The factory's App MAY also be installed on the scratch repository, so that an installed build can be tried there from the server before it first writes to a target repository. The identity of a commit and of a comment is read from the credential (5.6), and this specification holds the name of neither App.
- **GH-11b.** Through M1 the operator creates each App by hand in GitHub's settings (D-38): owned by the operator's account and installable on that account only, with the permissions of the table above, with no webhook, since webhooks are not scheduled (22), and installed on selected repositories, never on all. From M1a `sf doctor` checks the permissions (CLI-1), so a slip made by hand is found; the scratch App's were read back by a script when it was created (`docs/research/17-scratch-app.md`). The scratch App is created in M0, before `sf` holds any GitHub code (22), so that a probe that needs an App identity can be answered by a script (23.2). The factory's App is created at the start of M1b, ahead of step 1 of 20.1 (BOOT-6): nothing that runs on the server in M0 or M1a writes to GitHub. `sf app create` and its manifest flow arrive in M5, with the rest of the onboarding automation (20.1). The flow needs a browser, which the server does not have.
- **GH-12.** Installation tokens are minted in the control plane, restricted to the one repository in use, cached until shortly before expiry, and treated as opaque strings of unknown length.

### 5.8 GitHub gateway

All GitHub traffic passes through one in-process gateway. The roadmap says which of its rules M1a builds for one foreground run and which arrive with the daemon in M2 (22, D-39).

- **GH-13.** Mutations go through a single serial queue with at least 1 s between requests. Nothing in the queue is dropped. A pending write to a comment or check run may only be superseded by a newer write to the same object.
- **GH-14.** Reads run with bounded concurrency (default 8), use an ETag cache with `If-None-Match`, and follow `x-ratelimit-*` and `retry-after` headers with exponential backoff and jitter. The HTTP client's own retry is disabled; every retry goes through the gateway.
- **GH-15.** Priority applies between runs, never within one: claim and merge first, then state transitions (which include marker writes), then check runs.
- **GH-16.** The gateway keeps content-creating requests under 250 per hour per installation *(tunable; GitHub's documented secondary limit is about 500 per hour and 80 per minute)* and pauses dispatch when the budget is nearly used.
- **GH-17.** REST is used for writes and simple reads, with a pinned `X-GitHub-Api-Version` (currently `2026-03-10`). GraphQL is used for composite reads (issue with labels, events, blockers, sub-issues, linked pull request and check rollup) and GraphQL-only mutations.

### 5.9 Event intake

- **GH-18.** The source of truth for change detection is a level-triggered poll: `GET /repos/{o}/{r}/issues?state=all&sort=updated&since=<watermark>` with an ETag, every 30 s *(tunable)*, refreshing only the items that changed. Open pull requests of active runs are polled for reviews and check results on the same tick. The default-branch commit produced by a factory merge is polled for its check results as well (12.3).
- **GH-19.** A full resync of all open work items runs every hour to catch anything the watermark missed.
- **GH-20.** The factory needs no inbound network endpoint. Webhooks are out of scope for v1; if added later they only enqueue "reconcile issue n now".

---

## 6. Work items and specs

### 6.1 Tiers

| Tier | Use | Required before `state:ready` | Plan gate |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | Chore: dependency bump, typo, formatting | Outcome and at least one acceptance criterion | No |
| 1 | Task or bug with one verifiable outcome | Full issue form (6.2) | Only if `risk:high` |
| 2 | Feature spanning several issues | A parent issue describing the feature | No: each proposed sub-issue is approved on its own |

A tier 2 parent is never implemented directly. It follows the parent pipeline (7.4): its plan proposes sub-issues, which the factory creates in `state:triage` for the operator to approve or close one by one.

### 6.2 Issue form

Task issues are created from an issue form (`.github/ISSUE_TEMPLATE/factory-task.yml`) that renders these sections, in this order, as level-3 headings:

| Section | Content |
|---|---|
| Outcome | One or two sentences: what is true when this is done. |
| Spec refs | Requirement ids and the spec file path, or "none". |
| Context | Crates, modules or files likely involved; links. |
| Acceptance criteria | A list of criteria, one to a line. Each has an id, a verification method and a statement in the form *WHEN ⟨condition⟩ THE SYSTEM SHALL ⟨behaviour⟩* (WI-3). |
| Out of scope | What must not change. |
| Stop conditions | Situations in which the agent must stop and ask. |

GitHub does not document the layout of the body a form produces. As observed (P-4), it is one block per field in the form's order, with a blank line between blocks: the line `### <label>`, a blank line, then the field's text exactly as submitted. A field left empty gets the text `_No response_`. Nothing in a field's text is escaped, nothing follows the last field's text, and nothing on the issue records that a form made it.

- **WI-1.** The factory parses the body with a tolerant section parser keyed on heading text. It reads the body after the sanitisation of SEC-4 and accepts either line ending. A section starts at a level-3 ATX heading, outside a fenced code block, whose text is one of the six names, compared without regard to case. It runs to the next such heading or to the end of the body. Any other heading is part of the section it stands in, and text before the first section belongs to none. A section's text is what lies between, without the lines at either end that hold nothing but white space. A section whose text is then empty, or is `_No response_` and white space only, is empty.
- **WI-1a.** A name that starts more than one section fails the readiness lint (6.4), and the factory uses no section of such a body. A heading typed into a field is the form's own heading in the body, so such a body does not say where the field ends, and neither the first nor the last section of a name is reliably the field (P-4).
- **WI-1b.** The four bodies that P-4 recorded, with the fields as submitted, are fixtures of the parser's tests: the parser returns every field of the two bodies made from ordinary text, reports the repeated names of the third, and in the fourth finds no Stop conditions section, because that heading is inside a fence that an earlier field left open. Only the web interface renders a form, so no script repeats P-4: seeing whether GitHub changed the layout takes a new submission by the operator.
- **WI-2.** A `[NEEDS CLARIFICATION: …]` marker anywhere in the body blocks dispatch.
- **WI-3. How an acceptance criterion is written (D-45).** One criterion is one line of the Acceptance criteria section, in the text that WI-1 returns for that section:

  ```text
  - AC-1 (test): WHEN a required section is missing THE SYSTEM SHALL name its heading in the lint output.
  ```

  - **The line.** It holds, in this order: a bullet, which may be left out; the id; the verification method in parentheses; a colon; and the statement. The bullet is `-`, `*` or `+` with at least one space or tab after it. The id is `AC-`, in capitals, and a number from 1 to 9999 without a leading zero. The method is `test`, `property`, `review` or `manual`, in small letters. The statement is the rest of the line and holds at least one character that is no white space. Spaces and tabs may stand before the opening parenthesis, before the colon, after it and at the end of the line, and they are part of nothing. The line begins with the bullet, or without one with the id: an indented line is no criterion. A line ends at a line feed, and a carriage return before the line feed is part of the line ending and of no statement (WI-1).
  - **The ids.** Each id stands once in the section. The ids need not begin at 1, follow one another or stand in order, and the list is read in the order written. Removing a criterion therefore leaves the others their ids, and a review comment or the feedback of an earlier run that names `AC-3` still names the same criterion. A list held to `AC-1 … AC-n` in order was not taken: it would have every later criterion renumbered.
  - **The statement.** The host does not hold it to the form *WHEN ⟨condition⟩ THE SYSTEM SHALL ⟨behaviour⟩*. That form is what the section asks of the writer, and a statement without a condition is accepted.
  - **Every other line.** A line of the section that holds a character other than white space and is no such line fails the rule: prose, a nested or indented bullet, a numbered item, a task box, a fence, an id or a method with a typing error. So does a section with no criterion, and one in which an id stands twice. What is reported names each such line by its number in the section and says what is wrong with it. Nothing that the page shows as a criterion is so dropped without a sign. What a writer has to say about the criteria goes into Context.

    | Line | Read as |
    |---|---|
    | `- AC-1 (test): WHEN x THE SYSTEM SHALL y.` | `AC-1`, `test` |
    | `* AC-12 (property): WHEN x THE SYSTEM SHALL y.` | `AC-12`, `property` |
    | `AC-3 (manual) : The README names the command.` | `AC-3`, `manual` |
    | `1. AC-4 (review): WHEN x THE SYSTEM SHALL y.` | No criterion: a numbered item |
    | `- [ ] AC-5 (manual): WHEN x THE SYSTEM SHALL y.` | No criterion: a task box |
    | Two spaces, then `- AC-6 (test): WHEN x THE SYSTEM SHALL y.` | No criterion: indented |
    | `- AC-7 (Test): WHEN x THE SYSTEM SHALL y.` | No criterion: the method is none of the four |
    | `- **AC-8** (test): WHEN x THE SYSTEM SHALL y.` | No criterion: the id is not at its place |
    | `- AC-09 (test): WHEN x THE SYSTEM SHALL y.` | No criterion: a leading zero |
    | `- AC-9 (test):` | No criterion: no statement |

  - **Why one line.** A criterion that ran on to the next line of the form would take in a criterion line with a typing error as part of the one above it, and the host would hold the agent to one criterion fewer than the page shows. A criterion over several lines was not taken, and neither was a section in which lines that are no criterion are passed over.
  - **Why no numbered item and no task box.** One form is enough, and the rule above reports the others by their line. Ticking a task box on the page is an edit of the body by whoever ticked it (P-8), which rule 6 of 5.3 and 7.7 take for an edit after the approval. One body of P-4 holds a line of each kind, whose own statement says that it is still to be read. Those statements were the probe's and no rule.
  - **Where the code lies.** The criterion parser is a pure function of `factory-core`, beside the section parser (19.1): the text of the section in, and out either the list of criteria, each with its id, its method and its statement, or the lines that failed, each with its number and its reason. It is a rule over lines, as the section parser is, and needs no crate of 19.2. M1a builds both parsers (22, WI-4). Of the bodies of WI-1b, the one with every field filled gives two criteria, and the one with only the two required fields gives one. In the body with the fence left open, the Acceptance criteria section gives one criterion and three lines that fail, those of a closed fence of tildes. In the body with the repeated names, the second section of that name, given to the parser by itself, gives two criteria and three lines that fail: the numbered item, the task box and the clarification marker.

### 6.3 Specs in a target repository

- Living specs live at `docs/specs/<capability>/spec.md`. Each requirement has a stable id `R-<CAP>-<NNN>` and acceptance scenarios.
- A tier 2 change proposes a delta under `docs/changes/<parent issue>-<slug>/` with `ADDED`, `MODIFIED` and `REMOVED` requirement sections. Exactly one sub-issue of a tier 2 plan is the spec-delta issue (`type:spec-delta`); it is blocked by all its siblings and merges the delta into the living spec.
- Spec files are protected paths (11.3): changing them outside a spec-delta issue trips a flag.

The format is implemented natively. The factory does not shell out to spec-kit, OpenSpec or Kiro.

The factory's own repository does not use this layout yet (D-18, D-19).

### 6.4 Readiness lint

`sf issue lint <n>` and the dispatcher both run the same check. An issue is ready when: required sections for its tier are present and non-empty (WI-1); no name starts more than one section (WI-1a); the Acceptance criteria section holds at least one criterion and no line that fails WI-3; no clarification marker exists; spec refs resolve at the default-branch head or in the parent's delta; the title that the host generates for a pull request is fit for one (9.4); the body is no larger than 32 KiB after sanitisation (SEC-4). The check also gives the warning of SEC-4 for an HTML comment that nothing closes, in the body or in a comment of the snapshot, and a warning leaves the issue ready.

A lint failure creates no run. The factory moves the issue to `state:needs-human` with `needs:clarification` and posts one comment listing the failed rules, keyed `<!-- factory:lint <sha256 of title and body> -->`. `state:ready` on a `needs-human` issue that has no run is an ordinary dispatch candidate.

- **WI-4. What the claim holds an issue to before the lint is enforced (D-45).** M3 enforces the lint on the issue, with the label and the comment above (22). Until then the claim ends on an issue that fails one of the rules below, with no run row and nothing written to GitHub (8.2), and `sf run` names every rule that failed and not the first alone, each with what broke it: the skip token, the name that repeats, or the lines that WI-3 reports.

  | Rule | What reads it in M1 |
  |---|---|
  | The title that the host generates for a pull request is fit for one (9.4) | `publish`, for the title of the draft pull request |
  | The body is no larger than 32 KiB after sanitisation (SEC-4) | The task block of the implementer (10.4) |
  | No name starts more than one section (WI-1a) | Every use of a section |
  | The Outcome section is there and is not empty (WI-1) | The pull request body, from its first cut (VAL-14a) |
  | The Acceptance criteria section is there, holds at least one criterion and no line that fails WI-3 | The check of an implementer's result (10.5), and from M1b the criterion evaluation and the manifest (11.6, VAL-14) |

  - **Which sections.** The two that tier 0 asks for (6.1), whatever tier label the issue carries: tiers arrive with M3 (22), and no stage of M1 reads another section by its name. An agent reads the other four in the task block, as text.
  - **What waits for M3.** The sections that a tier requires, the spec refs, the clarification marker of WI-2, the label and the comment of a lint failure, and `sf issue lint`. The command in M1a, with the rules of the table, was not taken: `sf run` reports them all at once, and the command would be built a second time around the whole lint.
  - **The way on.** The operator edits the issue, removes `state:ready` and applies it again, since rule 6 of 5.3 asks for a `labeled` event later than the edit, and runs `sf run` once more.
  - **Why the claim ends.** M1a has to know the criteria of an issue, by their ids and their methods, to check a result against them, and M1b to decide when a pull request is ready (VAL-14). With no parser before M3, which is what the roadmap had, both would rest on the list that the agent returned. A body that the parsers cannot read gives the host no such list, and claiming it all the same, with the criteria left out of the result check, was not taken: the gate would be absent exactly where the issue is badly written. D-35 had the claim end on a body over its cap for the same reason, and this item holds that rule with the others.

### 6.5 Discovered work

An agent MUST NOT widen its issue. It reports out-of-scope findings in the `follow_ups` field of its result (10.5). The control plane files each as a new issue in `state:triage` with `factory:origin`, linked to the source issue, up to 3 per run *(tunable)*. Factory-filed issues are never auto-approved: the factory never applies `state:ready` to any issue.

---

## 7. Lifecycle

### 7.1 Two levels

The **public state** is the `state:*` label: five values that humans read and set. The **internal stage** is the run's position in the pipeline; it appears in the status comment and in check runs, never as a label.

### 7.2 Public state transitions

| From | To | Trigger | Who |
|---|---|---|---|
| (new) | `triage` | Issue opened from a form, or filed by the factory | Human or factory |
| `triage` | `ready` | Label applied | Authorised actor only |
| `ready` | `in-progress` | Claim (8.2) | Factory |
| `ready` | `needs-human` | Readiness lint fails (6.4) | Factory |
| `ready` | `triage` | Edited after approval (5.3 rule 6), an author or an editor that SEC-2 does not admit (5.3 rule 7), or unauthorised `state:ready` | Factory |
| `in-progress` | `in-review` | All automated gates green; pull request marked ready | Factory |
| `in-progress` or `in-review` | `needs-human` | Escalation (7.5), or a human applies the label | Factory or human |
| `in-review` | `in-progress` | Human review requests changes; a required check turns red; merge conflict | Factory |
| `in-review` | closed (completed) | Pull request merged | GitHub through `Closes #n`, or the factory if auto-close is off |
| `in-progress` | closed (completed) | Tier 2 parent only: all children closed as completed | Factory |
| `needs-human` | `ready` | Label applied (resume signal) | Authorised actor only |
| `needs-human` (via `ready`) | `in-progress` or `in-review` | Resume (7.5.1) | Factory |
| any | closed | Issue closed without a merged pull request | Human; the run is cancelled (7.7) |

`factory:hold` suspends any of these.

### 7.3 Internal pipeline

```
claim → plan → [plan gate] → [author_tests] → implement → capture → integrity → verify
      → publish → ci → review → ready → human_review → merge → done
```

| Stage | Kind | Runs in | Summary | On failure |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `claim` | host | — | Snapshot, run row, branch ref, label, status comment (8.2) | No run is created |
| `plan` | agent, read-only | sandbox | Return a plan: approach, files, test strategy, risks, questions (10.5). Skipped for tier 0. | Agent failure (8.6) |
| plan gate | wait | GitHub | Required for `risk:high`; otherwise skipped (7.4) | — |
| `author_tests` | agent | sandbox | Optional (M3). A separate session writes failing acceptance tests from the criteria; their paths are then locked for `implement`. | Escalate |
| `implement` | agent, sole writer | sandbox | Change the workspace to satisfy the criteria; emit a result (10.5). | Agent failure (8.6) |
| `capture` | host + sandboxed git | sandbox | Build the candidate commit from the workspace and journal its SHA (9.4). | Rejected paths → `implement`, verify round +1 |
| `integrity` | deterministic | host | Diff policy on the candidate (11.3). | `fail` → `implement`, verify round +1; `flag` → continue, mark the pull request; a hit of the literal-token scan → escalate (SEC-13) |
| `verify` | deterministic | sandbox | Run the policy's commands on a clean export of the candidate (11.2). | → `implement`, verify round +1 |
| `publish` | host | — | Push the journalled candidate; upsert the draft pull request; report the factory checks. | Transient retry; foreign commit → escalate |
| `ci` | wait | GitHub | Wait for the required CI checks on the head SHA (11.4). | Red check → `implement` with the failing log excerpt, CI round +1. Required check missing at the timeout → escalate with `infrastructure` (VAL-8). |
| `review` | agent, read-only | sandbox | Clean-context reviewer returns a verdict (11.5). | Blocking findings → `implement`, review round +1 |
| `ready` | host | — | Evidence manifest written; pull request marked ready; label `state:in-review`; review requested (4.2). | Cannot fail: its preconditions are checked at the end of `implement` and `review` |
| `human_review` | wait | GitHub | Wait for a submitted review (12.2). | Changes requested → `implement`, human round +1 |
| `merge` | host | — | Merge coordinator (12.3). | Conflict → base update (12.4) |
| `done` | host | — | Final status comment; run directory scheduled for removal. | — |

After any return to `implement`, the pipeline re-runs every later stage in order on the new candidate. A change after review always passes `capture`, `integrity`, `verify`, `ci` and `review` again.

**Round caps** *(tunable)*: plan 2, verify 3, CI 2, review 2, human 3, merge-conflict 2. Exceeding a cap escalates, except the merge-conflict cap, which triggers one automatic reset (7.6) if the run has not been reset before. Through M1 the engine starts no round: the operator's next `sf run` does, the counter moves and no cap is enforced (ORCH-10).

**Slots.** A run holds a concurrency slot from `claim` through `review`, including `ci`. It releases the slot in `human_review`, at the plan gate, in `await_children`, in `needs-human` and on hold, and re-acquires one before any return to a stage that runs in a sandbox (an agent stage, `capture` or `verify`), including re-entry at `integrity` after a base update (12.4). The run waits in its current stage until a slot is free.

### 7.4 Plan gate and tier 2 parents

**Plan gate.** It applies to `risk:high` issues. The factory posts the plan as one comment, records `plan_sha256` in the marker, removes any `factory:plan-approved` label, and moves the item to `state:needs-human` with category `plan-approval`.

- **PLAN-1.** The plan comment is rendered by the host from the planner's result, with agent text encoded (GH-5a). `plan_sha256` is the SHA-256 of the comment body as posted. A plan that does not fit one comment (60,000 characters) is a planner failure with a request to reduce.
- **PLAN-2.** An authorised actor approves by applying `factory:plan-approved` and then `state:ready`. The approval is valid only if its `labeled` event is later than the last edit of the plan comment and of the issue body, and the comment's current SHA-256 equals `plan_sha256`. When it consumes a valid approval the factory writes `plan_approved_by` to the marker and then removes the label; validity is evaluated once, at consumption.
- **PLAN-3.** `state:ready` without a valid approval is a revision request: `plan` runs again with the updated snapshot (plan round +1).

**Tier 2 parent pipeline:** `claim → plan → decompose → await_children → done`. A parent has no plan gate. The operator approves or closes each proposed sub-issue instead, so nothing the factory proposes is worked on without its own approval (INV-4).

| Stage | Kind | Summary | On failure |
|---|---|---|---|
| `decompose` | host | Create the proposed sub-issues from the planner's result (PLAN-4, PLAN-5) in `state:triage`, link them as sub-issues of the parent, create the blocked-by links, record `children` in the marker, and post one comment on the parent listing them. | Transient retry; otherwise escalate `infrastructure` |
| `await_children` | wait | Holds no slot. When every child is closed: if all were completed, close the parent as completed; otherwise escalate with `clarification`, so the operator can close the parent or open a new one with a revised scope. | — |

- **PLAN-4.** A plan proposes at most 10 sub-issues *(tunable)*. Each proposed body must pass the tier 1 readiness lint before any sub-issue is created. When the feature changes a spec, exactly one sub-issue is the spec-delta issue (6.3).
- **PLAN-5.** Sub-issue titles and bodies are the one place where agent-proposed text becomes an unfenced issue body, because the operator reads and approves exactly that text. The host builds each one once: the planner's text with the neutralisations of GH-5a applied but no fencing, capped at the 32 KiB lint limit (a longer body is a planner failure). PLAN-4 lints exactly these bytes. They hold the zero-width spaces of GH-5a, which SEC-4 removes before the parser, the lint or an agent reads the text. The issue body is the first-line `factory:subissue` marker (8.5), a newline, then those bytes, so the section parser (WI-1) and the readiness lint apply as they do to a human-written issue.
- **PLAN-6.** The factory applies `factory:origin`, the plan's proposed `risk:*` label and, on the spec-delta issue, `type:spec-delta` when it creates a sub-issue. Labels the factory applied at creation to an issue it authored are covered by the operator's approval: they are honoured once an authorised actor applies `state:ready` to that issue, as long as they have not changed since creation. The operator can change them before approving, under SEC-1.

The parent has a fence ref with no commits and no pull request; the ref is deleted when the parent's run ends. `factory:restart` on a parent in `decompose` or `await_children` is rejected with a comment.

### 7.5 Escalation

`needs-human` is a durable state, not an error. The run stays non-terminal and keeps its run row, branch, pull request and workspace. On escalation the factory:

1. Interrupts any stage execution and releases the concurrency slot.
2. Sets `state:needs-human` and records `escalation` in the marker with a category: `clarification`, `spec-conflict`, `plan-approval`, `budget`, `verification`, `review`, `merge-conflict`, `policy-denial`, `spec-changed`, `infrastructure` or `human-request`.
3. Posts one comment: what was tried, the evidence (links to checks, short log excerpts), one specific question or decision, and how to resume.
4. Mentions the approver once. It does not repeat the mention.

An agent may also stop on its own with status `needs_human` (a stop condition was met, the criteria conflict, a criterion is inapplicable, or the task is impossible as written). This is a valid, unpenalised outcome and does not count against retry caps.

A human applying `state:needs-human` to an active run is an escalation with category `human-request`.

Escalation and its resume arrive with M2 (22). Until then ORCH-11 says what `sf run` does where this specification escalates.

While a run is in `needs-human`, only these rows of 7.7 are evaluated on every tick: the run's pull request is merged (the run enters `done`); issue closed and the pull request is not merged; `state:triage` applied by a human (the run is cancelled); `factory:hold`. The stop of 13.7 also applies. All other rows are evaluated once, at resume (7.5.1).

#### 7.5.1 Resume

A human resumes by answering (editing the issue or commenting) and applying `state:ready`. When the reconciler sees `state:ready` on an issue whose run is in `needs-human`, it does not run the claim. It:

1. Validates the label under SEC-1. If invalid, it removes `state:ready` and leaves `state:needs-human`.
2. Re-evaluates readiness rules 2–4 and 6 of 5.3. Rule 7 is not evaluated again: step 3 asks who edited since the last snapshot. Hold or an open blocker: the issue keeps `state:ready` and the resume waits. Lint failure: the factory removes `state:ready`, leaves `state:needs-human`, sets the category to `clarification` and posts the lint comment of 6.4. Rule 6 failure (title or body edited since the resuming `labeled` event): the factory removes `state:ready`, leaves `state:needs-human` and says so once. On a resume the factory never sets `state:triage`.
3. Chooses the re-entry stage:
   - Title and body unchanged (`title_body_sha256` matches): the recorded stage. A new trusted comment alone never forces a re-plan.
   - Title or body changed, and every edit since the last snapshot is by an authorised actor: `plan`, and any plan approval is void. If the recorded stage is `human_review` or `merge`, the change is noted and the run resumes at the recorded stage.
   - An edit by anyone else, or by an editor that cannot be identified: no resume. `state:ready` is removed, the run stays in `needs-human` with category `spec-changed`, and the remaining steps are not performed.
   - If a valid `factory:restart` is also present, the re-entry is a reset (7.6) instead. Without `state:ready`, `factory:restart` on a run in `needs-human` has no effect.
4. Waits for a slot unless the re-entry stage is `human_review` or `merge`. The issue keeps `state:ready` while it waits.
