5. Adds `state:in-progress` (or `state:in-review` if the re-entry stage is `human_review` or `merge`) and removes the other state labels.
6. Takes a new snapshot.
7. Sets the round counter of the gate that escalated to 0 and restarts that stage's deadline. For category `budget` it grants one more issue-budget increment (section 14) and counts it in `budget_grants`. For category `plan-approval` the plan counter is not zeroed: it counts revision requests (PLAN-3). When it exceeds the plan cap the factory does not re-plan; it escalates with `clarification`, and resuming from that escalation sets the plan counter to 0.

### 7.6 Rework and reset

- **In-place rework** is the default: rounds add commits to the same branch and pull request.
- **Reset**, in order: close the pull request; force-update `refs/heads/factory/issue-<n>` to the current default-branch head with a lease (the ref is never deleted, so the claim fence holds); discard the workspace and run-local caches; take a new `base_sha`, `policy_sha` and snapshot; zero all round counters; keep spend; attempt +1. The new attempt starts at `plan` with a handoff note saying what failed. Plan approval and locked tests do not carry over, so a plan gate that applied runs again.
- A reset happens on `factory:restart`, or automatically once when the merge-conflict cap is reached. A `state:ready` that resumes one of two escalations is handled as a reset too: that of a workflow change in a `type:ci` issue (11.3), and that of a hit of the literal-token scan (SEC-13). Attempts are capped at 2 *(tunable)*; a further reset request escalates instead.
- Every reset and every human rejection SHOULD be linked to a follow-up issue that changes guidance, a lint or a verifier (15.4).

### 7.7 Changes during a run

On every tick the reconciler compares each active run with GitHub. Rows are evaluated in this order:

| Observation | Action |
|---|---|
| The run's pull request is merged | Enter `done`, whatever the merge strategy and whoever merged. If the issue is still open, close it as completed. If the merged head is not one the factory pushed, record `foreign-merge` in the final status comment and on the dashboard. |
| Issue closed and the pull request is not merged | Cancel: run → `cancelled`; write the terminal stage to the marker; close the pull request; delete the branch per 18.2. |
| `state:triage` applied by a human | Cancel as above, leaving the issue open. |
| `state:needs-human` applied by a human | Escalate with `human-request`. |
| `state:ready` applied while the run is `in-progress` or `in-review` | Remove it and ignore it. |
| `factory:hold` applied | Interrupt and freeze. |
| `factory:restart` applied | If valid under SEC-1: reset (7.6) and remove the label; if the attempt cap is reached, escalate instead. If the public state was `in-review`, set `state:in-progress`. If invalid: remove the label and ignore it. A tier 2 parent in `decompose` or `await_children` rejects it (7.4). |
| `tier:*`, `risk:*` or `type:*` changed after claim | Escalate with `spec-changed`. |
| Title or body changed by an authorised actor before the `ready` stage (tier 2 parent: before `decompose`) | Interrupt, take a new snapshot, restart from `plan` in place. Not counted as a failure. |
| Title or body changed by anyone else, or the editor cannot be identified | Escalate with `spec-changed`. |
| Title or body changed after the pull request is ready, or on a tier 2 parent in `decompose` or `await_children` | Note it in the status comment; no automatic action. |
| Pull request closed by a human without merging | Escalate with `review`. |
| Branch head differs from every SHA the factory pushed | Foreign commit: escalate with `policy-denial`; never push over it. |

Before the reconcile loop exists (M2), `sf run` evaluates these rows when ORCH-9 says, and with the actions that ORCH-9 gives them.

---

## 8. Orchestrator

### 8.1 Engine

- **ORCH-1.** The engine is a pure function `step(State, Event) -> (State, Vec<Effect>)` with no I/O, clock or randomness. Time and ids arrive as events or injected values.
- **ORCH-2.** One engine task owns all run state. The `runs` row is the authoritative run state; the `events` table is an audit record and is never replayed to derive state.
- **ORCH-3.** Each transition is committed in one transaction: append the event, update the run row, record the effects. Effects fall into three classes:

| Class | Examples | Rule |
|---|---|---|
| External write | GitHub call, push | Stored in the outbox with an effect id; delivered at least once, in FIFO order per run. Completion is one transaction that marks the row done and appends the result event carrying the effect id. `step` ignores a result whose effect id it has already applied. Through M1 there is no outbox, and ORCH-7 says how a write is made. |
| Activity | Agent session, capture, verify (each including its setup commands) | Never in the outbox. Recorded as an activity row (id, sandbox record, process data, state). After a restart it is killed and marked abandoned; the engine receives `ActivityAbandoned` and requests a new one with a new id. |
| Timer | Stage deadlines, backoff, poll | Stored as a deadline on the run row and re-armed from the store at startup. |

- **ORCH-4.** The engine receives only coarse activity events: `ActivityStarted`, `UsageBatch` (all per-model-call usage records since the last batch, with the figures of BUD-9, at most once per 5 s and always before the end event), `LimitReached(kind)`, `LoopDetected`, `Stalled`, `ActivityEnded(outcome, chain_digest)`. In `gateway` mode the usage records in `UsageBatch`, and the spend the supervisor compares with the session's dollar limit, come from the model gateway's meter for the run's token; the CLI's own usage events are written to `events.jsonl` and used only to report a discrepancy. In `subscription` and `direct_key` modes the CLI's usage events are the only source (13.5, section 14). Idle, loop and per-session limits are evaluated in the supervisor from limits passed in the session spec. Raw and normalised agent events go only to `events.jsonl`, which the supervisor hash-chains. The supervisor's stdout reader never blocks on the engine channel.
- **ORCH-5.** `step` runs under `catch_unwind` per run; a panic moves that run to `needs-human` with `infrastructure`. A panic or I/O error in the store writer stops the daemon with a non-zero exit; restart recovery (8.4) is the handling.

### 8.2 Claim

The daemon is the only dispatcher for a store, enforced by an exclusive file lock on the data directory. No issue is claimed on a repository whose label set lacks a label of 5.2 (GH-3a), or for which the host lacks the toolchain or a check tool (WS-7a), or the price table a row for the model of a stage that the run would start (BUD-3). Nor is an issue claimed where GitHub lists a rule for the name of its work branch (GH-22). Claiming an issue is, in order:

1. Read the rules that GitHub lists for the name of the work branch (GH-22): where it lists one the claim ends here, with no run row and nothing written to GitHub. Read the issue as GH-21 says, which is one GraphQL query and, where the issue has them, the older pages of its events and its comments: check SEC-1 on the `state:ready` label, rule 6 of 5.3 and rule 7 (SEC-2); capture the snapshot; record tier, risk and type under SEC-1. An issue that fails one of the three is not claimed: the claim ends here with no run row, and 5.3 and 7.2 say what the factory writes to the issue. Read the default-branch head once: it is both `base_sha` and `policy_sha`. Refuse a base tree that contains a submodule entry, or that has the layout of a git repository at its root (WS-13). Resolve the toolchain and the check tools that this commit names (WS-7, WS-7a), and look up the row of the price table for the model that its policy names for each stage that this build runs a session of (BUD-3); where one is missing the claim ends here, with no run row and nothing written to GitHub. Sanitise the text of the snapshot (SEC-4). Run the readiness lint on the snapshot. Until the lint is enforced (M3, 22), the claim ends here on an issue that fails a rule of WI-4, a body over the cap of SEC-4 among them, with no run row and nothing written to GitHub.
2. Insert the run row carrying all of the above. A unique index on (repository, issue) for non-terminal runs makes this the authoritative local claim.
3. Journal the attempt, then create `refs/heads/factory/issue-<n>` at `base_sha` with `POST /git/refs`. GitHub refuses the call with a 422 when the ref exists, and with a 422 for every fault in the ref name or the object that probe P-1 tried, and only an undocumented message tells them apart. Where a ruleset restricts the creation of the ref, the 422 carries the message `Reference update failed` whether the ref exists or not (P-1), and the create of a new ref got the same message under two more rules (P-45, GH-22). A 422 from this call is therefore not a Deterministic failure under 8.6 until the claim has read the ref by its exact name, with `GET /git/ref/heads/factory/issue-<n>`:
   - The ref exists. It counts as created only on a replay, and only if its SHA equals the journalled `base_sha` of this run. A create is a replay when an earlier create of this run may have taken effect unseen: the attempt was already journalled when the daemon started (8.4), or through M1 when this `sf run` started (ORCH-7), or the earlier request ended in a Transient failure (8.6). A ref left by a `cancelled` run of this store is force-updated. Any other existing ref aborts the claim, a ref at `base_sha` that answers the first create of the run included, since this run did not make it: the run row is closed as `cancelled` and the issue goes to `state:needs-human` with category `policy-denial` (through M1 without that label: ORCH-11).
   - The ref does not exist (404). The create was refused for another reason and is a Deterministic failure (8.6), and the escalation carries GitHub's message. One such reason is a branch whose name collides with the ref, `factory` or one under `factory/issue-<n>/`, which GitHub reports only as `Reference update failed`. Another is a rule on the name of the ref, which it reports to the App with the same message and without naming a rule. GH-22 says what the claim then reads and what is reported.
4. Add `state:in-progress`, then remove `state:ready` and every other `state:*` label (`state:triage`, or `state:needs-human` left by a lint failure), and remove `needs:clarification` if present.
5. Create the status comment with the complete marker.

Running two daemons against one repository from different hosts is unsupported. The branch-ref step is a best-effort fence against that mistake, not a distributed lock. Neither GitHub's answer nor the ref says who created it (probe P-1), so a run that replays the create cannot tell its own ref from one that another daemon made at the same `base_sha` in the meantime.

**Deadlines.** There are no leases. The supervisor owns one set of deadlines per stage kind:

| Stage kind | Deadline |
|---|---|
| Agent | Idle: no agent event for 300 s *(tunable)* while no tool call is outstanding. Tool call: a single tool call outstanding for more than `tool_call_timeout_secs` (policy, default 1800 *(tunable)*). Wall clock and budget: section 14. |
| Deterministic (capture, verify, and the setup step of any activity) | The command timeout only. |
| Wait: `ci` | `ci.timeout_secs` from the push of the head SHA (default 3600). |
| Wait: `human_review`, plan gate, `await_children` | None; one nudge (PR-3). |

Neither stall deadline of an agent stage was measured. The 300 s are those of prior art (`docs/research/02-orchestration-architecture.md`), and D-22 set the turns, the wall clock and the spend of a session from a measurement and not these two. A subagent in the foreground is one tool call of the main loop: the Agent call returns when the subagent has ended (P-26). The host keeps every subagent in the foreground (BUD-10), so `tool_call_timeout_secs` also bounds a subagent's whole run. In the operator's interactive sessions the longest model call took 552 s and the longest tool call 2,489 s (`docs/research/15-session-sizes.md`). Those are whole calls and not the gaps between the events of a stream, and whether a long model call leaves such a gap on a headless stream was not observed. The five issues of M1b are the first measurement of either deadline.

If the supervising task itself dies, the activity row is found on the next reconcile, its processes are killed, and the stage restarts (counts toward Poison, 8.6).

### 8.3 Scheduling

Each tick, in order: reconcile active runs (7.7); validate policy (16.3) and the workflow audit (VAL-6a); fetch candidates; sort; dispatch while limits allow.

| Limit | Default *(tunable)* |
|---|---|
| Concurrent runs holding a slot, global | 4 |
| Concurrent runs holding a slot, per repository | 2 |
| Concurrent merges per repository | 1 (fixed) |
| Factory pull requests awaiting human review per repository | 3 |
| Free space on the data volume | No dispatch below `min_free_bytes` (operator) |
| Run directory size | Per-run quota, checked at stage boundaries and every 5 minutes; exceeding it escalates with `budget` |
| Spend (section 14) | per stage, per issue, per day |
| Dispatch schedule | Optional hours in which new stages may start (operator), to leave plan capacity for the operator's own interactive use |

When the review-queue limit is reached, no new `implement` stage starts in that repository; runs already past `implement` continue. Dispatch also pauses when the provider reports that a limit is reached (in `subscription` mode: until the plan limit's reported reset time; running sessions are interrupted and their stages restart afterwards without a round or failure being counted; the `rate_limit_event` with status `allowed` that every completed session emits is not such a report, probe P-22), on the GitHub write budget (GH-16), and when a circuit breaker is open (8.6). Before that pause exists (M2), a session that meets the plan's limit ends as BUD-9 says.

### 8.4 Restart recovery

On startup the daemon, before dispatching anything:

1. Takes the store lock.
2. Reaps every activity recorded as live: destroys its sandbox instance and sweeps for surviving processes (AG-18).
3. For each non-terminal run, compares the `runs` row with GitHub (labels, marker, branch head, pull request, checks) and with the run directory. GitHub wins. The run resumes at the start of its recorded stage, replaying journalled sub-steps (a journalled candidate SHA, a journalled push) instead of recomputing them. An export is never replayed (WS-14).
4. If the store is missing or corrupt, `sf rebuild` reconstructs runs from status-comment markers, branches and pull requests, under GH-5b:
   - The approver is re-read from the latest `labeled` event for `state:ready`.
   - A branch head that differs from the marker's `head_sha` is accepted only if it carries a `factory/integrity` check run from the factory App with this run id; otherwise it is a foreign commit.
   - A run whose marker stage is `ci`, `ready`, `human_review`, `merge` or `await_children`, or that is waiting at the plan gate, keeps that stage; a plan approved after a rebuild is re-read from the plan comment after its hash is checked against `plan_sha256`. A run rebuilt in `decompose` re-runs it; the sub-issue markers of 8.5 make that idempotent. A run rebuilt in any other stage restarts at `plan` with a workspace created from `head_sha` (from `base_sha` when nothing was published), because its handoff files are gone.
   - A marker that carries an `escalation` rebuilds the run in `needs-human`; these rules apply to its recorded stage at resume (7.5.1).

Before the daemon exists (M2), `sf run` does at its start what ORCH-8 says. `sf rebuild` arrives with the daemon (22).

### 8.5 Idempotency keys

| Side effect | Key and replay rule |
|---|---|
| Branch | `base_sha` and the attempt are journalled before the create is sent; on a replay, an existing ref equal to `base_sha` counts as created (8.2). |
| Candidate commit | Tree, parents, dates and message are journalled with the candidate SHA before any push. Recovery uses the journalled SHA and never rebuilds a journalled candidate. If the tree equals the previous candidate's, no new commit is made. |
| Push | The executor reads the remote ref first. Remote head equals the candidate: pushed. Remote head equals the previously journalled head: push. Anything else: foreign commit (7.7). |
| Pull request | Lookup of open pull requests by head branch before create. |
| Status comment | Comment id in the journal; on rebuild, GH-5. |
| Other comments | First-line marker `<!-- factory:event <run>/<seq> -->` in an App-authored comment, looked up before posting. |
| Check run | List check runs for the head SHA filtered by name and App id; update the one whose `external_id` matches, else create. |
| Merge | Read the pull request before every merge call: merged with our head SHA is success. A 409 from `merge-async` means a request is pending: poll the pull request's merged state for up to 10 minutes, then re-check eligibility. |
| Agent session | Session id generated and journalled before spawn; a restarted activity gets a new id. |
| Follow-up issue, sub-issue | First-line marker `<!-- factory:followup <run>/<index> -->` or `<!-- factory:subissue <run>/<key> -->` in the body; lookup by listing issues created by the App since the run started, never the search API. |

ORCH-6 says where a value lies that this table has journalled: on the `runs` row where a run reads it to go on, and in the event otherwise.

### 8.6 Failure handling

| Class | Examples | Handling |
|---|---|---|
| Transient | Network error, 5xx, provider overload, secondary rate limit | Exponential backoff with jitter, capped at 5 minutes; no round cost |
| Deterministic | 401, 403, 422, a push that GitHub rejects (GH-22), but for the one of step 5 of 12.4, invalid policy, missing sandbox, a toolchain or check tool missing after the claim (WS-7a) | No retry; escalate with `infrastructure` or pause dispatch |
| Gate failure | Capture, integrity, verify, CI or review failed | Bounded rounds (7.3) |
| Agent failure | Status `failed`, no result, schema-invalid result | Restart the stage once with a fresh session and the failure summary in the handoff; then escalate with `verification` |
| Stall | The idle or tool-call deadline of 8.2 passed | Interrupt; restart the stage once with a fresh session; then escalate |
| Budget | Any limit in section 14 reached; usage credits in use (BUD-8) | Stop the stage; escalate with `budget` (through M1: BUD-7) |
| Poison | The same stage was abandoned or restarted 3 times, not counting a start of a stage that follows the operator's command through M1 (BUD-7, ORCH-8) | Escalate with `infrastructure` |
| Systemic | 5 consecutive runs fail with the same class *(tunable)* | Open the circuit breaker: pause all dispatch, report through `sf status` and the dashboard issue (15.5) |

Through M1 the engine starts no round, restarts no stage and escalates nothing. The last column then holds for the Transient class alone. ORCH-10 takes its place where it has a round or a restart of `implement`, and ORCH-11 where it escalates or restarts another stage. Poison has no case before M2 (ORCH-8), and neither has Systemic, since nothing is dispatched.

### 8.7 Loop and no-progress detection

- **In session:** the supervisor hashes each tool call (name and arguments). A repeating pattern of period 1–3 within the last 10 calls *(tunable)* triggers an interrupt and a restart of the stage with a note describing the loop. A second detection escalates.
- **Across rounds:** if two consecutive rounds end with the same failing-check fingerprint or the same candidate tree, the run escalates without using its remaining rounds.

### 8.8 One foreground run, through M1

Through M1 the factory is `sf run`: one command in the foreground, attended, for one run (CLI-3). It is the engine of 8.1 without what the daemon brings. The outbox, the recovery of 8.4, the rounds that the engine starts, escalation and the reconcile loop arrive with M2 (22). The items below say what stands in the place of each until then (D-44). ORCH-6 says where the records lie, which holds in every milestone, and which tables M1a creates. ORCH-7 to ORCH-12 bind `sf run` through M1 and not the daemon.

- **ORCH-6. What M1 records.** M1a creates four of the tables of 18.1, each in the form that it has there: `runs`, `events`, `activities` and `spend`. A run that survives an interruption is M1a's exit criterion (22), and the next `sf run` goes on from what these four hold.
  - **`runs`.** The row is the authoritative run state from the claim on (ORCH-2), and it is what `sf run` reads where this specification has it resume from the journal (CLI-3). It holds what 18.1 lists and, with it, what a later `sf run` reads to go on: the snapshot with its two hashes, `base_sha` and `policy_sha` (8.2); the attempt, before the ref is created; the candidate of the current round, by its SHA and the SHA of its tree, before any push; the tree that the workspace was last captured from (9.4); the head that the factory last pushed (8.5); the hit of the literal-token scan that stopped the run, where there was one (SEC-13); and from M1b the id of the review that last started a round (ORCH-10) and `size_strikes`, the count of the size budget (11.3). Of what 8.5 has journalled before a write, these values are on the row before the write is sent, committed in the transaction that appends the event (ORCH-3). The event holds the rest for the audit (13.8): of a candidate its parents, its dates and its message. Nothing is read back from `events` (ORCH-2). A run that goes on reads the row and finds the commit in `host.git`.
  - **The candidate of the current round.** The row holds no candidate from a return to `implement` (ORCH-10) until the next capture has made one. A capture that was cut off is therefore made again, and the candidate of an earlier round is never taken for its result. The tree that the workspace was last captured from stays on the row through the return, since the next capture starts its index from it (9.4). Where a capture makes that very tree again, no commit is made (8.5) and the row gains no candidate: the session changed nothing, and ORCH-10 says what follows.
  - **`activities`.** One row for each stage execution: an implementer session, a capture or a verify, each with its setup commands (WS-4). The row is committed as live before the first exec, and it is the record of AG-15. When the stage execution ends, the row takes its outcome, its usage and its chain digest (ORCH-4). ORCH-8 says what becomes of a row that is still live when `sf run` starts. Which pid and which group the row holds at level `process` stays open, as D-40 left it.
  - **`spend`.** One row for each model call of a main loop, and for each session one remainder for each model that its subagents ran on (BUD-4, BUD-10), written from the `UsageBatch` events (ORCH-4). The `runs` row holds the sums. The spend of an issue is the sum over its runs, the cancelled ones among them (BUD-7). What a batch carries of the plan's window (BUD-9) stays in the `events` row of that batch.
  - **Not in M1.** `outbox` arrives with M2 (ORCH-7), `etags` with the ETag cache of GH-14, and `probes` with the capability probe, which is all that it holds (18.1, D-39). A migration adds each (18.1).
  - **Not taken.** Two tables, as the roadmap had them, with the one live activity, the candidate and the sums of spend on a wider `runs` row. M2 would then change the shape of that row, and M1's stage executions would leave no record but the event stream. The five issues of M1b are the first measurement of the session limits (section 14), and it is read from these records. All seven tables from M1a on was not taken either: three of them would be specified before anything reads them.
- **ORCH-7. External writes through M1.** Through M1 there is no outbox, and no write is stored before it is made. `sf run` makes each external write itself: the ref, the labels, the push and the pull request in M1a, and with them the status comment and the check runs in M1b. It makes them in the order in which `step` returned them, through the serial queue of GH-13, each under its key of 8.5, and it commits the result event of each with the `runs` row in one transaction (ORCH-3). The engine is the one of 8.1: `step` returns each write as an effect with an effect id, and it ignores a result that it has already applied.
  - **What stands in for delivery.** The `runs` row leaves a stage that writes only when every write of that stage has its result. Such stages are `claim` and `publish`, and from M1b `ready` and `done`; the cancel of ORCH-9 is held to the same rule. Where `sf run` ends before that, the next one starts the recorded stage over (ORCH-8). `step` asks for the writes of the stage again, and the replay rule of each key finds what is there already (INV-7). The create of the ref is a replay when the attempt was on the row before that `sf run` started, which 8.2 now names beside the start of the daemon. A write whose key is an id that the row holds only after the write is looked up on GitHub before it is made a second time. The pull request is looked up by its head branch (8.5). The status comment is the one that GH-5 finds on a rebuild, the most recently created App-authored comment whose body starts with the marker block, and it is this run's where its marker holds this run id.
  - **Why no outbox.** An outbox orders the writes of many runs and delivers what a restart left pending. One foreground run has one writer, and the order of its writes is the order in which it makes them. Nothing delivers a pending write but the next `sf run`, and that one starts the stage over in any case (8.4).
  - **Not taken.** A small outbox in M1a, for the four kinds of write that M1a makes. It would build delivery for a run that has nothing to be ordered against. The recovery simulation of 19.4 is written against the outbox, and the process-crash test is part of M2's exit (22). M1 has a row of its own in 19.4 for the property that the two check.
- **ORCH-8. The start of `sf run`.** It does three things, in this order.
  1. It takes the store lock (CLI-3). Whoever holds the lock is the one `sf` process on the store, so nothing supervises an activity that the store records as live.
  2. It reaps every activity that is recorded as live, of whichever run. It destroys the sandbox instance, sweeps for surviving processes (AG-18) and marks the row abandoned, which is step 2 of 8.4. It prints what it reaped. No stage starts until the sweep is empty.
  3. Where the store has no non-terminal run of the issue that the command names, it claims the issue (8.2). Where it has one whose recorded stage is `claim`, it first completes the claim, since the labels of such an issue are in the middle of step 4 of 8.2. It then reads GitHub as ORCH-9 says. Unless that read ends the run or stops it, or the row records a hit of the literal-token scan (SEC-13), the run goes on at the start of its recorded stage, and a sub-step that is journalled is replayed and not made again (8.4, 8.5).
  - **A stage execution that was cut off.** Its stage starts again as a new stage execution, with a new `<seq>`, a new home and, for a session, a new session id (8.5). A verify has a new export (WS-14). An implementer session finds the workspace as the last one left it (INV-11). A session that was cut off returned no result, and so no notes. The host writes into the handoff that the last session was cut off, as it writes which limit stopped one (BUD-7).
  - **Poison.** Through M1 every start of a stage follows a command of the operator's. None counts toward Poison (8.6), which so has no case before M2. The journal records each.
  - **An interrupt and a kill.** On SIGINT or SIGTERM `sf run` shuts down as 19.3 says: it interrupts the activity through the stop sequence (AG-12), records the activity as ended and releases the lock. A `sf run` that is killed outright records nothing. Its workers end with it (AG-16), its activity row stays live, and step 2 of the next start finds the row. Step 2 reaps the activities of every run and not those of the named run alone: a `sf run` that was killed on another issue left its row in the same store, and nothing else comes back for it.
  - **Not taken.** Reaping, and then exiting with a report, so that a second command restarts the stage: the operator who ran the command on the issue again has asked for the stage, and the report of what was reaped is printed before it starts. Refusing to start, with the clean-up left to the operator, was not taken either: the exit criterion of M1a would rest on a step that is made by hand.
- **ORCH-9. What `sf run` reads of GitHub.** There is no reconcile loop before M2. `sf run` reads GitHub for its run when it starts on a run that exists, before any stage starts (ORCH-8), and from M1b on every poll of the `ci` wait (ORCH-12). Of the issue it reads the state, the labels, the title and the body, and none of the lists of GH-21: no row below reads an editor, an event or a comment, and rule 7 of 5.3 is the claim's. It also reads the head of the work branch, and the pull request with its reviews. On what it read it evaluates the rows of 7.7 in the order of that table, with the three rows on a changed title or body read as two, and it ends at the first row that ends the run or stops it. While a session or a command runs it reads nothing: the operator's interrupt is the hold of M1.

  | Row of 7.7 | Through M1 |
  |---|---|
  | The run's pull request is merged | As 7.7: the run enters `done` |
  | Issue closed and the pull request is not merged | As 7.7: the run is cancelled |
  | `state:triage` applied by a human | As 7.7: the run is cancelled, and the issue stays open |
  | `state:needs-human` applied by a human | The run stops (ORCH-11) |
  | `state:ready` applied while the run is `in-progress` or `in-review` | As 7.7: removed and ignored |
  | `factory:hold` applied | The run stops (ORCH-11), in the place of the freeze |
  | `factory:restart` applied | The run stops (ORCH-11): the reset is M2, and the label is left as it is |
  | `tier:*`, `risk:*` or `type:*` changed after claim | The run stops (ORCH-11) |
  | Title or body changed before the `ready` stage, by whomever | The run stops (ORCH-11): a restart from `plan` is M2 |
  | Title or body changed after the pull request is ready, by whomever | Noted in the status comment, as 7.7 has it for an authorised actor's edit |
  | Pull request closed by a human without merging | The run stops (ORCH-11) |
  | Branch head differs from every SHA the factory pushed | The run stops (ORCH-11), and while it lives nothing is pushed over the commit (8.5) |

  - **How a change is seen.** A changed title or body is one whose text no longer has the `title_body_sha256` of the `runs` row (SEC-4). A changed `tier:*`, `risk:*` or `type:*` is a label set that differs from what the claim recorded (8.2).
  - **Who edited is not asked.** 7.7 has three rows for a changed title or body, and two of them turn on the editor. Through M1 `sf run` reads none: before the `ready` stage it stops for an authorised actor's edit as for any other. After it, 7.7 escalates for an edit by anyone else, and `sf run` notes that edit like the operator's own. A run of M1 takes no second snapshot (ORCH-11), so the changed text reaches no session. Who edited is asked from M2, with SEC-1 for all human inputs (22).
  - **A head that is no foreign commit.** The head of the work branch is the factory's own where it is `base_sha`, at which the claim created the ref, the head that the factory last pushed, or the candidate of the `runs` row. The last is a push that was cut off before its result was recorded, and the push rule of 8.5 takes it as pushed. Any other head is a foreign commit.
  - **A row that stops asks for no authority.** `sf run` stops for `factory:hold` and for `factory:restart` whoever applied the label, and the run goes on once the label is gone, whoever removed it. 5.2 honours both under SEC-1 alone, and SEC-1 for these inputs arrives with M2 (22). A label that can only stop work needs no check to be honoured, as SEC-1 says of `state:triage`. That anyone who can label can lift a hold is accepted for an attended run.
  - **`done` and `cancelled`.** Each is made with the writes that the milestone has built. The terminal marker and the final status comment are M1b's, with the status comment. A `sf run` that finishes or cancels a run reports it and exits with 0. It claims nothing in the same invocation.
  - **Cancelling a run.** Through M1 the operator cancels by one of the two rows: they apply `state:triage` or close the issue, and run `sf run` on it. The cancel closes the pull request, takes from the issue the state label that the run had set, and leaves every other label. To have the issue worked on again the operator applies `state:ready` and runs `sf run` once more. A `state:ready` that already stood beside `state:triage` at the cancel is left, being the start signal of 5.2, and that next `sf run` claims by it. The claim force-updates the ref which the cancelled run left (8.2). `sf cancel` goes through the control API and arrives with it, in M2 (CLI-4).
  - **Not taken.** Three rows alone, the merged pull request, the closed issue and `state:triage`, which is what finishing and cancelling need. A run would then go on working from a text that the operator has since changed, and `factory:hold` would do nothing before M2. A `sf cancel` of M1a that takes the store lock itself was not taken either: it saves the operator one label and is plumbed anew in M2.
- **ORCH-10. A failure that returns to `implement`.** Through M1 the factory starts no second implementer session by itself. Where 7.3 or 8.6 answers an outcome with a round or with a restart of `implement`, `sf run` records the return in three steps, and then reports and ends.
  1. It writes the feedback into `handoff/feedback/` (10.4).
  2. From M1b it makes the writes of the return on GitHub: the status comment, with the report as its last error (5.4), and the label where the public state changes (below). In M1a nothing is written to GitHub at such a failure: the issue keeps `state:in-progress`, and nothing is published.
  3. It commits the return in one transaction: the event, the `runs` row at `implement`, without a candidate (ORCH-6) and with the name of the feedback file of step 1, and for a failed gate the round counter of that gate, one higher. The handoff block of the next session takes the feedback that the row names, so a file that a cut-off `sf run` left without a return is given to no session.
  4. It reports the outcome on the terminal: the stage, what failed, and where the feedback and the full log lie. It exits with a failure.

  The writes of step 2 come before the transaction, so that the stage of the failed gate is left only when they have their results (ORCH-7). Where `sf run` is cut off between the two, the next one starts that stage over and comes to the return again.

  Run again on the issue, `sf run` starts `implement` with a fresh session (AG-9), with the whole of its session limits (BUD-7) and with that feedback in the handoff block (10.4). The operator's command stands in for what the engine does by itself from M2, the start of a round or the restart of the stage, as BUD-7 has it stand in for the resume and the budget grant.

  | Outcome | From | Feedback | Counter |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | Capture rejects a path or the subject (9.4) | M1a | The rejected paths, each with its reason, or the rule that the subject broke | verify |
  | Capture makes the tree that the workspace was last created or captured from: the session changed nothing (ORCH-6) | M1a | That nothing changed, with the feedback that the session had been given | None |
  | Integrity `fail` (11.3) | M1a | The rules that failed, each with its paths | verify |
  | Verify fails (11.2) | M1a | The gate feedback of 10.4 | verify |
  | A required check is red (VAL-8) | M1b | The tail of the failing step (VAL-9) | CI |
  | A review by an authorised actor requests changes (PR-1) | M1b | The review body and its line comments | human |
  | Agent failure: status `failed`, no result, or a result that the schema rejects (8.6) | M1a | The failure summary | None |
  | Stall: the idle or the tool-call deadline passed (8.2) | M1a | Which deadline it was | None |
  | A loop detected in the session (8.7) | M1a | The note that describes the loop | None |
  | A session limit, usage credits in use, or the plan's limit (BUD-7 to BUD-9) | M1a | Which limit it was | None |

  - **A session that returned no result.** After each of the last four rows nothing of the session is captured or published (BUD-7), and the `runs` row is at `implement` already. BUD-7 is this item for a session limit.
  - **A session that changed nothing.** In the full pipeline its round ends with the candidate tree of the round before, and the run escalates (8.7). Through M1 the same holds for a first session that leaves the workspace as it was created: no candidate is made of an empty change set, since there is nothing to publish.
  - **After a publish.** A red check and a review that requests changes come after the pull request exists. Where the state was `state:in-review`, the factory sets `state:in-progress` (7.2). The next candidate has the published head as its parent and goes to the same branch and the same pull request (7.6). CI runs on it, and an approval of the earlier head no longer counts (PR-2).
  - **Which `sf run` goes on.** A failure that occurs while `sf run` runs ends that `sf run`. A review is submitted while none runs. The `sf run` that finds it (ORCH-12) makes steps 1 to 3 and then starts `implement` itself, without step 4: the review is an authorised actor's (PR-1), who with one human is the operator (4.2), and the operator then ran the command.
  - **A review starts one round.** GitHub keeps a review after the head has moved. Step 3 therefore writes the id of the review to the `runs` row (ORCH-6), and a review whose id the row holds starts no second round. The next round is started by a later review that requests changes.
  - **Counters, and no cap.** The return after a failed gate is a round as `GLOSSARY.md` has it, started by the operator. Its counter moves, and the journal shows it, as from M1b the marker and the pull request body do (5.4, 11.6). No cap of 7.3 is enforced, and neither is the rule of 8.7 across rounds: exceeding either escalates, and escalation is M2. D-22 did the same with the limits of section 14, of which M1 enforces the session limits and records the rest. The caps are tunable, and the five issues of M1b are the first record of how many rounds an issue takes. Leaving every counter at 0 through M1, which the review of draft 4 proposed, was not taken: the marker of M1b would show no round on a pull request that went back several times.
  - **The size budget, from M1b.** Its first `fail` is an integrity `fail` of the table. `size_strikes` moves on the `runs` row in the transaction of step 3 (ORCH-6), and the second time the rule flags (11.3). The request to split asks the session for a smaller change: no follow-up issue is filed before M2 (22).
  - **Not taken.** Verify rounds in M1a, with the cap of 3 and a report at the cap. M1a would build the caps and the rule of 8.7 before the escalation that both lead to, and several sessions would run with nobody having read why the last one failed, in a milestone that is attended so that somebody does.
- **ORCH-11. A failure that stops the run.** Where the full pipeline answers with an escalation, a reset, a freeze or a new snapshot, or with the restart of a stage other than `implement`, `sf run` reports what it found, starts no stage and exits with a failure. The report goes to the terminal, and from M1b into the status comment as its last error (5.4). The `runs` row keeps its stage. No `state:needs-human` is set and no comment is posted: both belong to the escalation of 7.5, whose resume is M2. 12.3 had this rule for a merge conflict alone. The operator has two ways on. Where the cause can be removed, they remove it and run `sf run` again, which starts the recorded stage over (ORCH-8). Where the run cannot go on, they cancel it and approve the issue again (ORCH-9), and the new run starts from a new branch with a new snapshot. After a hit of the literal-token scan the second way is the only one (SEC-13).

  | What `sf run` found | The operator's way on |
  |---|---|
  | A Deterministic failure (8.6): a 401, a 403 or a 422 from GitHub, a push that GitHub rejects (GH-22), an invalid policy, a missing sandbox, a toolchain or a check tool that went missing after the claim (WS-7a) | Remove the cause and run again |
  | A setup command that failed (WS-4) | Run again, after removing the cause where there is one |
  | A capture that ran into its timeout, which 9.4 has as an abandoned activity whose stage restarts | Run again: `capture` starts over |
  | A required check that is still missing when the `ci` wait ends (VAL-8, ORCH-12), from M1b | Run again, which waits again |
  | `factory:hold`, `factory:restart` or a human's `state:needs-human` on the issue (ORCH-9) | Remove the label and run again. In the place of a reset: cancel and approve again |
  | A pull request that a human closed without merging (ORCH-9) | Reopen it and run again, or cancel |
  | A result with the status `needs_human` (7.5) | Answer by editing the issue, cancel and approve again |
  | A title or a body that changed since the claim, or a changed `tier:*`, `risk:*` or `type:*` (ORCH-9) | Cancel and approve again |
  | A foreign commit on the work branch (ORCH-9) | Take from the branch what is to be kept, then cancel and approve again. The next claim force-updates the ref (8.2), and the commit is on no branch after it. The report says so |
  | A test-merge that conflicts (12.3), from M1b | Cancel and approve again |
  | An integrity `fail` for a workflow change in a `type:ci` issue (11.3) | Commit the workflow by hand, then cancel and approve again. No patch is attached before M2: the report names the candidate, which lies in `host.git` |
  | A hit of the literal-token scan, in a candidate or in a text for GitHub (SEC-13) | Re-issue the token, then cancel and approve again. Until the cancel, `sf run` reports the hit again and starts no stage |

  - **`needs_human`.** `sf run` prints the questions of the result (10.5), and the host writes its notes to `progress.md` as after any result. The outcome is no failure of the agent (7.5). `sf run` exits with a failure all the same, because the run did not go on.
  - **A claim that ends.** Where step 1 of the claim refuses an issue, there is no run and nothing is written to GitHub (8.2), and `sf run` says why and exits with a failure. Where step 3 finds a ref that this run did not make, the `runs` row is closed as `cancelled`, as 8.2 has it. The issue keeps `state:ready` and gets no `state:needs-human`, and the report names the ref. The row is closed and does not keep its stage, because the next `sf run` would otherwise take its create for a replay (ORCH-7) and adopt the ref. A run that was closed so made no ref, and the ref is none that a cancelled run left (8.2): the next claim meets it again until the operator has deleted the branch. A create that GitHub refused for another reason is a Deterministic failure, and the row keeps the stage `claim`.
  - **The snapshot.** A run of M1 keeps the snapshot of its claim, and no `sf run` takes a second one. D-36 rests on that. The resume that takes a new one is that of 7.5.1, which is M2. An answer therefore reaches the factory through the text of the issue and a new run, and the cost is the workspace and the session of the run that is cancelled. A new snapshot at every start of `sf run` where title and body are unchanged, so that a comment of the operator's reaches the next session, was not taken. It builds a part of 7.5.1 in M1a, and an answer that changes an acceptance criterion changes the body in any case.
  - **Not taken.** The first half of the escalation in M1b, the label with its category and the comment. `state:needs-human` would then stand on an issue with no resume behind it, and a `state:ready` applied to that issue would be removed and ignored (ORCH-9).
- **ORCH-12. Where `sf run` ends, and where it waits.** `sf run` waits where a deadline bounds the wait, and ends where none does.
  - **M1a.** A build of M1a ends its pipeline with `publish`: `sf run` reports the draft pull request and exits with 0. The `runs` row then holds the stage `ci`, for which that build has no code. Run again on the issue, it makes the read of ORCH-9, reports that the pull request is published and exits with 0.
  - **`ci`, from M1b.** `sf run` stays in the foreground. It polls the check runs of the head every 30 s *(tunable)*, the interval of GH-18, and makes the read of ORCH-9 on each poll. The wait ends `ci.timeout_secs` after the push of the head (8.2) or after the start of this `sf run`, whichever is later, so that a `sf run` which starts on a run in `ci` waits once more. Every required check satisfied (VAL-8): the run goes on to `ready` and enters `human_review`. A red check: ORCH-10. A check that is still missing at the end: ORCH-11.
  - **`human_review`, from M1b.** The wait has no deadline (8.2), and `sf run` does not wait in it. When the run enters `human_review`, `sf run` reports that the pull request is ready for review and exits with 0. Run again on the issue, it makes the read of ORCH-9 and the test-merge of 12.3, each once. A merged pull request: the run enters `done`, and `sf run` finishes it as step 4 of 12.3 says. A review that requests changes and has started no round: ORCH-10. A test-merge that conflicts: ORCH-11. Anything else, an approval included, since under the strategy `human` the human merges (12.3): `sf run` reports that the run waits and exits with 0.
  - **Why it ends there.** `sf run` holds the store lock while it runs (CLI-3), and a second `sf run` cannot start beside it. A review can take days. A run in `human_review` holds no slot (7.3), and by the same reasoning it holds no lock: a second issue runs while the first waits for its review, and M1b's exit is five issues (22).
  - **What nothing does in the meantime.** No tick runs while no `sf run` does. A conflict with the default branch that arises during a review is found by the next `sf run` on the issue, or by GitHub when the operator merges. A run whose pull request the operator merged stays non-terminal in the store until `sf run` is run on its issue once more, though GitHub may have closed the issue by then (7.2). So that none is forgotten, `sf run` names at its start every other non-terminal run of the store, with its issue and its stage. The nudge of PR-3 goes to the dashboard issue, which is M2.
  - **Not taken.** Staying in the foreground until the pull request is merged, polling as GH-18 does, which is what 12.3 assumed: the lock is held for days, and the five issues run one after another unless the operator interrupts. Ending at every wait, `ci` included, was not taken either: the operator would run the command again to read a result that `ci.timeout_secs` bounds.

---

## 9. Workspaces and git

### 9.1 Layout

```
<data_dir>/
  store.db                         journal (section 18)
  mirrors/<owner>/<repo>.git       bare mirror, fetched by the host only
  advisory-dbs/                    the host's clone of the advisory database, fetched by the host only (VAL-1a)
  sandbox-exec/<id>/               home, tmp, target and proxy.sock of one `sf sandbox exec`, under an id of its own, removed when it ends (CLI-6)
  runs/<run_id>/
    workspace/                     implementer's clone (untrusted)
    host.git/                      host-owned bare repository for candidates and publishing
    quarantine/                    repository of the sandboxed staging git: its index and the objects it writes (9.4)
    ingest/                        object directory the host indexes a captured pack into before it enters host.git (9.4)
    target/                        CARGO_TARGET_DIR for implementer stage executions
    handoff/                       plan.md, progress.md, feedback/*.md, diff files (host-written)
    exec/<seq>/home/               fresh HOME, CARGO_HOME and agent config per stage execution
    exec/<seq>/tmp/                the temporary directory of the stage execution, which a worker sees as /tmp (SEC-6c)
    exec/<seq>/proxy.sock          the egress proxy's listener for the stage execution (SEC-10b)
    exec/<seq>/target/             CARGO_TARGET_DIR for every other stage execution (new and empty each time)
    exec/<seq>/export/             clean checkout of the candidate, for one verify or one review (WS-14)
    events.jsonl                   raw agent event stream, hash-chained
    stages/<seq>-<stage>.json      stage results
    logs/                          full command output
```

`handoff/` and `logs/` are host-written. Workers see a per-role, read-only view of them outside the workspace root: the reviewer's view excludes `progress.md` and implementer feedback. The host writes `progress.md` from the `notes` field of each result, so it never reads a file out of a workspace.

### 9.2 Workspace creation

- **WS-1.** The host fetches into the bare mirror, then creates `workspace/` with `git clone --no-local --no-checkout --single-branch --branch <default> <mirror> workspace`, checks out `base_sha` detached and removes the remote, all before any worker touches the directory. The checkout applies no in-tree attributes (WS-11). `--reference`, `--shared`, hard-linked and plain local-path clones are not used: the workspace MUST contain its own object store holding only objects reachable from the base and from this run's own candidates, because the mirror is not visible inside the sandbox and holds other runs' unmerged branches.
- **WS-2.** Directory names are derived from ids, never from issue text. The agent's working directory MUST be the workspace root.
- **WS-3.** The agent MAY run git in its workspace, including committing for its own checkpoints. Nothing in `workspace/.git` is ever used by the host.
- **WS-4.** Policy may define `setup` commands (for example `cargo fetch --locked`). They run inside the sandbox, with a timeout, at the start of every stage execution that builds: each implementer session and each verify. Setup is not a stage execution of its own: it runs as the first step of the activity it prepares, in the same sandbox instance with the same home and target directory, under `setup.timeout_secs`. A setup failure fails that activity with category `infrastructure`.
- **WS-5.** Run directories are removed 24 hours after a terminal state *(tunable)*; `target/` and exports are removed earlier (9.3).
- **WS-6.** The workspace tree MUST derive from the previous candidate. After a base update (12.4) the host discards `workspace/` and re-creates it from the update commit, so a later round can never revert what landed upstream.
- **WS-7.** The host resolves the toolchain that `rust-toolchain.toml` names at the base commit to a directory on the host, and puts that directory's `bin` first on the worker `PATH` (D-42). rustup and mise shims are never on the worker `PATH`: run in a directory that holds a `rust-toolchain.toml`, rustup 1.29.3 downloaded and installed the toolchain that the file named, for a command that only asked rustup's version (P-39). A missing toolchain, component or target is a blocking onboarding failure, and after onboarding it stops a claim as a missing check tool does (WS-7a).
  - **The file.** It is the file of that exact name at the root of the tree, read as data from the object database (INV-2). The file `rust-toolchain`, which rustup also reads, is not read. The host reads the table `[toolchain]` and three keys of it: `channel`, `components` and `targets`. A fourth, `profile`, may stand there and is tested for nothing: it names what rustup would install, and the host installs nothing. `channel` is a full version, three numbers written in digits with a dot between them, as `1.100.0`. The host compares no version: it looks for a directory of that name. A component's name is made of lowercase letters, digits and hyphens and begins with a letter; a target's name may hold a dot and an underscore as well. The host cannot resolve a file that is not valid TOML, that has no `[toolchain]` table or no `channel` in it, that holds another table or key, a `path` among them, or that holds a channel, a component or a target of another form. A tree without the file names no toolchain. Each of these counts as a missing toolchain, and the host says what is wrong with the file where it would name the toolchain. rustup itself takes a file without a `channel`, by its manual (P-39).
  - **Why a full version.** `stable`, `1.100` and a nightly are channels to rustup. It gave `1.100` and `stable` a directory each, beside that of `1.100.0`, and on that day all three held the compiler of 1.100.0 (P-39). What the first two hold changes when the operator next updates them, with no change in any repository. A dated channel, `nightly-2026-11-20` for one, names one build as a full version does. It was not taken with this decision, and no such directory was observed.
  - **The directory.** Operator configuration names one toolchains directory for the `rust` ecosystem, `[ecosystem.rust] toolchains_dir` (16.4). It has no default (CFG-4), and the factory's user needs to read it and nothing more. The host looks for the toolchain at `<toolchains directory>/<channel>-<host triple>`. The host triple is `x86_64-unknown-linux-gnu` or `aarch64-unknown-linux-gnu`, by the architecture that `sf` was built for (4.4). That is the name rustup gives a toolchain's directory, below the directory `toolchains` of its home (P-39); its manual gives the name of a toolchain and not where one lies. `RUSTUP_HOME=<dir> rustup toolchain install <channel> --profile minimal`, with `--component` for each component and `--target` for each target, therefore leaves the layout, and the key names `<dir>/toolchains`. The host runs neither rustup nor a program of the toolchain to find it: the version is what the path says, as for a check tool. A key that names a rustup home was not taken, since the host reads one directory of such a home. A layout of the factory's own, `<channel>` alone as WS-7a has `<name>/<version>`, was not taken either: no tool leaves it.
  - **What is present.** The toolchain is present when its directory holds `bin/rustc` and `bin/cargo`, each a regular file that the factory's user may execute, and the file `lib/rustlib/components` in it holds the line `rust-std-<host triple>`. The toolchains directory is the operator's, so the host follows a symbolic link there, at the toolchain's directory or below it, and the sandbox shows the directory that the link leads to. A program counts only where it lies in that directory once every link is followed: the sandbox shows that directory alone, and a link that leads out of it would lead nowhere there. That file is rustup's record of what a toolchain holds, one name to a line. A component is present when the file holds its name or its name with `-preview` after it, each with `-<host triple>` at the end or without: `clippy` stood there as `clippy-preview-aarch64-apple-darwin` and `rust-src` as `rust-src` (P-39). A target is present when the file holds `rust-std-<target>`. `rustup component remove` took a component's line and its programs away together, and `rustup target remove` the line and the libraries (P-39). A table in the code that names a file for each component was not taken, since a component that the table lacks could not be resolved. Testing the directory alone was not taken either: a missing component would then show as a failed verify round and not as a refused claim. The pages of rustup's manual that were read do not mention the file (P-39). Where a later rustup writes it otherwise, a component reads as missing, and the claim stops.
  - **What a worker is shown.** The sandbox shows the directory of that one toolchain read-only (SEC-6) and no other part of the toolchains directory. With the `bin` of a copy of a toolchain first on a cleared `PATH`, a crate was built, tested, linted and checked by rustfmt: the copy lay under another path and name, with no write permission on any entry, there was no rustup, and `HOME` and `CARGO_HOME` were elsewhere (P-39). `cargo` is then the toolchain's own program. It took no notice of a `rust-toolchain.toml` beside the crate that named another version, and it refused `cargo +1.97.1` as an unknown command. A candidate that changes the file is therefore built with the toolchain of its base commit all the same, and the change is flagged (11.3). The file that the next claim after the merge reads is the new one.
  - **Where the code lies.** What the file names is a pure function of the `rust` implementation of `Ecosystem` (9.5): from the bytes of the file and the host triple to the name of the directory and the list of what it has to hold, or to what is wrong with the file. `factory-daemon` looks on disk (19.1). M0 builds both, since `sf sandbox exec` resolves a toolchain (CLI-6).
  - **Not observed.** P-39 ran on macOS, with rustup 1.29.3 and the toolchain 1.100.0. No toolchain was looked for on Linux, and none was shown to a sandbox. The cold build of M0's exit check is the first (BOOT-3).
- **WS-7a.** A repository's setup commands and checks run programs that are no part of its toolchain: `cargo-nextest`, `cargo-deny` or `typos`, for example. Each is a check tool, and it reaches a worker as the toolchain does (D-27).
  - **The policy names them.** `[tools]` in the policy (16.2) holds one key per check tool, its name, with one exact version as the value. There is no range and no "latest". A name is made of lowercase letters, digits and hyphens and begins with a letter. A version is made of letters, digits, dots, hyphens and `+` and begins with a letter or a digit. A policy with any other name or version is invalid (16.3), because both become directory names on the host.
  - **The operator installs them.** The host installs nothing: it neither downloads nor builds a check tool. Operator configuration names one tools directory, `tools_dir` (16.4). It has no default, since no home directory is built in (CFG-4), and the factory's user needs to read it and nothing more. The host looks for a check tool at `<tools directory>/<name>/<version>/bin`, and the tool is present when that `bin` directory exists and holds at least one executable file. The host follows a symbolic link at `<name>/<version>` or below it as it does in the toolchains directory (WS-7): the sandbox shows the directory that the link leads to, and an executable file counts only where it lies in that directory once every link is followed. For a tool that is a crate, `cargo install --locked --version <version> --root <tools directory>/<name>/<version> <crate>` leaves that layout. The host does not run a tool to ask its version: the version is what the path says. `factory-daemon` makes the test, as it makes that of the toolchain (WS-7, 19.1).
  - **One set, wherever the toolchain goes.** A sandbox that is given the toolchain is given the check tools. The host puts each `bin` on the worker `PATH` after the toolchain's, in order of name, and the sandbox shows the directory `<name>/<version>` of each named tool read-only (SEC-6) and no other part of the tools directory. From M2 the guest image holds them too (SEC-9). There is one set and no list per role: the implementer has the check tools that verify has. A repository whose implementer is to run more checks than its verify list names more check tools.
  - **A missing one stops the claim.** A check tool that the policy names and the host lacks is a blocking onboarding failure (20.1), and `sf doctor` reports it (CLI-1). A policy merged after onboarding can name a version that the host does not hold, as a change to `rust-toolchain.toml` can name a toolchain. Step 1 of the claim therefore resolves the toolchain and the check tools of the commit it reads (8.2). Where one is missing the factory claims no issue on that repository, and it names each missing tool with its version, or the toolchain or component, when it refuses. A run takes up a newer policy or base commit at a reset or a base update (16.3). An activity of that run whose sandbox cannot be given the toolchain or a check tool fails as a Deterministic failure and escalates with `infrastructure` (8.6).
- **WS-10.** `host.git` is created at claim as a bare, single-branch clone of the mirror's default branch (`--no-local`), with its remote removed. Before every test-merge, base update or rebuild the host fetches the default branch (and, on rebuild, this run's work branch) from the mirror into it. It holds only objects reachable from the default branch and from this run's candidates. A workspace made from a commit that is not on the default branch (WS-6, 12.4, 8.4) is cloned from `host.git` at a temporary ref on that commit in the same way as WS-1; the temporary ref is deleted afterwards.

Linked worktrees are not used: they share refs, config and hooks with the hub repository, which conflicts with INV-2.

### 9.3 Build output

- `CARGO_TARGET_DIR` is outside the workspace. Implementer stage executions of one attempt share `runs/<run_id>/target`. `plan`, `author_tests`, `verify` and `review` each get a new, empty target directory on every execution, so a build there is cold each time. That cost is accepted; CI is the second clean-environment gate.
- `target/`, per-execution target directories and exports are deleted when the run enters `human_review` or a terminal state.
- No cache writable by a worker is shared between runs, or between the implementer and any other stage. `sccache` is not supported in v1. Seeding target directories from a host-built copy-on-write clone MAY be added after probe P-13 measures it.

### 9.4 Capture: building the candidate

Capture runs after the implementer's sandbox instance is destroyed and the process sweep (AG-18) is empty.

1. **Scan (host, `lstat` only, never following symlinks).** Fail if any entry other than the root `.git` has a name that git would treat as `.git` (case-insensitive, including filesystem-equivalent spellings), at any depth; or if any entry is not a regular file, directory or symlink. What git treats as `.git` is its rule for that one name and not the fold of VAL-5d, which knows no spelling of HFS+ or NTFS (P-29). The root `.git` is not entered. The scan covers ignored directories, and it records the type and size of every entry. It runs before any git command, because git leaves some of these entries out of its listings without a message (an untracked FIFO or socket, a `.git` entry it does not take for a repository, anything in an ignored directory), and git blocks when `.gitignore` is a FIFO (P-7).
2. **Stage (sandboxed git).** `quarantine/` is a repository the host creates anew for each capture: an empty object directory with `host.git`'s objects as an alternate, and a host-written configuration (hooks, filters, fsmonitor, system and global config all disabled; no in-tree attributes, WS-11). The host's git binary runs with `quarantine/` as its repository and the workspace as its work tree, in a new sandbox instance with no network and no credentials, under the filesystem policy of SEC-6 for capture. A capture is one stage execution, so the git commands of this step run one after another in that one instance (13.3). The staging git builds a fresh index from the tree the workspace was last created or captured from (the previous candidate's tree, `base_sha` on the first round, or W's tree in a merge-conflict round, 12.4), so upstream content is never counted as the round's change set. The index file lives in `quarantine/`; nothing in `workspace/.git` is read (WS-3). It then lists the change set: changed and deleted tracked paths with `git diff-files`, submodules ignored, and untracked paths with `git ls-files --others --exclude-standard`. The ignore rules are therefore the `.gitignore` files in the workspace as they stand at capture, the agent's edits included, and nothing else: `quarantine/` has no `info/exclude`, no global excludes file is configured, and the workspace's own `info/exclude` is not read. An ignore rule never drops a tracked path. The host checks the listing against the inventory of step 1, without touching the workspace again. It rejects any listed path that is neither a regular file nor a symlink (git lists a nested repository as its directory) or exceeds 5 MiB, and any change set over the total-bytes cap. If nothing is rejected, the staging git removes the deleted paths from the index (`git update-index --force-remove`), adds the others (`git update-index --add`) and lists the change set again. A path still listed was not staged, and that fails the capture: `update-index` skips a path it refuses and still exits 0. The staging git then writes a tree and emits the objects it wrote as one pack on its standard output (`git pack-objects --local`). A rejected path fails the capture; the host never builds a candidate that silently omits one.
3. **Ingest (host, bare `host.git`).** The host takes two things from the sandbox, the name of the tree and the pack, and opens nothing in `quarantine/`. It indexes the pack into `ingest/`, a new object directory with `host.git`'s objects as an alternate, using `git index-pack --strict` with `--max-input-size` set from the total-bytes cap. That recomputes the name of every object, runs the `fsck` checks, and fails if the pack refers to an object that is neither in the pack nor in `host.git`. The host then walks the new tree and rejects: any entry whose mode is not `100644`, `100755` or `120000`, so submodule entries cannot enter a candidate; two paths that have the same fold, unless the base tree holds both (WS-12); a path in which the new tree differs from the base tree and which lies below a directory with the layout of a git repository (WS-13); and, by the sizes of the blobs that differ from the previous tree, any file or change set over the capture limits. Only then does the pack move into `host.git`. After a rejection `ingest/` is deleted and `host.git` is unchanged.
4. **Commit.** `git commit-tree` with the previous published head (or `base_sha`) as parent. From a conflicting base update until the next push, the parents are instead W's two parents (previous published head, new default-branch head), never W itself, so the default-branch head stays an ancestor of the work branch. The commit carries the factory identity, journalled dates, the validated subject and the host-written body and trailers. Journal the candidate and tree SHA.

**Capture limits** *(tunable)*: 5 MiB per file, 50 MiB of new or changed bytes per candidate, and a 300 s timeout for the sandboxed staging step. They are keys of operator configuration, `[limits.capture]`, and these values are the defaults (16.4). A policy of version 1 has no key for them and lowers none (CFG-5). A capture timeout is an abandoned activity (8.6, Poison; through M1, ORCH-11).

**Subject validation.** The subject is a single line of at most 72 characters without control characters. A skip token is one of `[skip ci]`, `[ci skip]`, `[no ci]`, `[skip actions]`, `[actions skip]` and `skip-checks`, in any letter case. The subject is rejected, returning to `implement`, if it contains a skip token or anything GH-5a forbids. The forms of a reference are those of the encoder's module in `factory-core`, which M1a builds with capture (GH-5a, D-33). The pull request title and the squash title are generated by the host from the issue title, after the sanitisation of SEC-4, with every reference (GH-5a) removed; the squash message is host-written (5.6). No skip token is removed from a title (D-45). A generated title is fit for a pull request when it holds no skip token and at least one character that is no white space. The claim ends on an issue whose generated title is not fit (WI-4), and from M3 the lint refuses the issue (6.4), so no other title reaches a pull request. The rule reads the generated title and not the issue's own, because the removal of a reference can join what stood on its two sides.

**Export.** `verify` and `review` use a clean checkout of the candidate made from `host.git`: `git --git-dir=host.git read-tree <candidate>` into a temporary index, then `git --git-dir=host.git --work-tree=runs/<run_id>/exec/<seq>/export checkout-index -a` into that new, empty, host-created directory (bare `host.git` has no work tree of its own, and `checkout-index` needs one), with no filter configured and no attribute applied (WS-11; left to itself, `checkout-index` applies the candidate's own `.gitattributes`). `git archive` MUST NOT be used, because it honours `export-ignore` from the candidate's own `.gitattributes`. The export is writable for `verify` and read-only for `review`. It belongs to one stage execution, and no export is used a second time (WS-14).

**Publish.** Push the journalled candidate to `refs/heads/factory/issue-<n>` over HTTPS. The installation token reaches `git push` through a credential helper built into `sf` that receives it over an inherited pipe; it never appears in arguments, environment or a file (INV-1). Then create the draft pull request if no open one exists for the head branch, otherwise update its body, and report the three factory checks for the head SHA.

- **WS-8.** All git mutations run through the `git` CLI in one wrapper crate that parses only porcelain or NUL-delimited output. `gix` MAY be used for read-only fast paths. `git2` is not used. Fetches into a mirror are serialised per repository. Every git command of the host names its repository, with `--git-dir` or with the path that `init` and `clone` take, and none finds a repository from its working directory. The host's commands therefore work on a bare repository under the setting of WS-15.
- **WS-9.** The capture, ingest and export design MUST be prototyped in M0 against a hostile-tree suite before the pipeline depends on it: nested repositories (directory, gitfile, `.GIT` case variant) carrying `core.fsmonitor`; hooks and config; symlinked directories; FIFOs and hard links; `.gitattributes` with filters, `export-ignore` and `-diff`; an agent-edited `.gitignore`; LFS pointers; case collisions, which are two paths with one fold (WS-12); a directory with the layout of a git repository under a name other than `.git` (WS-13); executable bits; large files; an object from another run's branch must not be readable in a fresh workspace. Probe P-7 did this for the git commands of this section, without the factory's sandbox, and added cases: a `.git` entry that names no repository; a special file or a nested repository in an ignored directory; a FIFO named `.gitignore`; a tracked directory replaced by a symlink; attributes that change bytes (`eol`, `ident`, `working-tree-encoding`), in the base commit and in the round; and a hostile pack or tree name in place of the staging git's output. It left two cases open, which D-43 decided: its prototype rejected two names that differ only in case with a check that this section did not hold, and it admitted a directory with the layout of a bare repository. The suite asserts WS-12 and WS-13 for the two, with the trees and the layouts of probe P-40: a pair that a round adds is rejected and a pair that the base tree holds is not, and a path that a round changes below a layout is rejected whether the round or the base tree brought the layout in.
- **WS-11.** No in-tree attribute is applied when content moves between a tree and a directory. The workspace checkout (WS-1), staging and the export run with the attribute source set to the empty tree, as the diffs of VAL-5a do. Each therefore moves bytes unchanged: an untouched workspace is an empty change set, and every path of a candidate has in the export the bytes it had in the workspace. The source is set with the `--attr-source` option and not with the `GIT_ATTR_SOURCE` variable alone, because a git too old to know the option rejects it but ignores the variable and applies the attributes. The cost is that `text`, `eol`, `ident` and `working-tree-encoding` do nothing in a workspace, a candidate or an export: a file the agent writes with CRLF line endings is committed with them (P-7).
- **WS-12.** Two paths of a tree that have the same fold are rejected, unless the base tree holds both (D-43). The paths are those of every entry of the tree, at any depth and of any kind: a file, a symbolic link or a directory. The fold is that of VAL-5d. The base tree is the tree of the run's `base_sha`, the default-branch commit that the workspace derives from (8.2), which a reset and a base update replace (7.6, 12.4). In the round that resolves the conflicts of a base update it is already the tree of the new default-branch head (AG-11a). Ingest makes the test on the tree of every capture (step 3), and the host makes it on the tree of every test-merge and of every base update (12.3, 12.4). A rejection at ingest returns to `implement` as any rejected path does (7.3), with both paths in the feedback.
  - **Why.** git has no such rule. It staged two such names (P-7), and `fsck --strict` and `index-pack --strict` accepted each of seven such trees (P-40). On Linux an export held both paths of each. On a file system that is not case-sensitive it cannot. On the operator's Mac the export of `README.md` beside `readme.md` held one file, and `checkout-index` exited with 1; the directories `src` and `SRC` became one directory that held the files of both, with exit status 0; and a clone of such a branch left `git status` reporting a change in a work tree that nobody had touched (P-40). What verify and CI build on Linux is then not what a checkout on such a machine holds. VAL-5d already takes the two for one path.
  - **Which pairs.** The export on the Mac held one file as well where only full case folding or normalisation makes two names one: `straße.txt` beside `STRASSE.txt`, `é.md` as one code point beside the same as two, and `AGENTS.md` beside that name with a long s (P-40). APFS gave the answer of the fold in each of the 21 pairs of P-29. A comparison that folds the letters A to Z alone, as git's does (P-29), was not taken. Admitting what git admits was not taken either.
  - **Paths, and not the names of one directory.** Where a tree holds the directories `src` and `SRC`, the files `src/a.rs` and `SRC/a.rs` are one file on the Mac, whose export held one `a.rs` (P-40). A rule that compared the names inside each directory would admit the second file wherever the base tree holds the two directories.
  - **What the base tree holds.** A pair is admitted when the base tree has an entry at each of its two paths, whatever the run did to either. A repository whose default branch holds such a pair therefore fails no capture, and a round may change both files. A rule over the whole tree was not taken: claim and onboarding would then have to refuse such a repository, as they refuse one with a submodule entry (8.2, 20.1). The readiness report of onboarding lists each such pair of the tree at the default-branch head as an advisory item (20.1).
  - **The base tree, and not the previous tree.** The operator was asked with the previous tree of step 2 in this place. The two give the same answer but in two cases. A pair of the base tree of which an earlier round removed one path may come back. And after a base update that conflicted, the previous tree is that of W (12.4), which holds a pair that the merge made of one path of the run and one of the default branch. Against the previous tree that pair would be admitted in the round that follows. The base tree is by then the tree of the new default-branch head, which does not hold it.
  - **A name that is not valid UTF-8** is folded as VAL-5d folds such a path, with U+FFFD in the place of each ill-formed sequence. Two paths that differ only in such bytes are rejected as one, which is a rejection too many.
  - **Where the rule lies.** The fold is the function of VAL-5d, and the walk of a tree is capture's, in `factory-git` (19.1).
- **WS-13.** A run brings no git repository into a candidate under a name other than `.git`, and it changes none that the base tree holds (D-43). A directory has the layout of a repository when it holds an entry named `HEAD` together with entries named `objects` and `refs`, or together with an entry named `commondir`. An entry counts whatever it is: a file, a directory or a symbolic link. Paths are compared by the fold of VAL-5d, so the entries of two directories count as those of one where the paths of the two have the same fold. The host rejects every path in which a tree differs from the base tree (WS-12), whether the path was added, changed or removed, that lies below a directory which has the layout in that tree, or which has it in the base tree and still holds an entry in that tree. The root of a tree is such a directory like any other. Ingest makes the test on the tree of every capture (step 3), and the host makes it on the tree of every test-merge and of every base update (12.3, 12.4). The host reads the paths from the two trees (VAL-5a), and not from the listing of step 2. A rejection at ingest returns to `implement` as any rejected path does (7.3).
  - **Why.** git that is started in such a directory of a checkout, or anywhere below it, takes the directory for a repository and reads its configuration. In each layout that it took and that had a `config`, git ran an alias that the `config` named, and in each but the one with `commondir` it ran the fsmonitor of that `config` on `git status`: in the work tree and in a clone of it, on git 2.56.1 and on git 2.47.3 (P-40). The scan of step 1 refuses a name that git treats as `.git` and no layout, and git staged the files of such a directory like any others (P-7). A subverted implementer could so add what looks like the fixture of a test. Once merged, the command runs as whoever next starts git in that directory, on a checkout that holds that person's credentials.
  - **Why these names.** The rule is git's test for a git directory as it was observed. git took a directory for a repository where it held `HEAD` with `objects` and `refs`, also where those two were symbolic links or regular files of mode `100755`, and where it held `HEAD` with a file `commondir` that named a directory holding the two. It needed no `config` for that. It took none where `refs` was missing, where `HEAD` held another word, or where `objects` and `refs` were files of mode `100644` (P-40). The rule reads names, and neither the content of `HEAD` nor a mode, so it rejects the last two as well: a rejection too many. It reads a tree, and git tracks no empty directory. A directory of the base tree that lacks an entry of the layout, and gains it when a test or a person creates an empty `refs`, is no layout to this rule, and a run may change its `config`.
  - **Why the fold.** The Mac's file system took `head`, `Objects` and `REFS` for the three names. A tree with `fix/HEAD` in one directory and `FIX/config`, `FIX/objects` and `FIX/refs` in another became one directory in an export on the Mac, and git ran the alias of its `config` there (P-40).
