  - **What nobody observed.** No program has run under this filter. Whether a cold build or an agent session makes a call that it refuses, and what each then does, is probe P-37. Where one does, the table changes with the probe's record.
- **SEC-7.** The sandbox fails closed. `sf daemon` dispatches only at `hardened`; `process` is accepted for attended `sf run`, for `sf sandbox exec` (CLI-6) and for `sf contract` that a person starts (CLI-8); `none` only with the fake agent. If the required level cannot be provided, the run does not start.
- **SEC-8.** The sandbox wraps the whole agent process, not only its shell commands. The agent CLI's built-in sandbox covers only its Bash tool and is not relied on; AG-8 keeps it off.
- **SEC-9.** The microVM guest image is built by the host from a pinned base, contains the resolved toolchain, the check tools of the repository's policy (WS-7a) and the agent CLI, and is mounted read-only. Its digest is journalled for every run. Repositories that need another operating system to build are out of scope.

### 13.4 Network egress

- **SEC-10.** Workers have no direct network access. The only reachable endpoint is the control plane's egress proxy, which has a listener of its own for each sandbox instance. At level `process` the listener is a Unix socket that the sandbox shows, and the first process forwards a loopback port to it (SEC-10a). At level `hardened` it is a host-side interface that routes only to the proxy. The listener that a connection arrives on names the stage execution, and that is how a connection is attributed to a run (AG-19). SEC-10b says what the proxy speaks, at either level.
- **SEC-10a.** The first process of a sandbox at level `process` (D-40). It is `sf __sandbox-init`, a hidden subcommand of the one binary, as the supervisor shim is (AG-16). bubblewrap starts it as its command and with `--as-pid-1`, so it is PID 1 of the sandbox's PID namespace and bubblewrap puts no process of its own there. It forwards a worker's connections to the proxy, and it starts the worker's command.
  - **What it does,** in this order. It binds a listener to `127.0.0.1`, port 3128, in the sandbox's network namespace, whose loopback interface bubblewrap has brought up. It starts the command as the leader of a process group of its own (AG-11), on the standard input, output and error that it was itself started with, and it reads and writes none of the three. For each connection that the listener accepts it opens one to the proxy's socket (SEC-10b), which the sandbox shows at one fixed path, and it copies the bytes both ways until both directions have ended. It reads nothing of them and holds no rule: the proxy decides. As PID 1 it reaps every process that is left to it. When the command ends it exits with the command's status, or with 128 plus the number of the signal that ended the command, and the kernel then ends every process still in the namespace (AG-16).
  - **Why the listener comes first.** The port accepts connections before the command exists, so no command finds the proxy missing.
  - **The port.** It is one number for every instance, since each has a network namespace of its own. A program of the repository that binds port 3128 in a worker fails to.
  - **Signals.** bubblewrap passes no signal on to what it starts, and a SIGTERM ends its outer process. The backend starts it with `--die-with-parent`, so that end is a SIGKILL for PID 1 and with it for the whole sandbox. The signals of the stop sequence (AG-12) therefore go to the first process and not to `bwrap`. bubblewrap writes the pid of the first process, as the host sees it, to `--json-status-fd`. The backend reads it there and names that process to the stop sequence of `factory-proc`, which signals what a sandbox instance names: the command's group at level `none`, this one process at level `process`. `factory-proc` knows nothing of bubblewrap, and the shim hands the descriptor through unread. How the traits of 13.3 carry that name, and which pid and group AG-15 journals at this level, is left to the change that builds the backend. The first process passes a SIGINT and a SIGTERM on to the command's group. A SIGKILL ends the first process, and with its PID 1 the kernel ends the namespace. The group of AG-11 stays the command's own: the first process is no member of it.
  - **What a worker sees of it.** The sandbox shows the `sf` binary read-only (SEC-6): the file that the host itself runs, at one fixed path. The binary needs the loader and the C library of the OS baseline. A worker can start it. It finds no operator configuration, no credential and no store (SEC-6), and `sf __sandbox-init` does nothing that a worker cannot do itself, since a worker can connect to the proxy's socket directly. The first process runs as the worker's user, inside the sandbox, and the control plane trusts it with nothing: it holds no secret and decides nothing. What a worker does to it, the worker does to its own sandbox.
  - **Not `socat`.** `socat` as the forwarder was not taken. It would be a requirement of 4.4 on the server, in the development VM and on the hosted runners, in whichever version each installs. Something would still have to start it beside the command and wait for its port, and it passes no signal on. The server holds `socat` (4.4), and the sandbox does not use it.
  - **Where the code lies.** In `factory-sandbox`, as the half of the bubblewrap backend that runs inside the namespace (19.1). It starts the command through `factory-proc` (19.3) and needs no code of `factory-net`: what it shares with the proxy is the path of a socket. Its connections are tasks of one set that ends with it, since 19.3 allows no bare `tokio::spawn`.
  - **Other levels.** Level `none` has no first process, and the shim starts the command. What starts the command in the guest of level `hardened` is left to the change that builds the guest image (M2).
  - **What nobody observed.** None of this ran. What it says of bubblewrap was read in the manual and the source of 0.9.0 and 0.12.0 (`docs/research/19-bubblewrap-backend-sources.md`). Whether a kernel does with it what is said here is probe P-37.
- **SEC-10b.** What the egress proxy speaks (D-40).
  - **The listener.** At level `process`, one Unix socket for each sandbox instance, `exec/<seq>/proxy.sock` (9.1), with mode 0600. The host creates it before the instance and removes it afterwards. It lies in no directory that a worker can write, and the sandbox shows the socket and not the directory around it. The socket that a connection arrives on names the stage execution and with it the run (SEC-10). The proxy asks for no credential and ignores a `Proxy-Authorization` header. The path of a Unix socket is limited in length: where the path does not fit, the host does not create the instance and says why. What the listener is at level `hardened`, and how a worker there is told of it, is left to the change that builds the microVM backend (M2).
  - **One request.** The proxy speaks HTTP/1.1 and reads one request head from a connection, of at most 8 KiB and within 10 s *(both tunable; 8 KiB is the least that `hyper` accepts as its cap)*.
  - **What it allows.** A `CONNECT` whose target is `<host>:443`, where the host is a name on the list of SEC-11. The name is compared whole, after the letters A to Z are folded to lower case, with no wildcard and no suffix. Every entry of the list is a name, an entry of operator configuration too, and the port is 443 for each. An IP address is never on the list, so a target that holds one is refused. The proxy resolves the name with the host's resolver and connects. It answers `200` and then copies the bytes both ways, unread, until both directions have ended. It does not terminate TLS. It therefore sees host names and never a path, a header or a body, as the proxy in the harness of probe P-22 did.
  - **What it refuses.** Everything else. The proxy answers with an error, closes the connection and adds a record to the denied-request log. There are four reasons. Where more than one applies, the first in the order of the table is given.

    | Reason | The request | The answer |
    |---|---|---|
    | `malformed` | A head that cannot be read as HTTP/1.1, or one that is too long or too slow | What `hyper` answers by itself: `400`, `431` for a head that is too long, and nothing for one that is too slow. Such a head never reaches the proxy's own code, so the proxy chooses neither the status nor a text (the source of `hyper` 1.13.0) |
    | `method` | Any request that is no `CONNECT`. A plain HTTP request that a program sends through `HTTP_PROXY` is one | `403`, with one line that gives the reason |
    | `host` | A `CONNECT` to a name that is not on the list, or to an IP address | The same |
    | `port` | A `CONNECT` to a listed name on a port other than 443 | The same |

  - **A failure is no denial.** A listed name that does not resolve, or a host that does not accept the connection within 10 s *(tunable)*, gets `502`. The proxy logs it as a failure and not in the denied-request log: steps 5 to 7 of M0's exit check ask for that log to be empty (BOOT-3), and an outage of the registry is no request that the list refused.
  - **The denied-request log.** One record for each refusal: the time, the run and the stage execution, the method and the target as they were sent, cut at 255 bytes *(tunable)*, and the reason. A record with the reason `malformed` holds neither. Method and target are a worker's text, and they are data (INV-9). `sf sandbox exec` has no run and reports the records itself (CLI-6). Where a run keeps them is left to the change that builds the journal (M1a).
  - **The variables.** At level `process` the host sets `HTTPS_PROXY` and `HTTP_PROXY` to `http://127.0.0.1:3128`, the port of SEC-10a, and `NO_PROXY` to `localhost,127.0.0.1,::1`, and each of the three under its lower-case name too (AG-11). Programs differ in which spelling they read, and the harness of P-22 set both spellings of the first two. `NO_PROXY` keeps a connection to a test's own listener on the loopback interface away from the proxy. `HTTP_PROXY` is set although the proxy forwards nothing plain, so that a plain request is refused and logged, and does not fail unseen on a network without a route.
  - **No name resolves in the sandbox.** A worker reaches no resolver. A program that hands the name to the proxy needs none. `cargo` and the agent CLI ran behind a proxy only where names resolve (P-22), so whether each completes without a resolver is part of probe P-37.
  - **While the activity lives.** When the activity ends, the proxy closes the listener and every tunnel that is still open (AG-19).
  - **Not taken.** Forwarding a plain HTTP request to a listed host: the registry and the model endpoint are reached over HTTPS, and no host of SEC-11 needs it. A check that the server name of the TLS handshake is the name of the `CONNECT`: it would narrow the abuse of an address that several hosts share (SEC-12) and not that of the model host itself, and it would put a parser for a worker's bytes into the control plane. SOCKS, and HTTP/2 between a worker and the proxy.
  - **Where the code lies.** In `factory-net`, on `hyper` with HTTP/1 alone (19.1, 19.2). Its connections are tasks of one set for each listener, which ends with the listener (19.3). The proxy is given, as a parameter, the function that turns a listed name into a connection. A test then stands a listener on the loopback interface in for a host, which it needs where the job has no network (DEV-6).
- **SEC-11.** The proxy allows a short hostname list: the model endpoint for the credential mode (the model gateway in `gateway` mode; in `subscription` and `direct_key` modes the hosts the agent CLI needs for model requests, fixed by the contract test: in `subscription` mode `api.anthropic.com` alone, probe P-22); the crate registry (`index.crates.io`, `static.crates.io`) or a configured mirror, for which version 1 of the operator configuration has no key (CFG-5); plus hosts added in operator configuration (`[egress] extra_hosts`, 16.4). `github.com` and `api.github.com` are never on a worker's list. Every denied request is logged with the run id (SEC-10b). Without the traffic switch in the environment of AG-11 the agent CLI also asks for a telemetry host, which is not on the list.
- **SEC-12.** Hostname filtering does not stop abuse of an allowed multi-tenant host. The allowlist is kept short for that reason, and repository policy cannot extend it. Cargo git dependencies outside the list are a blocking onboarding failure.

### 13.5 Credentials

| Credential | Held by | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| GitHub App private key | Control plane, from a 0600 file or a systemd credential | Never passed to any child process. The key of the factory's App is on the server only. The key of the scratch App is a 0600 file on the operator's Mac, outside every repository, and the development VM reads it from there (GH-11a, DEV-4). From the change that builds the scheduled end-to-end run, which is M2 work (22, D-46), it is also a secret of that workflow's protected environment (19.4). |
| Installation token | Control plane | One repository, about 1 hour. Reaches `git push` only through the built-in credential helper over an inherited pipe. A test dumps the arguments and environment of every host child during a push and finds no token. |
| Operator's own GitHub credential | Not stored | Used once by `sf repo check` to read the bypass list of each contributing ruleset (DEP-4) |
| Subscription token, `subscription` mode (default) | Control plane (0600 file or systemd credential); worker environment | The operator creates it with `claude setup-token` from their own Claude Max plan, on a machine with a browser since the server is headless. They write it by hand into a file of mode 0600 that the operator configuration names, in `[credentials] subscription_token_file` (16.4), and `sf doctor` checks the mode (CLI-1). That holds until `sf init` exists, which takes the token and arrives in M5 (CLI-7, D-42). It is passed to agent sessions only (never to setup, capture or verify) as `CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN`. Allowed for `sf run`, for `sf daemon`, for the agent form of `sf sandbox exec` (CLI-6) and for `sf contract` (CLI-8). It is exposed to a subverted worker, so: it can only make model requests; the egress allowlist limits where it can be sent; and the literal-token scan searches every candidate and every text written to GitHub for its value (SEC-13), which also says what a hit does and what the operator does after one. The CLI runs non-bare (AG-3a). No dollar bill can run away while usage credits are switched off for the account: use is then bounded by the plan's limits, which the factory shares with the operator's own interactive use. A session that completes reports the account's credit state in its `rate_limit_event` (probe P-22), and BUD-8 says what the factory does with it. |
| Model API key, `gateway` mode | Control plane | Not scheduled: the operator uses a Claude Max plan only (decision D-2). It stays specified so that an API key can be added later without redesign. The worker gets the gateway address and a per-run token valid only there. The gateway replaces any authentication header the worker sends, injects the key, meters usage, and forwards only message-creation and token-counting paths. |
| Model API key, `direct_key` mode | Worker environment | Attended `sf run` at level `process` only; `sf daemon` refuses to start with it, because a stolen pay-as-you-go key has no ceiling the factory controls. Use a key with a provider-side spend limit. |

- **SEC-13. The literal-token scan (D-47).** Before the host publishes anything, it searches what it is about to publish for the literal value of every credential that a worker's environment holds. Through M1 that is the subscription token alone, the one secret of SEC-6a. The installation token and the App's private key are not searched for, because no worker holds them (INV-1). The scan is the only secret rule of this specification: no pattern of a secret is matched.
  - **What it searches for.** The exact bytes of the value, and no encoded form of it. A copy in base64 or in hexadecimal is not found, and neither is a value that a line break or any other byte divides (23.3). The scan finds an environment that was dumped into a file, a test that recorded one, and an attack that does not hide the value.
  - **The value.** It is the content of the token file (16.4) without the white space at its end. A command that is allowed the token (13.5) reads the file once, when it starts. Every session that the command starts is given that value, and the scan searches for it until the command ends, so a token that was issued again is used and searched for from the next start. Where the value is shorter than 32 bytes *(tunable)*, the command exits with a failure before it does anything else, `sf run` before the first step of ORCH-8, and `sf doctor` reports the file (CLI-1). A short value is found by chance, and an empty one in every text. Where the operator configuration names no token file, no agent session starts (16.4), and there is no value to search for.
  - **A candidate.** The integrity stage reads three things of a candidate, as bytes (11.3).
    1. Every blob in which the candidate's tree differs from the tree at `base_sha`, whole and whatever it holds. A binary file is read as any other, and the target of a symbolic link is the content of such a blob (VAL-5f).
    2. Every path of that diff, as VAL-5a has the host list them: with no rename detected, so a file that moved is there under both of its paths.
    3. The commit message of the candidate, whole. Capture validated its subject, the one line of it that an agent chose (9.4), and the host wrote the rest. Nothing is published before the integrity stage, so the subject is read early enough there.

    No line is counted as added. A file that changed is read whole, and a file that moved is read at its new path (VAL-5a).
  - **A text for GitHub.** `factory-github` searches the text of every write: a title, a body, a comment, the output of a check run. The parts that the host wrote are searched as they are sent. A part that passes through the encoder of GH-5a is searched as it was before the encoder changed it and before a cap cut it. A write of which any part has a hit is not made. Both searches are made in `factory-github`, on the way that every write takes, so no write goes around them. In M1a the texts are the title and the body of the pull request, and M1b adds the status comment and the output of a check run. The search comes before the encoder because the encoder puts a zero-width space into a text at the places that GH-5a names. A value that holds one of them is no longer its literal bytes once it is encoded, and a reader takes the character out again. Whether a token can hold such a place was not looked up, and the order does not rest on it.
  - **A hit stops the run.** It is no `fail` of 11.3, and VAL-4 does not hold for it: no feedback is written and no session starts. A hit says that a session wrote its own credential into its work, by accident or because it was steered. After a return to `implement` the next session would start in the workspace that still holds the value, with feedback that speaks of it. Through M1 the stop is that of ORCH-11. Where the hit is in a text for GitHub, the stage that makes the write stops in the same way.
  - **A run that a hit stopped never goes on.** The `runs` row records the hit (ORCH-6). Run again on that run, `sf run` first reads GitHub as it does for any run that exists (ORCH-9), so a cancel takes effect. Unless that read ends the run, `sf run` reports the hit again and starts no stage. The first way on of ORCH-11, to remove the cause and run again, is not open here. Once the operator has re-issued the token the scan searches for the new value, and the old one would pass in the same candidate and be published. The operator re-issues the token, cancels the run and approves the issue again (ORCH-9). The new run has a workspace and a snapshot of its own.
  - **A value in the issue.** Where the value stands in a part of the issue that the host writes to GitHub again, which in M1a is the title and the outcome (VAL-14a), the candidate holds no hit and is pushed, and the write of the pull request is the one that is not made (9.4). A value in any other part of the issue is written nowhere and is no hit. GitHub holds the value already, in the issue. The operator takes it out of the issue and re-issues the token. A run that follows does not find the old value, since the scan no longer searches for it.
  - **From M2.** A hit is an escalation with the category `policy-denial` (7.5). Its only resume is a reset (7.6): a `state:ready` on such an escalation is handled as one, as it is after a workflow change in a `type:ci` issue (11.3). A category of its own was not taken. The rest of that escalation is M2 work.
  - **The report.** It names the place of a hit, and never the value or a byte that stands beside it. For a candidate the place is the path of the blob, or that a path holds the value, or that the commit message does. For a text it is the surface that the text was for, such as the body of the pull request. A path that holds the value is written with the marker `[credential]` in the place of the value. The report goes to the terminal and into the journal, and from M1b into the status comment as its last error (ORCH-11), which passes through the search as every write does.
  - **What is not searched.** The journal, the log of a session, the feedback files and the terminal. They stay on the host. The run directory of a run that a hit stopped holds the value, in its workspace and in `host.git`, until the retention of 18.2 removes it, which `sf gc` applies from M5. The factory's user can read the token file in any case.
  - **The search.** It is a pure function of `factory-core` (19.1): bytes and the values in, the places of the hits out. It uses the substring search of `memchr` (19.2). The integrity stage and `factory-github` both call it. 19.4 has its test, which plants a made-up value and never the operator's token.
  - **Not taken.** A set of secret patterns, which the table of 11.3 named as "Secret pattern in added content" until D-47 and nothing defined. The one secret that a worker holds is found by its value. Anything else that a pattern could match is text that an agent copied from the repository or from the issue, which GitHub holds already, or a test fixture that looks like a key, and that would fail a good candidate. Building the set later, with the onboarding of other repositories (M5), was not taken for the same reason, and neither was a stated set in M1a with a crate for regular expressions (19.2). A return to `implement` at a hit, and a search for encoded forms, were not taken either.
  - **Not observed.** What issuing a token again does to the one before it. This item has the operator re-issue the token after a hit, and nobody saw whether the old token then stops working. That is probe P-42.
  - **Left open: what a re-issue leaves behind.** After a re-issue the scan knows the new value alone. Another run of the store that is not terminal may hold the old value in its workspace, and its next candidate would pass. Whether that does harm is the question of P-42: a token that no longer works can be published without it. Until the probe is answered, nothing in this item covers such a run (23.3).

### 13.6 Autonomy levels and classes

| Level | Meaning |
|---|---|
| A0 | Lint and triage only; no code. |
| A1 | Plan only; the plan is posted for the operator. |
| A2 | The factory opens pull requests; the operator approves each one; the factory merges. Every class of work starts here. |
| A3 | A class of work merges without review. |
| Lights-out | The goal (G6): every class that a repository's tier 0 and tier 1 work falls into has earned A3. The operator still approves each issue (INV-4). |

**Classes.** Autonomy is granted per class of work, never per repository. A class is defined in operator configuration and has two parts:

- A **predicate** over the diff, deterministic and evaluated by the integrity check: an allowlist of path patterns (VAL-5b), a maximum changed-line count and allowed file modes. For dependency bumps the `Ecosystem` supplies it (for Rust: only version and checksum fields of crates already in `Cargo.lock`, the same registry source, and a minimum release age). A class defined only by labels or tier is invalid.
- The **evidence** it requires beyond the standard gates. The factory produces a mix of software, and what can be checked automatically differs by kind: for example a mutation-score floor for library and CLI code, a holdout scenario suite for service behaviour (11.7), browser-driven checks for user interfaces. A kind of work for which no such evidence is defined cannot be a class and stays at A2.

The expected ladder is: documentation; dependency bumps; test-only changes; small tasks in library and CLI code; then service code with scenario suites.

**A3 conditions**, all required for a head to merge without review: `enforced` mode with a verified bypass list; sandbox level `hardened`; risk within the class's ceiling (default `risk:low`), valid under SEC-1; the diff satisfies the class predicate; no integrity flag; all required checks and the class's evidence pass. For such a head the `human_review` stage is skipped, and 12.3 step 1 takes the class decision in place of an approval.

**Promotion** is an operator action (`sf class promote`), offered only when the class has its record: at least 30 merged pull requests at A2, a 30-day revert-or-rework rate under 5%, and the reviewer agent's verdict agreeing with the operator's on at least 95% of them *(all tunable; no published evidence supports specific numbers; decision D-14)*.

**After promotion** the factory marks 1 in 10 auto-merged pull requests of the class *(tunable)* for after-the-fact review on the dashboard issue; a problem found there counts as an escaped defect. Any tripwire in 15.3 demotes the class to A2 automatically; it must earn promotion again.

**Open design point.** How an A3 merge satisfies a ruleset that requires an approval, without the factory approving (INV-5), is not settled. Two candidate layouts are recorded as probe P-18 and the choice is made in M4. Until then 12.3 has no exception to PR-2.

Repository policy can only lower what operator configuration allows.

### 13.7 Kill switches

Each has a test.

1. `sf pause` / `sf drain`: stop dispatch locally; `drain` lets running stages reach a checkpoint.
2. A GitHub-visible stop: an open issue labelled `factory:stop` in the repository, valid under SEC-1, checked every tick. All stage executions in that repository are interrupted and nothing is published or merged. The stop stays in force until an authorised actor removes the label or closes the issue; closure by anyone else, or through a pull request, is ignored. If the stop check itself fails, no new stage starts and nothing is published or merged; running stage executions are interrupted after 3 consecutive failed checks *(tunable)*.
3. Suspend the App installation in GitHub settings.
4. Revoke the model API key.

### 13.8 Audit

Every run keeps an append-only record: actor and permission at trigger, snapshot hash, policy SHA, sandbox level and record, models, gate verdicts, cost, candidate SHAs (journal), and tool calls with allow and deny decisions (`events.jsonl`). The supervisor hash-chains `events.jsonl`; the engine journals the final digest of each activity; the run's combined digest is written into the pull request body.

---

## 14. Budgets and cost

Limits are enforced by the control plane. In `gateway` mode usage is metered by the gateway. In `subscription` and `direct_key` modes usage comes from the agent CLI's own reports, and dollar figures are the API-equivalent cost the CLI reports, not money spent: they still stop runaway sessions and make runs comparable, but the binding constraint in `subscription` mode is the plan's rate limit (8.3). For the session of a stage the agent CLI's own caps are not relied on, with one exception: its budget cap is what stops a session for what subagents spend (BUD-10). Its turn cap covers one message and no subagent (BUD-6). The session of `sf sandbox exec` belongs to no stage and has the CLI's caps alone (CLI-6).

| Scope | Default *(tunable)* | Standing |
|---|---|---|
| Session: turns / wall clock | 200 / 90 min | Set by the operator for M1 (D-22) |
| Session spend: implement | $20 | Set by the operator for M1 (D-22) |
| Session spend: plan / review | $3 / $3 | Assumed (D-14); not enforced before M2 |
| Issue, cumulative across rounds and attempts | $40; each resume from a `budget` escalation grants a further $20 | Assumed (D-14); not enforced before M2 |
| Repository per day | $100 | Assumed (D-14); not enforced before M2 |
| Global per day | $150 | Assumed (D-14); not enforced before M2 |
| Factory eval runs | A separate budget; not counted against the daily limits | Assumed (D-14); not enforced before M2 |

Idle and tool-call time are the stall rules of 8.2, not budgets.

The three values of D-22 rest on a measurement of interactive sessions on the factory repository, not of factory sessions (`docs/research/15-session-sizes.md`). They keep the mark: the first measurement of what they limit is the five issues of M1b.

- **BUD-1.** Enforcement leaves 10% headroom below each limit.
- **BUD-2.** Reaching a session or issue limit stops the session and escalates with `budget`. Reaching a daily limit pauses dispatch until the next UTC day; running sessions finish their stage. Before escalation exists (M2), BUD-7 takes the place of the escalation, as ORCH-10 and ORCH-11 do for every other failure.
- **BUD-3.** Model id and effort are settings of the policy for each stage (`[models]`, 16.2), and the price table is a table of operator configuration (`[prices]`, 16.4), all loaded at runtime (D-42). Nothing about models or prices is compiled in.
  - **The table.** It holds one row for each model id, as `modelUsage` of a `result` names the model. A row holds five prices in dollars per million tokens: input, output, cache read, and cache write at a lifetime of five minutes and of one hour. One session held both kinds of cache write, the main loop's at one hour and a subagent's at five minutes (P-26). A row that lacks one of the five makes the file invalid. A model whose price depends on the length of the prompt, as the list has it for `claude-haiku-5-5` (`docs/research/15-session-sizes.md`), has no form in the table, and what its row holds is left open.
  - **Why not the policy.** A price is a fact about the provider and not about a repository. In a policy, a change under `.factory/**` could lower a price and with it loosen a limit, which a policy may not do (CFG-1).
  - **What reads it.** The supervisor's running figure between two results, and nothing else (BUD-4). The dollars recorded for a session are the agent CLI's own, so a wrong row moves the threshold of BUD-1 and no record. A call of the main loop is priced by the row of its own model, and by the row of the session's model where the table has none for it.
  - **A model without a row.** The host starts no session on a model that the table has no row for. Step 1 of the claim looks up the row for the model that the policy names for each stage that the build runs a session of, and the refusal names the model (8.2). In M1 that is the model of `implement` alone, so a policy that names a model for `review` needs no row for it before M2. `sf doctor` reports the same from M1a (CLI-1).
- **BUD-4.** Spend is recorded per model call (input, output, cache-read and cache-write tokens, and dollars), in the table `spend` from M1a on (ORCH-6), and rolled up per stage, run, issue and merged pull request. Cumulative spend appears in the status comment and the pull request body. Where usage comes from the agent CLI, the output tokens of a model call are those of its `message_delta` event, which `--include-partial-messages` adds to the stream (AG-1), and its input, cache-read and cache-write tokens are those of its `assistant` events, which match the call. The output count of an `assistant` event is a placeholder (probe P-22, observed in `subscription` mode). The CLI reports dollars only in `result`, per model and as a running total for the session. The dollars recorded for a session are those of its last `result`, never a sum over results; between results the supervisor's running figure is computed from the calls' token counts and the price table of BUD-3. A subagent's calls are recorded as BUD-10 says.
- **BUD-5.** The primary cost metric is dollars per merged pull request, counting failed and abandoned runs. Token volume and pull request count are not treated as progress.
- **BUD-6.** What a session limit counts. The supervisor counts every turn the session's stream shows, a subagent's included, from the session's start to its end, however many messages the session is given. It counts the main loop's turns as the model calls that BUD-4 records, and a subagent's as the message ids among the `assistant` lines that carry a `parent_tool_use_id` (BUD-10). Wall clock runs from the session's start to its end. Spend is in API-equivalent dollars as the agent CLI counts them, the one unit of spend in M1. The `implement` value is set against the list prices of the model the operator runs that stage on (D-22). The CLI's own caps (AG-14) count otherwise, as probe P-26 observed on a stream-JSON input. Its `--max-turns` counts the main loop's calls for one message: the count starts over with each message, a subagent's calls are not in it, and a subagent is neither held to it nor stopped when the main loop reaches it. So it is no second line for a session's turn limit, and the supervisor's count is the only one that covers a session of several messages or a subagent. The CLI's budget total runs on over the session and includes what subagents spend. Neither cap ends the process: after an `error_max_turns` result the CLI takes the next message with a fresh count, and after `error_max_budget_usd` it answers every later message with that result and no model call.
- **BUD-7.** Through M1 the control plane enforces the session limits and no other row of the table: the turns, the wall clock and the `implement` spend. The other rows bound rounds, escalations and unattended dispatch, which arrive with M2; spend is rolled up per issue from M1a all the same (BUD-4). A session limit that is reached is a Budget failure (8.6), the wall clock like the turns and the spend. `sf run` then interrupts the session, records which limit it was, reports it and exits with a failure. The report goes to the terminal, and from M1b into the status comment as well. Nothing of that session is captured or published. Run again on the same issue, `sf run` resumes from the journal (CLI-3) and restarts the stage with a fresh session and the whole of its session limits: in M1 the operator's command stands in for the resume and the budget grant of 7.5.1. A session stopped at a limit returns no result, and so no notes for `progress.md`: the host writes into the handoff which limit stopped it, as it writes the failure summary after an agent failure (8.6), so that the fresh session knows it continues work that was cut short. Such a restart is the operator's and does not count toward Poison (8.6); the journal records it. ORCH-10 gives the same handling to every other outcome that the full pipeline answers with a round or with a restart of the stage (D-44).
- **BUD-8.** Usage credits (13.5). A session whose `rate_limit_event` shows usage credits in use (`isUsingOverage: true`, or `rateLimitType: "overage"`) is stopped at once, as a session that reached a limit: its dollars are then money. The one state observed is that of an account with credits off, `overageStatus: "rejected"` (probe P-22). In M1 any event that shows something else, an event without that field included, makes `sf run` print a warning and nothing more: the run is attended, and a session can then cost little more than its spend limit (BUD-10). What the daemon does while credits are switched on is decided with M2 and may be stricter (D-14); the stop holds for it as well. Which fields show credits switched on, and what a session that draws on them emits, was not observed, and the operator does not switch credits on to observe it: the stop rests on the documented fields (probe P-26).
- **BUD-9.** M1 sets no limit in units of the plan. The control plane records, from the session's `UsageBatch` (ORCH-4), the window, the utilisation, the reset time and the credit state of every `rate_limit_event`, so that the share of the plan's window a run costs can be measured before such a limit is set. A session that meets the plan's limit in M1 ends as under BUD-7, with no pause and no restart by the factory: the pause of 8.3 is M2. `sf run` keeps the raw stream lines of the rejection, which no probe observed (P-22), and prints the reset time.
- **BUD-10.** Subagents (AG-20). Probe P-26 observed what the stream gives of them. A model call of a subagent leaves `assistant` lines that carry the `parent_tool_use_id` of the Agent tool call that started it, with one message id per call. The input, cache-read and cache-write counts on those lines are real. The output count is a placeholder, and `--include-partial-messages` adds no `message_delta` for a subagent's call, so what a subagent's calls put out cannot be added up from the stream. The `total_cost_usd` and `modelUsage` of `result` include a subagent's calls, each under the model it ran on, which need not be the session's. The CLI's budget cap counts them and its turn cap does not (BUD-6). Four rules follow.
  1. **Records.** The per-call records of BUD-4 cover the main loop. What a session's subagents spent is recorded as one remainder per model: the session's totals less its main-loop calls.
  2. **Turns.** Every call of a subagent counts as a turn (BUD-6). A foreground subagent's call that holds only text or thinking leaves no line unless the CLI is given `--forward-subagent-text`, so AG-1 gives it.
  3. **Foreground.** The host keeps subagents in the foreground: it sets `CLAUDE_CODE_DISABLE_BACKGROUND_TASKS=1` in the environment of an agent session (AG-11). Every subagent of the probe ran in the foreground under it, and the contract test asserts that one still does (19.4). Without it the CLI starts a subagent in the background unless the model asks otherwise. The main loop then returns a `result` before the subagent has finished, a second `result` follows with no message from the host, and a budget cap reached while only background subagents run can stop them with no `result` and an exit code of 0. The structured result of AG-6 and the dollars of BUD-4 are read from a session's last `result`, and neither reading holds then. A command that the Bash tool runs in the background gave two results in the same way, and the variable takes that option from the Bash tool as well.
  4. **The cap.** The supervisor's running figure lacks a subagent's output tokens, so it falls short while subagents run, and it is the CLI's own cap (AG-14) that stops a session for what they spend: at the limit and not at the threshold of BUD-1. The session then ends in an `error_max_budget_usd` result. In the probe that result's total was 1% to 15% above caps of a fraction of a cent. That is a floor: a subagent stopped at the cap can make one more call after the `result`, which the stream does not show, and in the two sessions where a later `result` told, the total stood 18% and 26% above the cap. So the dollars recorded for a session that the cap stopped during a subagent's work can fall short by a call.

---

## 15. Observability

### 15.1 Logs and traces

- `tracing` spans carry repository, issue, run id, attempt and stage. Logs are JSON lines on disk; a human-readable format on the terminal.
- OpenTelemetry export is optional and behind a feature flag: one span per stage and per model or tool call. GenAI attribute names are isolated in one module pinned to a semantic-conventions version, because those conventions are not yet stable.
- `TRACEPARENT` is set in each worker's environment.
- Run and issue ids go on spans and logs, never on metric labels.

### 15.2 Metrics

| Group | Metrics |
|---|---|
| Flow | Ready → pull request time; pull request → merge time; human review latency; pull request size |
| Yield | Merge rate; first-pass verify and CI rates; rounds per gate; escalation rate by category; abandonment |
| Quality | Rework and revert rate within 30 days; escaped defects (bug issues linked to a factory pull request); reviewer agreement with humans |
| Cost | Dollars per run and per merged pull request; cache-read share |
| Safety | Integrity flags and fails; capture rejections; egress denials; SEC-1 rejections; budget stops; hits of the literal-token scan (SEC-13) |

`sf metrics` prints them from the journal; no external metrics system is required.

### 15.3 Tripwires

Evaluated daily per repository. Any of these demotes the affected class to A2 (13.6) and opens a dashboard notice: 30-day revert-or-rework rate above 10%; an escaped defect in an auto-merged change; a red default-branch CI run after a factory merge (which also pauses merges, 12.3); any hit of the literal-token scan in a published commit; three budget stops in a day *(all tunable)*.

### 15.4 Feedback into the harness

Every escalation, reset, human "changes requested" and escaped defect is classified (`spec-gap`, `missing-context`, `missing-guardrail`, `agent-error`, `flake`, `infrastructure`). `sf metrics --failures` reports classes over time. For classes other than `agent-error`, the factory files a `state:triage` issue proposing the durable fix: a spec change, an `AGENTS.md` or docs change, a new lint or structural test, or a flake quarantine.

### 15.5 Dashboard issue

Each repository has one pinned issue, maintained by the factory and edited in place: active runs, queue, items waiting on a human with their age, yesterday's spend, breaker, mode and sandbox-level status. It is the only place the factory nudges.

---

## 16. Configuration

### 16.1 Layers

| Layer | Location | Trust | Holds |
|---|---|---|---|
| Operator | `~/.config/sf/config.toml` | Trusted | Credentials and their mode, data directory and disk limits, repository allowlist, classes and their autonomy (13.6), dispatch schedule, required CI checks and acknowledged workflows per repository, egress allowlist, global budgets and concurrency, the session and capture limits (section 14, 9.4), the price table (BUD-3), the sandbox level (13.3), agent CLI path, the toolchains directory (WS-7), the tools directory (WS-7a) |
| Repository policy | `.factory/policy.toml` and `.factory/prompts/*.md` at the default-branch head | Reviewed, but changeable by pull request | Stages enabled, verify and setup commands, the check tools and their versions (WS-7a), per-stage model and limits, round caps, size budget, extra protected paths and required checks, instruction directories, generated-file patterns, merge settings |
| Issue | Labels | Honoured only under SEC-1 | Tier, risk, priority, type, hold, restart |

The table says what each layer holds once every milestone is built. The keys that version 1 of each file has are those of 16.2 and 16.4 (CFG-5).

- **CFG-1.** Repository policy can only tighten operator settings: lower budgets and concurrency, lower autonomy, add protected paths and required checks, set `merge.update_when_behind = "always"`, and disable `plan` (for tier 0, and for tier 1 issues where the plan gate of 7.4 does not apply), `author_tests`, and `review` for tier 0. It cannot lower the sandbox level, extend the egress allowlist, shorten built-in protected paths or the required-check list, disable `capture`, `integrity`, `verify` or human review, or name credentials. Naming a check tool installs nothing: a version reaches a worker only if the operator installed it (WS-7a).
- **CFG-2.** Files are TOML, parsed with unknown fields rejected, and each has a `schema_version`. A JSON Schema is generated for editor support. That of the operator configuration of version 1 is `crates/factory-core/schema/operator-config-v1.json`, generated from the types of `factory-core` and held to them by a test of that crate. A build refuses a file whose `schema_version` it does not know. A key joins a version without a new number where every file that was valid stays valid and means what it meant: the key may be left out, and a build then does what it did before the key existed. Every other change takes a new number (D-42). An older build rejects a file that holds a key it does not know, so after a rollback (BOOT-3) the operator may have to take a key out again.
- **CFG-3.** A change to `.factory/**` is itself a protected-path change and is never auto-merged.
- **CFG-4.** No home directory is built into the code or into the systemd unit (AG-17). `~` in the table above is the home directory of the user the process runs as, wherever that is. The factory reads and writes no other path under a home directory unless the operator configuration names it.
- **CFG-5.** The key sets of version 1 (D-42). 16.4 holds every key of the operator configuration, and 16.2 every key of a policy. The operator configuration of version 1 holds what M0 and M1 read. What the specification gives the operator configuration beyond that has no key yet: classes and their autonomy, the dispatch schedule, disk limits, `min_free_bytes` of 8.3 among them, the budgets of section 14 other than the session limits, concurrency, acknowledged workflows, and a registry mirror (SEC-11). Each joins 16.4 in the change that specifies what reads it, by the rule of CFG-2. Naming them all now was not taken: each would be designed with nothing to read it, ahead of D-14 and D-15. A policy of version 1 likewise has no key for the budgets, the concurrency or the autonomy that CFG-1 lets a policy lower, the session limits among them, and none for the capture limits. The limits are keys of operator configuration alone, with the numbers of 9.4 and section 14 as their defaults, so that the operator tunes one without a new build. A key in both files, the policy's the lower, was not taken for version 1: M1a would build the comparison of two layers for numbers that no policy sets (BOOT-6). The keys join the policy when a second repository needs them, which is M2 (22). A number of this specification is a key only where 16.2 or 16.4 names it. Every other number is a constant of the build, a number marked *(tunable)* included. M0 builds the types of the whole operator configuration of version 1, since the operator writes one file and a key that a build does not know is an error (CFG-2); each key is read from the milestone that 16.4 names for it. The types of a policy arrive with its parser, in M1a (BOOT-6). The tested range of the agent CLI is no key: it is a constant of the build (AG-5, D-46).

### 16.2 Example policy

```toml
schema_version = 1
ecosystem = "rust"

[stages]
plan = "auto"            # auto = by tier and risk; false never removes the plan gate
author_tests = false
review = true

[models]
plan      = { id = "claude-opus-5-5",   effort = "high" }
implement = { id = "claude-opus-5-5",   effort = "medium" }
review    = { id = "claude-sonnet-5-5", effort = "high" }

[limits]
plan_rounds = 2
verify_rounds = 3
ci_rounds = 2
review_rounds = 2
max_changed_lines = 600
tool_call_timeout_secs = 1800

[setup]
commands = ["cargo fetch --locked"]
timeout_secs = 600

[verify]
commands = ["cargo fmt --all --check",
            "cargo clippy --workspace --all-targets --locked -- -D warnings",
            "cargo nextest run --workspace --locked"]
timeout_secs = 1800

[tools]                         # check tools, each at one exact version (WS-7a)
cargo-nextest = "0.9.105"

[ci]
timeout_secs = 3600
extra_required_checks = []      # the base list is operator configuration (VAL-8)

[merge]
strategy = "factory_serial"
update_when_behind = "on_conflict"

[instructions]
directories = ["/docs/**"]      # the default; a match admits a file that an instruction reference names (AG-2a, AG-2b)

[paths]                         # any depth, unless a pattern starts with "/" (VAL-5b); *, ** and ? (VAL-5c)
protected_extra = ["migrations/**"]
generated = ["**/*.snap"]
lockfiles = ["Cargo.lock"]
```

Model ids and tool versions are examples; they are configuration and will change. The example holds every key of a policy of version 1 (CFG-5). What the factory repository's own policy holds is in BOOT-6.

### 16.3 Reload

Policy is re-read when the default-branch head changes. An invalid policy is rejected: the last valid version stays in force for running stages, new dispatch in that repository pauses, and the dashboard shows the error. An unreadable path pattern is one of the things that make a policy invalid (VAL-5c). An `AGENTS.md` that the instruction set cannot start from is another (AG-2b). A run uses the policy commit recorded at claim for its whole life, except that a reset or a base update picks up the current one.

### 16.4 Operator configuration, version 1

Every key of the operator configuration (CFG-5, D-42). The operator writes the file by hand until `sf init` exists (CLI-7).

```toml
schema_version = 1
data_dir = "/srv/sf/data"                                # 9.1
tools_dir = "/srv/sf/tools"                              # WS-7a

[credentials]
mode = "subscription"                                    # 13.5
subscription_token_file = "/etc/sf/subscription-token"   # mode 0600

[github]                                                 # read from M1a
app_id = 123456                                          # 5.7
private_key_file = "/etc/sf/app-key.pem"                 # mode 0600

[sandbox]
level = "process"                                        # 13.3

[agent]
cli = "/opt/claude-code/claude"                          # 10.3

[ecosystem.rust]
toolchains_dir = "/srv/sf/rustup/toolchains"             # WS-7

[egress]
extra_hosts = []                                         # SEC-11

[limits.session]                                         # section 14; read from M1a
turns = 200
minutes = 90
implement_usd = 20.0

[limits.capture]                                         # 9.4; read from M1a
file_bytes = 5242880
candidate_bytes = 52428800
timeout_secs = 300

[prices."claude-opus-5-5"]                               # BUD-3; read from M1a
input = 4.00
output = 20.00
cache_read = 0.20
cache_write_5m = 5.00
cache_write_1h = 8.00

[[repository]]                                           # read from M1a
name = "sapientpants/sf-scratch"
required_checks = []                                     # VAL-8; read from M1b
```

The paths, the App id and the repository are examples. The limits are the defaults, and the prices are the list prices of that model as `docs/research/15-session-sizes.md` holds them, fetched on 2026-11-21. The table holds a row for the model of the one stage that M1 runs a session of (BUD-3). A key is read from M0 unless the example names a later milestone.

| Key | Holds | Where the file leaves it out |
|---|---|---|
| `schema_version` | `1` | The file is invalid |
| `data_dir` | The data directory (9.1). In M0 `sf sandbox exec` creates the directories of its instance below it (CLI-6) | The file is invalid |
| `tools_dir` | The tools directory (WS-7a) | No check tool is present |
| `[credentials] mode` | `"subscription"`. Version 1 has no other value: the API-key modes of 13.5 are not scheduled (22) | `"subscription"`, the default of 13.5 |
| `[credentials] subscription_token_file` | The file that holds the subscription token and nothing else, with mode 0600 (13.5) | No agent session starts |
| `[github] app_id`, `private_key_file` | The id of the GitHub App that this build authenticates as, and the file of its private key, with mode 0600 (5.7, 13.5). Both keys or neither. Nothing authenticates with them before M1a; `sf doctor` checks the mode of a file that the key names from M0 (CLI-1) | A command that needs GitHub refuses. The server holds no key before M1b (GH-11b) |
| `[sandbox] level` | `"process"` or `"hardened"`; SEC-7 says which command accepts which. `none` is no value of the file: a test sets it in code, with the fake agent (SEC-7) | The file is invalid |
| `[agent] cli` | The executable of the agent CLI (10.3) | No agent session starts |
| `[ecosystem.rust] toolchains_dir` | The toolchains directory (WS-7) | No toolchain is present |
| `[egress] extra_hosts` | Names that join the list of SEC-11 for every sandbox instance, each compared as SEC-10b says | No name is added |
| `[limits.session] turns`, `minutes`, `implement_usd` | The session limits of section 14: turns, minutes of wall clock, and dollars of an `implement` session | The defaults of section 14 |
| `[limits.capture] file_bytes`, `candidate_bytes`, `timeout_secs` | The capture limits of 9.4: bytes of a file, bytes of a candidate, and seconds of the staging step | The defaults of 9.4 |
| `[prices."<model id>"]` with `input`, `output`, `cache_read`, `cache_write_5m`, `cache_write_1h` | One row of the price table (BUD-3) | No session starts on that model |
| `[[repository]] name` | A repository that the factory may work on, as `owner/repo`. The entries are the repository allowlist | The factory works on no repository |
| `[[repository]] required_checks` | The required CI checks of that repository, each with `name` and `app_id` (VAL-8) | The list is empty |

- **CFG-6.** What makes an operator configuration invalid, beyond CFG-2 (D-42). Every path is absolute: the file expands neither `~` nor a variable, and a path that is not absolute makes the file invalid. A limit is a whole number greater than zero, `implement_usd` a number greater than zero, and a price a number that is not negative. A key that the table above marks so may not be left out, and `[github]` holds both of its keys or neither. A command that reads an invalid file does nothing else: it names the key and what is wrong with it. `sf doctor` reports the same (CLI-1).

---

## 17. Command line and control API

One binary, `sf`. Every command accepts `--json`.

| Group | Commands |
|---|---|
| Setup | `sf init` · `sf app create` · `sf repo add\|check\|remove <owner/repo>` · `sf doctor` · `sf sandbox exec` · `sf contract` |
| Operate | `sf daemon` · `sf run <owner/repo#n>` (same engine, one item, foreground, resumable) · `sf status` · `sf show <run\|issue>` · `sf logs <run> [--follow]` · `sf pause` · `sf resume` · `sf drain` · `sf cancel <run>` · `sf retry <run>` |
| Inspect | `sf cost [--since]` · `sf metrics [--failures]` · `sf policy check\|explain` · `sf issue lint <n>` · `sf class list\|promote\|demote` |
| Maintain | `sf gc` · `sf rebuild` · `sf export <run>` · `sf eval run\|report` · `sf self-update [--rollback]` |

- **CLI-1.** `sf doctor` reports on the installation: credentials, App permissions, CLI versions and flags, sandbox availability at the configured level, git version, the toolchain and the check tools for each repository (WS-7, WS-7a), enforcement mode and the capability probe. Each check arrives in the milestone that builds what it reads (D-39):

  | From | Checks |
  |---|---|
  | M0 | The operator configuration parses as version 1 (CFG-2, 16.4); each credential file that it names exists with mode 0600 (13.5); the `git` version (4.4); `bubblewrap` is installed in a version that 4.4 accepts and unprivileged user namespaces are enabled (4.4); a sandbox instance at the configured level starts and runs a command (13.3); the agent CLI is present, and which version it is |
  | M1a | The App's permissions against the table of 5.7 (GH-11b), with App authentication; the agent CLI's version against the tested range (AG-5) and its flags against the stub model endpoint (AG-1), with the driver; the toolchain and the check tools for each repository, and a row of the price table for the model of each stage that the build runs a session of (BUD-3), with the policy parser; the value of the subscription token is as long as the literal-token scan asks (SEC-13) |
  | M1b | The enforcement mode (4.1), with the status comment that shows it (DEP-1) |
  | M2 | The capability probe (4.3, GH-3a) |

  `sf doctor` takes no command of the operator's and answers no probe of 23.2. It has no `--probe`: 23.2 says how a probe is answered.
- **CLI-2.** The daemon serves HTTP/JSON on a Unix socket with mode 0600 in the data directory: `GET /v1/state`, `GET /v1/runs/{id}`, `GET /v1/runs/{id}/events` (stream), `POST /v1/refresh`, `POST /v1/pause|resume|drain`, `POST /v1/runs/{id}/cancel|retry`. There is no TCP listener by default. The API is a convenience: the daemon is correct without any client.
- **CLI-3.** `sf run` and `sf daemon` share all engine code and the store. `sf run` takes the store lock, so it cannot run beside a daemon or a second `sf run` on the same store; run again on the same issue, it resumes from the journal. Section 8.8 says what it does through M1: at its start (ORCH-8, ORCH-9), at a failure (ORCH-10, ORCH-11), and where it ends or waits (ORCH-12). It exits with 0 or with a failure as those items say. The failure status tells no two reports apart; `--json` gives the report as data.
- **CLI-4.** `sf cancel` is equivalent to a human applying `state:triage`. `sf retry` restarts the recorded stage with its stall and poison counters cleared. Both go through the control API (CLI-2) and arrive with it, in M2 (22). Through M1 the operator cancels a run by the label or by closing the issue (ORCH-9), and running `sf run` again is the retry (ORCH-8).
- **CLI-5.** `sf policy check` reads a policy as a reload does and reports what makes it invalid (16.3). For a valid policy it gives the two warnings of VAL-5c about its path patterns, each with the list and the pattern, and the warning of AG-2b for a comment that nothing closes, with the file. A warning does not make a policy invalid. It also prints the instruction set of the commit whose policy it reads, and the instruction references whose file was left out, each with the reason (AG-2b). That list is information: it is neither an error nor a warning.
- **CLI-6.** `sf sandbox exec` runs one command in one sandbox instance, outside any run (D-39). It is how the operator runs M0's exit check (BOOT-3) and, by hand, the entry point of step 2 of 20.1, and what it reports of a build is what the operator records for probe P-13. The operator starts it as the factory's user, the account that the control plane runs under (4.4). It has two forms.
  - **The command form**, `sf sandbox exec --dir <path> [--target <dir>] [--tool <name>=<version>]... -- <command>`, runs the command line as a setup or a verify command is run, with no model credential.
  - **The agent form**, `sf sandbox exec --dir <path> --model <id> --agent <prompt>`, runs one agent session that is given the prompt as its one message. The host prepares it as it prepares the session of a stage: the configuration directory of AG-4, the settings file of AG-8, the invocation of AG-1 without a result schema, and the environment of AG-11 for an agent session, the subscription token included (13.5). Three values of that invocation have no source here, since the command reads no policy and is no role. The session has the Bash tool and no other, which is all the exit check asks of it; AG-22 lists the tools of a role. The permission mode is `dontAsk`, the one that the sessions of probe P-6 ran with on the server. The settings file leaves the CLI's shell-tool timeout as it is, since `tool_call_timeout_secs` is a key of policy (AG-8). The session's caps are the CLI's own, `--max-turns 10` and `--max-budget-usd 1` *(tunable)*. The host ends the session at its first `result` line, by the stop sequence of AG-12: with stream-JSON input the CLI waits for a further message after a result (probe P-26).
  - **The instance.** Either form creates one instance at the configured level, the level that `sf doctor` checks (CLI-1). SEC-7 accepts `process` for this command as for an attended `sf run`, and never `none`. The instance has a proxy of its own (SEC-10) with the allowlist of SEC-11. The proxy admits connections while the command lives and not after it (AG-19). The filesystem policy is that of SEC-6. `--dir` names the directory that the instance shows read-write as the working directory, where a stage has its workspace or its export. The command gets a new, empty home, `CARGO_HOME` and target directory, as a verify execution does (9.1, 9.3). The host creates them below the data directory (16.4) and removes them when the command ends. `--target` names a target directory to use in place of the new one, and the host leaves that one as it is. The toolchain is the one that the `rust-toolchain.toml` in `--dir` names, resolved as WS-7 says, and each `--tool` names a check tool, found as WS-7a says. Neither is read from a commit or from a policy, since the command has neither: the host reads the file from the directory, as data. Where `--dir` holds no such file the instance is given no toolchain, as in the agent session of the exit check. Where the file cannot be resolved, or the toolchain, a component, a target or a named check tool is missing, the command creates no instance: it says what is missing and fails. The command is started and stopped as every sandboxed command is (AG-11, AG-12, AG-16).
  - **What it reports.** Both forms report the wall-clock time, the size of the target directory at the end, and the proxy's denied-request log. The command form also reports the exit status of the command line, and exits with it. The agent form reports the subtype of the `result` line that ended the stream, or that there was none, and how many uses of the Bash tool the stream holds. It exits with 0 only where that subtype is `success`. It does not report the exit status of the agent CLI, which the host stops.
  - **What it is not.** It is no run: it reads no issue, takes no store lock, journals nothing and writes nothing to GitHub. It is no gate either: what it reports is for the operator to read.
  - **What M0 builds for it.** The command joins the bubblewrap backend, the egress proxy and the supervisor shim, which the roadmap had as three items of M0 that nothing joined. For the agent form M0 builds the part of `factory-agent` that prepares a session: the files of AG-4 and AG-8, the argument list of AG-1 and the environment. Of the stream protocol M0 builds what this command needs and no more: it writes the one message, and it reads the lines as far as the `result` line and the uses of the Bash tool. The rest of the protocol, event normalisation, the limits and the structured result are the driver's and arrive in M1a (22). A flag of `sf doctor`, which the review of draft 4 proposed, was not taken, since `sf doctor` takes no command of the operator's (CLI-1).
- **CLI-7.** `sf init` writes the operator configuration (16.4) and takes the subscription token into its file (13.5). It arrives in M5, with the other commands that set the factory up, `sf repo add` and `sf app create` (22, D-42). Until then the operator writes both files by hand, the configuration from the example of 16.4, and `sf doctor` says what is wrong with it (CLI-1). One server is set up once, so nothing before M5 waits for the command. Dropping it was not taken.
- **CLI-8.** `sf contract` runs the contract test of 19.4 on the machine it is started on, against the agent CLI that the operator configuration names (D-46).
  - **Why a command.** The test has to pass where the sessions run, as the account that runs them. On the server that is the factory's user, which reads the subscription token (13.5). The check command is run there by the operator's own account, on a checkout (DEV-7), and a test started from that checkout would vouch for the sessions of another account. From M2 the test also runs on a schedule, by a daemon that holds a build and no checkout. A test of the repository, run through `cargo`, was not taken. The cost is that the cases ship in the build.
  - **Who starts it.** On the server the operator, as the factory's user, as CLI-6 says of `sf sandbox exec`. In the development VM a session, from a development build: the command reads nothing from GitHub and writes nothing to it, so BOOT-2 keeps no development build from it.
  - **What it runs.** The cases of the row "Contract" of 19.4. A case is one agent session or several, each in a sandbox instance of its own at the configured level, with a proxy of its own (SEC-10). Each session has the tools of a role, which its case names (AG-22). Through M1 the cases name the implementer alone, the one role that the build runs. The host prepares each as it prepares the session of a stage: the configuration directory of AG-4, the settings file of AG-8, the invocation of AG-1 and the environment of AG-11, the subscription token included. It creates the working directory of a case below the data directory (16.4), plants in it what the case names, and removes it when the case ends. `--model <id>` names the model, as it does for the agent form of `sf sandbox exec`. How many sessions a run of the command takes, and the caps of each, are left to the change that builds it; they count against the operator's plan (D-2).
  - **The version.** It runs on the version of the CLI that it finds, in the tested range or not, and reports that version. That is how a version is tried before it joins the range (AG-5).
  - **What it reports.** The CLI's version, and each case with whether it passed and, where it did not, what the stream or the instance showed in place of what the case asserts. It exits with 0 only where every case passed.
  - **What it is not.** It is no run: it reads no issue, takes no store lock, journals nothing and writes nothing to GitHub. The host records no pass. The tested range is a constant of the build (AG-5), and the gate of BOOT-3 is the operator's to keep. How the daemon learns of a pass, for its schedule and for the second layer of AG-3a, is left to M2.
  - **When.** Through M1 a person starts it: a session in the development VM, for a change to the driver, to the cases or to the tested range, and the operator on the server, as BOOT-3 says. The schedule arrives with M2 (22).
  - **Where the code lies.** The cases, each with what it plants and what it asserts, are data of `factory-agent`. `factory-daemon` runs them, since it is the crate that joins the sandbox, the proxy and the driver for a stage (19.1). M1a builds both, with the driver.

---

## 18. Local store and retention

### 18.1 Store

SQLite in WAL mode, accessed from one writer thread.

| Table | Content |
|---|---|
| `runs` | One row per run: repository, issue, stage, attempt, rounds, spend, deadlines, ids of branch, pull request and status comment; the snapshot, the candidate and the last pushed head (ORCH-6). Authoritative run state, with a `state_version`. |
| `events` | Append-only audit journal of engine events |
| `outbox` | Pending external writes with effect ids and retry state |
| `activities` | Stage executions: kind, sandbox record, pid, pgid, start time, model, usage, outcome, chain digest |
| `spend` | Per model call usage records |
| `etags` | Conditional-request cache for the gateway |
| `probes` | The results of the capability probe (4.3) per repository |

- ORCH-6 says which of these tables M1a creates and which arrive with M2.
- Every table that refers to a work item includes the repository id, so one daemon serves several repositories.
- Migrations are forward-only, hand-rolled on `PRAGMA user_version`. `sf self-update` drains, copies `store.db` with the SQLite backup API to `store.db.pre-<version>`, then migrates. Rollback reinstalls the previous binary, restores that file and reconciles (GitHub wins).
- Each release converts the previous `state_version` on load; an unknown version moves the run to `needs-human` with `infrastructure`.

### 18.2 Retention

| Data | Default *(tunable)* |
|---|---|
| Target directories, exports | Removed when the run enters `human_review` or a terminal state |
| Workspace and run directory | Removed 24 h after a terminal state |
| Agent event streams and transcripts | 30 days; 180 days for failed, reset or reverted runs |
| Journal rows | Kept; event detail pruned after 180 days |
| Remote branch | Deleted on merge; 14 days after a close without merge |

Transcripts are never uploaded to GitHub. `sf gc` applies retention; `sf export <run>` writes a run's directory and journal rows to an archive.

---

## 19. Rust implementation

### 19.1 Workspace

One virtual Cargo workspace: edition 2024, resolver 3, `rust-version = "1.100"`, `publish = false`. The toolchain is pinned in both `mise.toml` and `rust-toolchain.toml`.

| Crate | Responsibility | May depend on (normal dependencies) |
|---|---|---|
| `factory-core` | Domain types, effects and events, the pure engine, policy and config types, the `Ecosystem` interface with its implementations (9.5), issue parser, readiness lint, integrity rules, prompt assembly with the instruction set (AG-2b), sanitisation, the outbound encoder with the form of a path (GH-5a, GH-5c), the search of the literal-token scan (SEC-13) and the translation of a pattern into a `CODEOWNERS` entry with its comparison (VAL-3a) as pure functions, `Clock` and `IdGen` | (no I/O crates) |
| `factory-store` | `Store` trait, SQLite journal, outbox, migrations | core |
| `factory-proc` | Process groups, supervisor shim, stop sequence, line codec, timeouts | — |
| `factory-sandbox` | `Sandbox` traits, `SandboxPolicy`, backends; of the bubblewrap backend also the first process (SEC-10a) and the seccomp filter (SEC-6e) | proc, core |
| `factory-git` | `Git` trait, `git` CLI wrapper, mirrors, workspaces, capture, export, publishing | proc, sandbox, core |
| `factory-github` | `GitHub` trait, octocrab adapter, gateway, GraphQL documents | core |
| `factory-agent` | `AgentDriver` trait, Claude driver, event normalisation | proc, sandbox, core |
| `factory-net` | Egress proxy (SEC-10b), model gateway, the stub model endpoint of AG-1 | core |
| `factory-daemon` | Supervisor, scheduler, reconciler, effect executors (including setup and verify), control API | all of the above |
| `factory-cli` | The `sf` binary | daemon |
| `factory-testkit` | Fake agent binary, `fake-github`, fixtures | core, proc, store, git, agent, github |
| `xtask` | Developer automation | — |

Each port trait is defined in the crate that owns the seam. Core defines domain data and effects only; `factory-daemon` maps effects to port calls. A structural test over `cargo metadata` enforces these directions for normal dependencies; dev-dependencies may additionally include `factory-testkit`.

`factory-net` and `factory-sandbox` do not depend on each other, and the sandbox's way to the proxy needs neither to (D-40). `factory-net` opens the proxy's listener for a stage execution on a Unix socket (SEC-10b). `factory-daemon` puts the path of that socket into the `SandboxPolicy`, and the first process of `factory-sandbox` forwards to it (SEC-10a). `factory-cli` reaches the two hidden subcommands, `sf __supervise` of `factory-proc` (AG-16) and `sf __sandbox-init` of `factory-sandbox`, through `factory-daemon`, the one crate that its row names.

`factory-daemon` is the crate that looks at what the host has installed (D-42). It looks up on disk what `Ecosystem` returns for a toolchain (WS-7) and tests the directory of a check tool (WS-7a), and it puts the paths it found into the `SandboxPolicy`. `factory-sandbox` mounts those paths and reads no operator configuration, which is why it was not given the lookup. A crate of its own for it was not taken either: it would be a row of the table above for two functions. `factory-cli` reaches the lookup for `sf doctor` and `sf sandbox exec` through `factory-daemon`.

### 19.2 Stack

Versions are those current on 2026-11-18, unless a row gives another date. Pre-1.0 and fast-moving crates are pinned exactly: one exact version in the row, and the manifest that names the crate asks for that version and no other (`=x.y.z`). A row of such a crate that still gives a series, or no version, gets the exact version and its date in the change that first adds the crate to a manifest. M0 resolves the table as one set (22): a throwaway package that names every crate of it, at the exact version where the row has one and within the series where it has none, MUST resolve (`cargo generate-lockfile`). A row that gives no version at all leaves the crate at any version. Every crate means every one that a manifest of the workspace may name: the programs of the table, which no manifest names, are not in the set. That package is no part of the workspace, so it brings no pin forward. The set resolved on 2026-11-27, with cargo 1.100.0, and no row changed for it (`docs/research/20-stack-resolution.md`). A concern without a row is decided in the pull request that first needs it, and added to this table there.

| Concern | Choice | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| Async runtime | `tokio` 1.x, `tokio-util` 0.7.20, exact pin (`CancellationToken`, `TaskTracker`) | A cancellation token tree: root → run → activity. `tokio-util` 0.7.20 was current on 2026-11-27 |
| Processes | `process-wrap` 10, with its default features off but `tokio1`, `process-group` and `kill-on-drop`; the length-capped `LinesCodec` of `tokio-util` (AG-13); `nix` 0.31.4, exact pin, with its default features off but `fs`, `process` and `signal`, in `factory-proc` | Process groups and sessions. `process-wrap` brings in `nix` already, with `fs` and `signal` (`cargo tree`, 2026-11-27), so no crate joins the tree. `nix` 0.32.0 was current on 2026-11-27 and was not taken: `process-wrap` 10 asks for 0.31, and the tree would hold both. `nix` sends the signals of the stop sequence to a group (AG-12) and clears close-on-exec on the ends of the shim's pipes (AG-16) |
| Git | `git` CLI ≥ 2.47.3 (4.4) | `gix` read-only at most; no `git2` |
| GitHub | `octocrab` 0.54.x, exact pin, built-in retry disabled | Behind the port trait; hand-written GraphQL with serde types; a test asserts a failed POST is not re-sent by the client |
| HTTP | `reqwest`; `axum` 0.8 for the Unix-socket API and the gateway | |
| Egress proxy | `hyper` 1.x and `hyper-util` 0.1.21, exact pin, with HTTP/1 alone, for the one request head that the proxy reads (SEC-10b); `tokio` for its tunnels and for the forwarding of the first process (SEC-10a) | `axum` 0.8.9 and `reqwest` 0.13.5 resolved both already, at `hyper` 1.13.0 (`cargo tree`, 2026-11-26), so no crate joins the tree. No router of `axum`: a `CONNECT` has no path |
| Seccomp | `seccompiler` 0.5.0, exact pin, in `factory-sandbox` as a dependency for Linux targets alone, for the filter of SEC-6e | Current on 2026-11-26. Pure Rust; it depends on `libc` and on nothing else (`cargo tree`, 2026-11-26), and its licence is `Apache-2.0 OR BSD-3-Clause`. What its source says is in `docs/research/19-bubblewrap-backend-sources.md` |
| Store | `rusqlite` 0.43 (`bundled`) | One writer thread; migrations hand-rolled (`rusqlite_migration` currently requires an older `rusqlite`) |
| Config | `serde` 1, `toml` 1, `schemars` 1, in `factory-core`, `toml` with its default features off but `parse`, `serde` and `std` | Hand-rolled layering. `toml` also reads the manifests and lockfiles of the dependency rule (VAL-5g). Nothing writes TOML, so its `display` feature is off. The types of the operator configuration (16.4) are read from bytes with `toml`'s parsed table, whose spans name the key of an error (CFG-6), and `schemars` generates the JSON Schema of CFG-2 from the same types |
| Path patterns | A matcher of its own in `factory-core` (VAL-5c); `icu_casemap` and `icu_normalizer` 2.4.0, exact pins, for the fold of VAL-5d, `icu_normalizer` with its default features off but `compiled_data` | Current on 2026-11-21, with the tables of Unicode 18.0.0 compiled in. `reqwest` and `octocrab` bring in `icu_normalizer` already, through `url` and `idna` (P-29). Their data crates carry the licence `Unicode-3.0`, which `deny.toml` allows |
| Outbound text | The encoder of GH-5a and the form of a path of GH-5c, pure functions of `factory-core`; `icu_properties` 2.4.0, exact pin, for the escaped set of GH-5c and the characters that SEC-4 removes | A package that depends on `icu_casemap` and `icu_normalizer` 2.4.0 alone resolves `icu_properties` 2.4.0 already (`cargo tree`, 2026-11-21), so no crate joins the tree |
| Literal search | The substring search of `memchr` 2 (`memmem`), in `factory-core`, for the literal-token scan (SEC-13) | `Cargo.lock` holds 2.8.3 already, through `serde_json` (`cargo tree`, 2026-11-26), and `pulldown-cmark` brings the crate in too, so no crate joins the tree. It searches bytes for bytes and reads no pattern |
| Markdown | `pulldown-cmark` 0.13.6, exact pin, default features off, in `factory-core`, for the instruction references of AG-2b and, with its table extension on, for where the raw HTML of a text lies (SEC-4); and in `xtask`, with the same extension, for the check of local Markdown links (DEV-2) | Current on 2026-11-21. A pull parser that reads a string and opens nothing. Without its default features it brings in `bitflags`, `memchr` and `unicase` and nothing else (`cargo tree`, 2026-11-21); its licence is MIT. What it reports for a code span, a link and a comment is probe P-31, and what GitHub's page hides beside it is probe P-32 |
| CLI | `clap` 4 (derive) | |
| Errors | `thiserror` in libraries; `anyhow` in the binary only | Library errors carry a failure class: transient, permanent, needs-human, rate-limited with reset time |
| Observability | `tracing`, `tracing-subscriber`; `opentelemetry` 0.33 behind a feature | |
| Testing | `cargo-nextest` 0.9.152, `insta`, `wiremock`, `proptest`; failpoints behind a cargo feature | `cargo-nextest` is the latest that mise listed on 2026-11-27, and pinned in `mise.toml` (DEV-2) |
| Mutation testing | `cargo-mutants` 27.1.0, run by `cargo xtask mutants` (DEV-2a) | The latest that mise listed on 2026-11-27, the version that probe P-38 ran, and pinned in `mise.toml` (DEV-2) with mise's `cargo:` backend, which builds it from crates.io where `cargo-binstall` is not installed, as it is not in the VM or on the runners: its release on GitHub had no binary for Linux on arm64 on 2026-11-27, only for Linux and macOS on x86-64 and for Windows, which the development VM and the arm64 job of CI need |
| Coverage | `cargo-llvm-cov` 0.9.2, as `cargo llvm-cov nextest --workspace --locked`, with no floor (DEV-2); the component `llvm-tools-preview` in `rust-toolchain.toml` | The latest that mise listed on 2026-11-27, and pinned in `mise.toml` (DEV-2) with mise's `aqua:` backend, from the binaries of its release. It runs the tests with nextest, as the test step does |
| Supply chain | `cargo-deny` 0.22.1; `cargo-shear` 1.14.1 as the unused-dependency check; committed lockfile, `--locked` everywhere | Both the latest that mise listed on 2026-11-27, and pinned in `mise.toml` (DEV-2). `cargo-shear` parses the source, takes `--locked` and `--offline`, and reads its settings from the tables `metadata.cargo-shear` of the manifests. `cargo-machete` 0.10.1 was not taken: it searches the source for names, and its more accurate mode, `--with-metadata`, may change the lockfile, as its own output says |
