| Spelling | `typos` 1.52.2, run over every file of the work tree that git does not ignore, the dot directories in it but `.git`, with `typos.toml` as its one file (DEV-2b) | The latest that mise listed on 2026-11-27, the version that probe P-43 ran, and pinned in `mise.toml` (DEV-2). It is no cargo subcommand, so the version check of DEV-2 does not ask it. What it has to accept in the records of `docs/research/` is said in `typos.toml`, as a file type that names those records alone, and no record is rewritten for it. typos matches the glob of a type against the name of a file and not its path, so the type names the records by their file names |
| Cross-references | `cargo xtask references`, a task of `xtask` with no crate and no program of its own | The forms that it knows are those of DEV-2c. No regex crate is used: the forms are few, and the task reads them by hand |
| Markdown links | `cargo xtask links`, a task of `xtask` on `pulldown-cmark` (above), and no program of its own | It reads every `.md` file of the work tree but those under `.git` and `target` at the root, follows no link, and fails on a link or an image whose path, without its fragment and query and percent-decoded, does not exist or leaves the repository; a path that starts with `/` is read from the root, as GitHub reads it. A destination with a scheme, one that starts with `//` and a fragment alone are not local. `lychee` was not taken: it would be one more pinned tool with configuration files of its own to protect (BOOT-4), for a check that needs none of its network |
| Release | `cargo-dist` 0.34 with attestations; `cargo-auditable` | DEV-12 says what builds a release and what holds the workflow, DEV-13 how one is verified and installed |
| CI | `actions/checkout` 7.0.1, pinned to its commit SHA (VAL-6), and no other action; mise 2026.11.14, installed by the job from `mise.run` as in the development VM | The latest release of `actions/checkout` on 2026-11-27. DEV-5 and DEV-6 say what the workflow runs |
| Development VM | Lima 2.3.0 on the Mac, and no older than 2.0.0, which `lima.yaml` asks; mise 2026.11.14 in the VM, installed by its provisioning from `mise.run` | Current on 2026-11-27 and the versions that the operator's Mac holds. DEV-3 says what the VM holds and how the check uses it (D-53) |

Not used: any community Claude SDK crate, `git2` for mutations, `figment`, `sled`, `serde_yaml`, `rig-core` in the core, `globset` or any other glob crate (VAL-5c), a crate for regular expressions and any set of secret patterns (SEC-13), `libseccomp` or any other binding to a C library for the filter (SEC-6e), an MCP server (the agent needs no factory tools in v1; its result and follow-ups travel in the structured result).

### 19.3 Conventions

- Workspace lints deny `unwrap_used`, `expect_used`, `await_holding_lock`, and, through `disallowed_methods`, bare `tokio::spawn` outside the supervisor and `std::process::Command::new` and `tokio::process::Command::new` outside `factory-proc`. `xtask` is exempt. `unwrap_used` and `expect_used` do not bind in test code: `clippy.toml` sets `allow-unwrap-in-tests` and `allow-expect-in-tests`. With both, clippy 0.1.100 let an `unwrap` and an `expect` pass in a `#[test]` function, in a helper of a `#[cfg(test)]` module and in a helper of an integration-test file, and without them it refused each (a throwaway crate on the operator's Mac, 2026-11-26). They bind in all other code with the two settings as without, so the fakes and fixtures of `factory-testkit` are held to them as any crate's code is.
- A panic in a task fails that run and never the daemon, except in the store writer (ORCH-5).
- Shutdown on SIGINT or SIGTERM: stop dispatch, interrupt activities through the stop sequence, flush the journal, release the lock, within 30 s.

### 19.4 Testing

| Layer | What | Since |
|---|---|---|
| Engine | Property tests over event sequences for the invariants of 3.1: one active run per issue, round caps respected, no merge effect without a valid approval, a duplicated or re-delivered result event leaves state unchanged | M0 |
| Path patterns | The examples of VAL-5b to VAL-5e and the pairs of probe P-29 as tables. Property tests: an unanchored pattern without a wildcard matches a path exactly when the two fold to the same string or the folded path ends in a `/` and the folded pattern; an anchored pattern matches nothing that the same pattern without its `/` does not; canonically equivalent spellings of a path get the same answer from every pattern | M1a; M0 for the fold of VAL-5d |
| Dependency rule | The tables of VAL-5g and the cases of probe P-34 as tables: each manifest of the probe, and each lockfile with the one it was made from, with the outcome and the reason. Property tests: a file equal to its base draws no flag; a file written again with the same data, in another order of keys, with other spacing and comments, or with an entry as a string in place of a table that holds `version` alone, draws the outcome it drew before; a manifest in which nothing changed but version requirements, and a lockfile in which nothing changed but the `version` of packages, each with the `checksum` that goes with it, draw no flag | M1b |
| Binary and large files | The binary rule as a table (VAL-5i): a blob that is binary at the base alone, in the candidate alone and on both sides, added, changed and removed; a binary file that becomes a symbolic link and a link that becomes a binary file, each flagged, and a link whose target changes, which this rule does not flag. The groups of VAL-14c: a file at each of the two limits and one below, a diff at each of the two totals and one below, and a file that the size budget exempts | M1b |
| Instruction set | The examples of AG-2b as a table: each form that is an instruction reference and each that is none, a link in a file below the root, a name with `..`, a symbolic link, a file over each cap, a file that names `AGENTS.md`, a third level, a comment that nothing closes, and each case of `AGENTS.md` itself. The factory repository's own `AGENTS.md` and `GLOSSARY.md` as a fixture, with the set of BOOT-6 as the answer | M1a |
| Snapshot | The texts of probe P-32 as a table for the sanitisation of SEC-4, each with the tokens that are left and whether the warning is due; a title; a comment over its cap, a total that is reached and a body over its cap. Property tests: the text that is left holds no character of the removed set; it is the input less what was removed, in the same order; no size in a snapshot is over its cap; the comments of a snapshot are an unbroken run of the newest, less those over their own cap | M1a |
| Issue | The four bodies of probe P-4 as fixtures of the section parser (WI-1b), each with the criteria and the failing lines that WI-3 names for it, and the lines of the table of WI-3. Property tests: a section made of lines of the form with distinct ids is read back as those ids, methods and statements, in the order written; a line added to it that holds a character other than white space and is no criterion fails the section; neither answer depends on the line ending. For the claim, with `fake-github` returning each list in pages as probe P-8 saw GitHub return it: an issue whose only rename by another account, one whose last rename and one whose `labeled` event of `state:ready` lies on an older page of the events; the count of the comments left out where the walk reaches the oldest comment, where a comment on an older page does not fit, and where the issue has more comments than the pages of GH-21 hold; an edit history of 100 entries; an author, an editor and an actor that the response does not name; and each rule of WI-4, alone and with another, with every failed rule in what `sf run` reports; and a repository for which `fake-github` lists a rule for the name of the work branch, of a type of the table of GH-22, of a type that the build does not know, and on the second page of the list, with every entry in what `sf run` reports. In each case that ends the claim the store holds no run row and `fake-github` no write | M1a |
| Outbound text | The cases of probe P-15 as a table for the encoder of GH-5a, and the examples of GH-5c. Property tests for the form of a path, before the encoder: the written form holds no line break and no character of the escaped set, it holds a space only between double quotes, and the path is recovered from it byte for byte. The listing of the protected-path rule (VAL-3b): the example of that item, a path that two patterns match, a file of the instruction set, and a pattern that matched more paths than the cap. From M1b, the criteria of VAL-14b with an evidence text that holds a vertical bar, a line break, a link and a run of backticks: the table and the headings that the host wrote stand as they do without it | M1a |
| Recovery (simulation) | The real engine and the real SQLite store with scripted executors driven by a fault plan: crash before commit, after commit before the effect, after the effect before the acknowledgement, duplicate completion, reordered completion. Property: no duplicate external write, and convergence to the fault-free state. | M2 |
| Process crash | A daemon built with failpoints is killed with SIGKILL at each stage boundary and restarted, against `fake-github` | M2 |
| One foreground run (M1) | With the fake agent against `fake-github`, what ORCH-6 to ORCH-12 say of `sf run`. Built with failpoints, it is killed with SIGKILL during each stage and after each external write of `claim` and `publish`, and run again: the activity that it left is reaped and its row abandoned, the stage runs as a new stage execution, and `fake-github` holds no second ref, label change or pull request. Each outcome of ORCH-10 leaves the run at `implement` with its feedback and its counter, also where `sf run` is killed between two of its steps and run again, and the counter then moved once. Each case of ORCH-11 leaves the stage as it was, but for a claim that meets a ref it did not make, whose row is closed; in M1a neither item writes to GitHub. A create that `fake-github` refuses while the ref is absent, after a read that listed no rule, is reported with the entries of a second read (GH-22). A capture that was cut off after a return is made again, and no earlier candidate reaches a gate. Each row of ORCH-9, with a cancel and the claim that follows it, also with `state:ready` beside `state:triage`, and a head that is the candidate of the row is no foreign commit. From M1b: a red check, a review that requests changes, a merge and a test-merge that conflicts, each found by the `sf run` that ORCH-12 names, and a review that started a round starts no second one | M1a; M1b for the cases that the row gives it |
| Literal-token scan | The search of SEC-13 as a table: a value at the start, in the middle and at the end of the bytes, behind a NUL byte, twice, cut short by one byte, and in another letter case, which is no hit. Property test: the search reports a hit exactly where the bytes hold the value. With the fake agent against `fake-github`, and with a made-up value in the token file, never the operator's token: the fake agent leaves the value in a text file, in a binary file, in the target of a symbolic link, in a path and in the commit subject, each in a run of its own, with a value that is short enough for a subject (9.4). Each run stops at `integrity`, `fake-github` holds no push and no pull request, and the report names the place and holds the value nowhere. An issue whose outcome holds the value, and one whose title does, each stop their run at the write of the pull request, which `fake-github` does not receive; the candidate was pushed before it and holds no hit. A run that a hit stopped starts no stage when `sf run` is run again, also after the token file was given another value, and it is cancelled by the `sf run` that finds `state:triage` (ORCH-9). A token file whose value is too short starts no session. From M1b: the value in a text for the status comment and in one for the output of a check run | M1a; M1b for the surfaces that it builds |
| Drivers | A `fake-agent` binary that replays scripted stream-JSON and misbehaves on demand: hang, garbage, oversized line, loop, ignore SIGINT, a `setsid` grandchild holding stdout, the daemon killed mid-session. Recorded fixtures from the real CLI. | M1a |
| Git | Real `git` in temp directories with the hostile-tree suite of WS-9; the trees of probe P-40 as a table for WS-12 and its layouts as one for WS-13, each with its outcome where a round brings it in and where the base tree holds it, and a tree that a base update merged from two that each pass (12.4); a verify that starts again after its commands changed the export runs on the candidate's bytes, in a directory that holds nothing else (WS-14); the diff of a candidate that moves a protected file, a workflow and a symbolic link holds each old path as a deletion and each new one as an addition, also with `diff.renames` set in `host.git` (VAL-5a); the entries of VAL-5f as a table, one for each change to a symbolic link that probe P-30 lists and one for a link under a new name; a round after a base update never reverts upstream content; a base update where the default branch added a tracked file matching an ignore rule keeps that file; a candidate built after a gate failure on an unpublished update commit has the new default-branch head as an ancestor | M0 for the hostile-tree suite and the prototypes; M1a for the cases of capture, of verify and of the diff (WS-12 to WS-14, VAL-5a, VAL-5f); M2 for those of a base update |
| GitHub | `fake-github`: a stateful HTTP fake implementing the REST and GraphQL subset the gateway uses, hosted by the test process so it survives a killed daemon; pushes go to a local bare repository. `wiremock` for adapter edge cases including rate-limit and ETag paths. M1a builds the fake, in `factory-testkit`, for the calls that claim and publish make, and M1b gives it the calls that M1b adds: a call joins the adapter and the fake in one change (D-46). What the fake answers is what a probe of 23.2 saw GitHub answer, wherever one did. `wiremock` and the scratch repository alone through M1 were not taken: the rows "Issue" and "One foreground run (M1)" hold M1a's claim and its `sf run` against the fake | M1a for the calls of claim and publish; M1b for the calls that it adds |
| Sandbox | Per backend: read the host home, a credential file, or the arguments and environment of a host process; see or signal a host process; reach a non-allowlisted host; write outside the allowed paths; escape through a setuid binary or a mount; create a user namespace (SEC-6b); read `/etc/claude-code` (SEC-6d). For the bubblewrap backend also: the filter of SEC-6e as a snapshot, for each architecture every name with its condition and its error code; one call of each row of that table, which gets what the row says, the error code or, for a call of another architecture, the end of the process; a connection to the port of SEC-10a, which reaches a listener that stands in for a listed host and no other; a SIGTERM to the first process, which reaches a command that then ends by itself, and a SIGKILL, after which no process of the sandbox is left; and that `/tmp` is the directory of SEC-6c. On the Mac they run in the development VM, with the rest of the set (DEV-3). The tests at level `process` also run in CI; those at level `hardened` do not (DEV-7). They are no part of the plain test run: `cargo xtask check` runs them as two steps of their own, one for each level (DEV-2a), and no worker runs them (SEC-6b). | M0 at level `process`; M2 at level `hardened` |
| Egress proxy | The requests of SEC-10b as a table, against a listener on the loopback interface that stands in for a listed host: a `CONNECT` to a listed name on port 443, in either letter case, carries bytes both ways; a name that is not listed, a name of which a listed one is only the end, an IP address, another port, a plain request and a head that is too long are each refused with their reason, and each leaves one record in the denied-request log; a host that does not answer gets `502` and leaves none; a tunnel that is open when the activity ends is closed, and the socket accepts nothing afterwards (AG-19). They need no sandbox and are part of the plain test run | M0 |
| End to end | With the fake agent against `fake-github` on every pull request: through M1 that is the row "One foreground run (M1)", and from M2 the daemon. Against a scratch GitHub repository only in a scheduled workflow on the default branch, behind a protected environment, as the scratch App (GH-11a). That workflow is M2 work (D-46): through M1 every run against the scratch repository is attended and started from the development VM (DEV-4) | M1a, as the row "One foreground run (M1)"; M2 for the daemon and for the scheduled run |
| Contract | The cases that `sf contract` runs against the installed agent CLI, inside the sandbox at the configured level (CLI-8). They assert the flags and message shapes the driver relies on; that the tools of a session's `system/init` are those of its role and `StructuredOutput`, and no other, and that a subagent uses no tool that the session lacks (AG-22, P-41); the lock-down of AG-3a, with the cases of probe P-21 as its fixture: nothing planted in the working directory takes effect, whether it is there at the start, picked up from a subdirectory or written mid-session, and a test of the build holds that fixture to the built-in protected-path list, which matches every file that a case plants there (VAL-5h); that a worker cannot change the configuration of AG-4; that a session completes with only the model host of SEC-11 reachable and nothing in the proxy's denied-request log; and the stream shapes of probe P-26 that the limits rest on: that under the environment of AG-11 a subagent starts in the foreground and its message gets one `result`, that in a session that completes each call of a subagent leaves an `assistant` line with a `parent_tool_use_id`, and that `modelUsage` includes a subagent's calls; and that the CLI keeps the output of a subagent's task and its socket under `TMPDIR` (SEC-6c). M1a builds all of it with the driver, the lock-down included (D-46), and CLI-8 says who starts it and when | M1a |
| Encoder against GitHub | The encoder of GH-5a against GitHub itself (probe P-15). The test writes what the encoder makes of a mention, of each form of a reference that GH-5a lists and of a character reference into a comment on an issue of the scratch repository, as the scratch App, and reads the comment back as `bodyHTML`. It fails where that HTML holds a `user-mention` or an `issue-link`: in the probe the first went with a notification and the second with a cross-reference in every case. It needs the key of the scratch App, so it is no step of `cargo xtask check` and no hosted runner runs it on an event of VAL-6 (DEV-7). Through M1 a session starts it in the development VM, and from M2 the scheduled workflow of the row "End to end" runs it too. It writes to no other surface of GH-5a, and how it is started is left to the change that builds it | M1a, started in the development VM; M2 in the scheduled workflow |

The last column names the milestone whose change first builds a layer, and what a later milestone adds to it (D-46). Where BOOT-3 and 21.2 speak of the harness tests of this section, they mean the tests that `cargo xtask check` runs (DEV-7): the layers that exist at the commit in question, less what needs KVM, the Claude Code login or the key of the scratch App.

### 19.5 Checks and CI for the factory repository

- **DEV-1.** One command, `cargo xtask check`, runs every check of the factory repository. An agent session runs it before each commit, the pre-commit hook runs it again (DEV-9), and CI runs the same command, so the local set and the CI set cannot drift apart. The command was first written on 2026-11-21 (D-23), with formatting, clippy and `cargo test`, which stood in for the test runs of DEV-2. The run in the development VM (DEV-3) was added on 2026-11-27 (D-53), and the `cargo deny` step and the unused-dependency check on the same day, with the task that fetches (DEV-14). On the same day `cargo nextest run --workspace --locked`, `cargo test --doc --workspace --locked` and `cargo doc --workspace --no-deps --locked` with `RUSTDOCFLAGS` set to `-D warnings` took the place of `cargo test`. The first two are, word for word, verify commands of 11.2 (BOOT-6). `typos` and the check of local Markdown links joined on the same day (19.2), and so did the cross-reference check (DEV-2c), the mutation step (DEV-2a) and coverage, `cargo llvm-cov nextest --workspace --locked`. Every other check of DEV-2 is added to this command, and nowhere else, when it is built.
- **DEV-2.** The set: `cargo fmt --check`; `cargo clippy -D warnings`; `cargo nextest run --locked`; `cargo test --doc`; `cargo doc` with warnings denied, broken intra-doc links included; `cargo deny check`, in the form that DEV-2a gives; the unused-dependency check, `cargo shear --locked --offline` (19.2); the structural test; `typos` over code and documents; a cross-reference check that every requirement id and section number cited in `SPEC.md`, `GLOSSARY.md`, `AGENTS.md` and the code resolves; a check of local Markdown links; `cargo mutants --in-diff` on the changed code, failing on a surviving mutant; coverage with `cargo llvm-cov`, reported without a floor until there is code to measure. DEV-2a says more of three steps of this set: the sandbox tests, the mutation testing and `cargo deny`. DEV-2c names the forms of an id and of a section number that the cross-reference check knows. From the change that adds `.factory/policy.toml`, the structural test of 19.1 also checks that the policy's `[paths] protected_extra` lists every path of BOOT-4. That part of the test is written in the same change as the policy: before the file exists it would have nothing to read. That change is the onboarding pull request of BOOT-6, and it gives the test one more part: that every `[verify]` command of the policy is, word for word, a step that `cargo xtask check` runs. That `CODEOWNERS` passes the comparison of VAL-3a is checked from the same change on, by a test of `factory-core` and by no part of the structural test (BOOT-6). Each check tool of this set is pinned in `mise.toml` at one exact version, from the change that adds its check to the command, and interactive sessions and CI use that version. The pin alone does not decide which program runs: cargo looks for a subcommand in `$CARGO_HOME/bin` before `PATH`, and on the operator's Mac a `cargo-deny` 0.18.5 there ran in the place of the 0.22.1 that mise had installed, under `mise exec` too (observed on 2026-11-27). Before its steps run, in the VM and on the Mac alike (DEV-3), `cargo xtask check` therefore asks each check tool that they run as a cargo subcommand for its version, and fails by name where it is not the pin. The policy names the same check tools for a worker (WS-7a, BOOT-6), so the onboarding pull request gives the structural test a further part: that the policy's `[tools]` holds the check tools that `mise.toml` pins, which is every tool there but the toolchain, each at the same version, and no other. If commits become too slow, mutation testing moves to pre-push; no check is dropped.
- **DEV-2a.** Three steps of the set need more than their check tool (D-28, D-41).
  - **The sandbox steps.** The sandbox tests of 19.4 are two steps of their own, one for each level, and `cargo nextest run` runs none of them. How they are kept out of that run is left to the change that builds them. The step at level `hardened` arrives with its tests, in M2 (DEV-7).
  - **The mutation step.** The diff that `cargo mutants --in-diff` is given runs from a base commit to the work tree as it stands, new files included. The base is the commit that `SF_BASE_SHA` names where that variable is set (AG-11a), and otherwise the merge base of `HEAD` and `origin/main`. Where neither gives a commit the step fails by name; it is never left out. In CI on a push to `main` the diff is empty, and the step says that it had nothing to mutate: the change of every commit there was mutated on its pull request (DEV-8). The step is `cargo xtask mutants`, a task of `xtask` that finds the base, writes the diff, without rename detection, with a copy of the index to which every file of the work tree that git does not ignore is added, so that the repository's own index is left as it is, and runs `cargo mutants --in-diff` on it with `--test-tool=nextest`, `--cargo-arg=--locked` and its `mutants.out` in the build output, not in the work tree. `cargo mutants` runs without the `CARGO_TARGET_DIR` of the check, so each copy that it mutates builds in a build output of its own: with the variable the mutated builds wrote into the check's build output, and a later run of the check in the VM tested the binaries that they left there (observed on 2026-11-27). An empty diff starts no `cargo mutants`. The version check of DEV-2 asks `cargo mutants` for its version, and not the task. In the development VM the copy of the work tree holds no history (DEV-3), so on the Mac the command finds the base and writes the diff before it runs the set there, and gives the VM the diff and not the history: the file `xtask/mutants.diff` in the VM's build output, which the variable `XTASK_MUTANTS_DIFF` names to the task. The command sets that variable in the VM alone, and where it is set the task reads the diff it names and finds no base itself. Where the Mac finds no base the command fails by name before the VM runs.
  - **The `cargo deny` step.** The step is `cargo deny --locked --offline check`, in every place. `--offline` is what keeps `cargo deny` from the network. Version 0.22.1 no longer has the `--disable-fetch` that 0.18.5 had, and without `--offline` it clones the advisory database from `github.com`, or fetches into its clone, in every run, and fails where it cannot (P-38). The step reads the database where `cargo deny` looks by default, under `$CARGO_HOME/advisory-dbs`, and `deny.toml` names no `db-path`. Of the four checks of `cargo deny` the advisories alone read it, and the step stays one step. It fails where the database is missing, and where its last fetch is older than `maximum-db-staleness`, which `deny.toml` sets to 7 days *(tunable)*; the tool's own default is 90. With a failure of this step the command names the task that fetches (DEV-14). Inside the limit a commit needs no network. A fetch that fails would renew the time that `cargo deny` reads, so the task that fetches puts that time back (DEV-14).
- **DEV-2b. One configuration file for each tool (D-49).** The factory repository keeps the configuration of each tool that its checks rest on under one name, the toolchain's and that of mise as well as each check tool's (VAL-5j), and the structural test fails when the tree holds a file under another name that the tool reads unasked. The names are those that probe P-43 found. The test holds them, the plain names as a table and the rules for mise and for `.cargo/config` as code beside it.

  | Tool | Its one file | Fails the test |
  |---|---|---|
  | clippy | `clippy.toml` | `.clippy.toml` |
  | rustfmt | `rustfmt.toml` | `.rustfmt.toml` |
  | rustup | `rust-toolchain.toml` | `rust-toolchain` |
  | cargo | `.cargo/config.toml` | `.cargo/config` |
  | `cargo deny` | `deny.toml` | `.deny.toml`, `deny.exceptions.toml`, `.deny.exceptions.toml` |
  | typos | `typos.toml` | `_typos.toml`, `.typos.toml`, `pyproject.toml`; and `.ignore`, which is no configuration and takes files out of what typos reads |
  | mise | `mise.toml` | `.tool-versions`, `.miserc.toml`, `.miserc.local.toml`; every other name that begins with `mise.` or `.mise.` and ends in `.toml`; at the root the directories `mise/`, `.mise/` and `.config/mise/`, and the files `.config/mise.toml` and `.config/miserc.toml` |

  - A name of the third column fails at any depth, but for the directories and the two files that the table gives to the root. A name is compared without regard to the case of ASCII letters. `cargo nextest` and `cargo mutants` read one file each, `.config/nextest.toml` and `.cargo/mutants.toml`, so neither has a row. `cargo shear` reads its settings from the manifests, which the pattern `Cargo.toml` of BOOT-4 protects, and has no row either.
  - **A link in the place of a directory fails too.** An entry that is no directory and whose own path is `.config`, `mise`, `.mise` or `.config/mise` at the root, or `.cargo` at any depth, stands where a tool looks for a directory. Where it is a symbolic link the tool reads through it: cargo did (P-30). The test does not follow the link to see what lies behind it.
  - The test walks the files and the symbolic links of the tree as it lies in the working directory, without `.git` and `target` at the root, and follows no link. It reads no index of git, so a file that git does not track fails it as well: a `mise.local.toml` of one clone changes the tools of the check there. It needs no repository, so it runs wherever the tests of `xtask` run: in `cargo xtask check`, and in verify through the `cargo nextest run` of the policy's list (BOOT-6).
  - **Why a test and not an argument.** A flag on such a file is one line of a listing, on a repository where nearly every pull request carries a flag (BOOT-4). A test that fails is a red check. Passing each tool its configuration as an argument was not taken, since for clippy and for `cargo deny` it leaves a file of the tree in effect (VAL-5j).
  - A candidate can change the test, and that is a change under `xtask/**`, which is flagged.
  - The test does not look for a first name in a second place. An empty `clippy.toml` in the directory of a crate switches the root's rules off for that crate (P-43); the pattern `clippy.toml` flags it at any depth.
  - The part exists from the change that recorded the probe.
- **DEV-2c. The forms that the cross-reference check knows.** The check is `cargo xtask references`, a task of `xtask` (19.2). It reads `SPEC.md`, `GLOSSARY.md` and `AGENTS.md`, and every other file of the work tree that is no Markdown file as the code, where it can be read as UTF-8, without `.git` and `target` at the root, as the walk of DEV-2b does. The other Markdown files are not read: the records of `docs/research/` cite the drafts that they were written against, and are left as written. A failure names the file, the line and the citation.
  - **An id is defined** where it begins a line, or an item `- ` of a list, in bold and a full stop follows it, as in `**DEV-2.**` and `**VAL-5j. The names…**`, or where it is the first cell of a table row, as in `| D-21 |`.
  - **An id is cited** as a word of capitals, a hyphen, digits and at most one small letter, with no letter, digit, `_` or `-` on either side, whose capitals are those of an id that `SPEC.md` defines. `UTF-8`, `SHA-256` and the `AC-1` of WI-3 are therefore no citations, a new kind of id is known from its first definition on, and one whose number has a leading zero is a citation that fails.
  - **A section is defined** by a heading of two to four `#` that begins with its number: `## 19.`, `### 19.5`, `#### 7.5.1`.
  - **A section number is cited** as one to three parts of digits joined by full stops, none of which is 0 or begins with 0, with no letter, digit or `_` after it, where it stands after a `(` that begins the line or follows a space, after `, ` or `; `, or after one of the words *SPEC*, *section*, *sections*, *of*, *in*, *and*, *or*, *to*, *under*, *per*, *by*, *see*, *as*, *that*, *which*, *what*, *where* and *from* and a space, the case of the word aside, where no hyphen joins the word to the one before it. A number of one part is cited only after the first three words, as in "section 14"; one of three parts only where its first two parts number a section. The rest is read as a version, as in "git 2.47.3" and "macOS 27.0.1", and a text that cites a version after one of those words writes the name before it, as in "of git 2.42". A citation in another form, as at the start of a line, is not checked.
- **DEV-3.** On macOS, `cargo xtask check` runs the whole set of DEV-2 inside the development VM, on every run, as the command runs on Linux, so Linux-only code is checked whatever a change touches (D-41). Outside the VM it runs clippy and the tests a second time, for the macOS build, and nothing else: they keep the workspace compiling on macOS (4.4). The set cannot run outside the VM. The sandbox backends are compiled out of a macOS build, so a sandbox step has nothing to start. And `cargo mutants` 27.1.0 on the Mac reported every mutant of a function under `cfg(target_os = "linux")` as missed, which fails the mutation step for any change to Linux-only code (P-38). Each step has its run in the VM, so none is reported as not run (DEV-11). A VM that cannot be created or started fails the command by name. The VM holds the toolchain and the check tools that `mise.toml` pins, and it reaches the network as the Mac does. How it is shown the work tree, and where its build output lies, is said below (D-53). The VM is Lima running the latest Debian stable on the Mac's architecture, with `vmType: vz` and nested virtualisation, which gave such a VM a `/dev/kvm` on the operator's Mac, an M4 Max (P-36). Lima's documentation asks an Apple M3 or later and macOS 15 or later for it. The device belongs to group `kvm` and the user that Lima creates is no member, so the configuration adds that user to the group. In that VM the bubblewrap tests of P-16 passed with no setup step, on the `bubblewrap` version of the server, and the device answered `KVM_CREATE_VM` for a member of the group; no microVM was booted in it (P-36). The configuration is committed, and `cargo xtask` creates and starts the VM. It sets `safe.bareRepository = explicit` in the VM's system configuration of git (WS-15), so a test of 19.4 that runs git on a bare repository there names it, as the host does (WS-8). x86-64 coverage comes from CI.
  - **The configuration** is the file `lima.yaml` at the root of the repository, and the VM is the Lima instance `sf-dev`. It names Lima's template of the images of Debian 13 and not the template `debian`, which names the same images and also mounts the home directory of the Mac. Its provisioning installs `bubblewrap`, git, a C toolchain and mise, at the version of 19.2, and the command runs `mise install` in the work tree's copy before the set, so the VM holds what `mise.toml` pins at the commit that is checked. A VM that the command creates is started, stopped and started again: the provisioning adds the user to `kvm` after Lima's first login has begun, a login keeps the groups it began with, and Lima logs in anew after a restart (observed on 2026-11-27).
  - **A change to the configuration.** It reaches the VM only when the VM is created anew. The command keeps a copy of the `lima.yaml` that it created the VM from beside Lima's own files of the instance, and where the work tree's file differs from that copy it says so, with the command that deletes the VM, and runs the set in the VM as it is. Every work tree shares the one VM, so a failure there would stop the check on every other work tree once a branch that changes the file had the VM made anew. The command deletes no VM itself: the VM holds the login of DEV-4.
  - **The work tree.** Nothing of the Mac is mounted in the VM. On every run the command copies into it the files of the work tree that git tracks or neither tracks nor ignores, as they stand and with their times, and replaces the copy of the run before. The copy lies at `~/sf/tree` in the VM, below the path that the work tree has on the Mac, so that two work trees have two copies. It holds no `.git`: the mutation step, the one step that needs the history, is given a diff that the Mac wrote in its place (DEV-2a). A file that git ignores is not in the copy, and the structural test, which runs on the Mac with the tests too, finds it there (DEV-2b).
  - **The build output** lies on the VM's own disk, at `~/sf/target` below the same path, and stays from one run to the next. With the files' times kept, cargo builds again only what changed.
- **DEV-4.** Development builds of `sf` run in the VM (BOOT-2). It holds the operator's Claude Code login (D-2), which shares quota with the operator's interactive sessions, and no GitHub credential beyond the private key of the scratch App (GH-11a), which it reads from a file on the Mac (13.5).
- **DEV-5.** The CI workflow follows VAL-6, except that a network namespace takes the place of the container (DEV-6). It has one job per architecture, each running `cargo xtask check` (DEV-1) on a GitHub-hosted runner: `check (x86-64)` on `ubuntu-24.04` and `check (arm64)` on `ubuntu-24.04-arm` (D-20). The labels are pinned, and `ubuntu-latest` is never used, because the setup that bubblewrap needs differs between Ubuntu releases (P-16). The workflow has no path filter, so both checks are reported on every pull request. There is no macOS job. The repository has no self-hosted runner: one would run pull-request code on the operator's own machine. The workflow is `.github/workflows/ci.yml`, written on 2026-11-27, and its two jobs are the two entries of one matrix.
- **DEV-6.** Each job installs `bubblewrap`, lifts Ubuntu's AppArmor restriction on unprivileged user namespaces (`kernel.apparmor_restrict_unprivileged_userns=0`), and fetches the toolchain and the check tools, the dependencies and the advisory database with `cargo xtask fetch` (DEV-14), and as much of the history of `main` as the merge base of the mutation step needs (DEV-2a). It then runs the check in a network namespace that holds only a loopback interface. That is the build without network access of VAL-6, without its container: under Docker's default confinement bubblewrap cannot create its namespaces (P-16). The job enters the namespace as P-16 did, with `ip netns` and `setpriv` as the runner's user, and gives the check an environment that it names in full: the home, the user and the `PATH` of the runner's user and the homes of cargo and rustup, with `CARGO_TERM_COLOR` for the log, and no other variable of the runner. The checkout leaves no token in the clone, and the history that it fetches for the mutation step is the whole history. Before the check, a step of the job shows in its log that the namespace holds no interface but `lo` and that a command there reaches neither `github.com` nor `1.1.1.1`. Because the job has no network, a sandbox test of 19.4 uses a listener on the loopback interface as the host that must not be reached; against an internet host it would pass whatever the sandbox did.
- **DEV-7.** Where each check runs:

  | Checks | Hosted runners | The Mac, outside the VM | Development VM | Server | A worker's sandbox |
  |---|---|---|---|---|---|
  | The DEV-2 set, with every test of 19.4 that needs neither KVM, the Claude Code login nor the key of the scratch App, the sandbox tests at level `process` included | On every event of VAL-6, on both architectures | Clippy and the tests a second time, for the macOS build. They are no steps of their own: the set runs whole in the VM (DEV-3) | The whole set, on every run of the check on the Mac (DEV-3) | Run by the operator, under their own account, on a checkout of a tagged commit before that build is installed, from `v0.1.0` on (BOOT-3) | When the implementer runs the check (AG-21): every step but the sandbox step at level `process`, which is not run (SEC-6b, DEV-11). The `cargo deny` step reads the database that the host shows (VAL-1a) |
  | Sandbox tests at level `hardened` (from M2) | Never: not run (DEV-11) | Never: the step runs in the VM | With the rest of the set (DEV-3) | On a schedule | Never: not run (SEC-6b, DEV-11) |
  | Tests that need the operator's Claude Code login: the contract test of 19.4 and any real agent turn | Never: the workflow holds no secret (VAL-6) | Never | When a session runs them (DEV-4), the contract test from a development build (CLI-8) | The contract test: through M1 started by the operator, as BOOT-3 says; from M2 on a schedule (CLI-8) | Never |
  | The test of the encoder against GitHub (19.4), which needs the key of the scratch App | Never on an event of VAL-6. From M2 in the scheduled workflow of 19.4, behind its protected environment | Never | When a session runs it (DEV-4) | Never: the server holds no key of the scratch App (GH-11a) | Never |

  The x86-64 hosted runners have `/dev/kvm` and the arm64 runners do not, and GitHub does not support nested virtual machines on either (P-16). The workflow does not open the device to the runner's user, and a test that was not run is never reported as passed: the command reports its step as not run (DEV-11). Nothing that runs on the server reports a GitHub check. The scheduled end-to-end run against the scratch repository (19.4) is a workflow of its own and not a required check. On the server the operator runs the command with every step that it has through M1, from the first tag on (D-39), after the fetch task (DEV-14). It is the one place where the sandbox tests at level `process` meet the kernel and the `bubblewrap` of the host that real sessions run on: the server holds `bubblewrap` 0.12.0 and a hosted runner installs 0.9.0 (4.4, P-16). The operator's own account runs it and holds the toolchain and the check tools that `mise.toml` pins (4.4); the factory's user needs neither for it. That account cannot open `/dev/kvm` (4.4), so which account runs the step at level `hardened` on the server is left to the change that adds that step, in M2 (DEV-2a). Starting that run in M2, with the scheduled runs, was not taken: through M1 the exit check would then show that a sandbox runs a build on the server, and not that it holds there.
- **DEV-8.** The ruleset for `main`: a pull request is required, with zero approvals; the two checks of DEV-5 are required, with GitHub Actions as their source; force pushes and deletions are blocked; there is no bypass actor. The repository has had a ruleset named `main` since 2026-11-19, and it has held the two checks since 2026-11-27, when the operator added them with the workflow on `main` and both checks reported there by GitHub Actions. Read back through the API that day, it holds all of this. Approvals are zero because the operator writes every pull request merged in M0 and M1a and cannot approve their own (20.3). A change that renames a job changes the ruleset with it. This ruleset is weaker than the one of 20.3, and under it alone `sf` would detect `factory-enforced` (4.1). No build of `sf` runs on the factory repository before M1b (BOOT-6). In M1b, once the factory reports its check runs (5.5), the operator creates the ruleset of 20.3 beside it (step 5 of 20.1), and this one stays as it is (D-41). The two checks of DEV-5 are the repository's `required_checks` (VAL-8). The bypass of 20.3 would take both checks from a pull request that the operator wrote, and sessions write such pull requests through M1 (BOOT-1). It does not reach the rules of a second ruleset (DEP-5). With both rulesets a factory pull request needs what 20.3 asks, and a pull request of the operator's needs the two checks. The mode detection accepts the pair (DEP-5). The cost is one that this ruleset has from its first day: a pull request on which a check does not turn green does not merge, whoever wrote it, and a fault of GitHub Actions stops every merge. Replacing it with the ruleset of 20.3 was not taken.
- **DEV-9.** The pre-commit hook is the file `.githooks/pre-commit`, committed in the factory repository and named by `core.hooksPath`; the repository uses no hook manager (D-23). The hook runs `cargo xtask check` (DEV-1) and names no check itself. Before that it unsets the variables through which git hands a hook the repository and the index of the commit being made, the list of `git rev-parse --local-env-vars`, so that a test of 19.4 that runs git in a directory of its own works on its own repository (P-27). `core.hooksPath` is a setting of the clone and is not committed. A `SessionStart` hook in `.claude/settings.json` sets it to `.githooks` at the start of every Claude Code session on the repository, and a clone used in any other way is given it by hand. The value is relative, so it also holds in a linked worktree. The hook checks the work tree as it stands, which is the commit when every change is staged; CI checks the commit itself. There is no pre-push hook. If mutation testing moves there (DEV-2), it is a second file in `.githooks/` that runs a task of `cargo xtask`. The hooks cover the interactive sessions of D-16 only: the control plane runs no hook from a workspace (INV-2), a worker reads no project settings (AG-3a), and nothing on a clone stops a human from skipping a hook. What holds for every commit that reaches `main` is the ruleset of DEV-8, which names no bypass actor and stays when the ruleset of 20.3 arrives (D-41).
- **DEV-10.** A Claude Code session on the factory repository is refused a command that makes git skip its hooks (D-23). `.claude/settings.json` holds a `PreToolUse` hook on the Bash tool, and its command hands the event to `cargo xtask pre-tool-use`. The task reads the command line as written, splits it into its commands and their words with the shell's quoting undone, and takes each word `git`, with or without a path, as the start of a git command. It refuses, with exit code 2 and a reason, a `git commit` on which `--no-verify` is in force, written out, abbreviated or as `-n`; a `git push` on which it is in force; and a git command given its own `core.hooksPath` through `-c` or `--config-env`. Which spellings git accepts, and which words are the value of another option, is as probe P-27 observed. The push is refused although there is no pre-push hook, so that adding one (DEV-9) changes nothing here. The hook's command has two properties. It starts the task only for an event that holds the letters `git`, so that most calls neither wait for a build nor are refused while the task does not build; the `if` field of Claude Code is not used for this, because it let wrapped git commands through. And it turns every exit code of the task but 0 into 2, because Claude Code lets a call go on after any other failure of a hook; the task likewise refuses an event in which it finds no command. The first property rests on what P-27 observed. The second rests on Claude Code's documentation of hooks, which was read for the probe and which the probe did not exercise. A deny rule was not chosen: it is a pattern over the text of the command and cannot tell `-nm` from `-m "naming"`. The refusal guards against a habit, within the limits DEV-9 states for the hooks. The task expands nothing and runs nothing. It does not see a flag or a `git` that comes out of a variable, a substitution, an alias or a function; a command in a string or a file handed to an interpreter, or in the text of a here-document; a `git` whose letters quoting breaks up; the `GIT_CONFIG_*` variables, an included configuration file or a change to the clone's `core.hooksPath`. It refuses the rare command that has the words `git commit -n` as arguments of another program. A session of another coding agent, or one started with the project's settings switched off, has no such hook and only the rule in `AGENTS.md`. The refusal was observed in a session in its default permission mode, and whether it holds where permissions are bypassed was not tried (P-27).
- **DEV-11.** What `cargo xtask check` does where a step cannot run (D-28). A step has three outcomes: passed, failed and not run. The command has one set of steps in every place (DEV-1), and no shorter form for a worker. A step is not run only where the table of DEV-7 says that it does not run in that place, which it says of the sandbox steps alone (DEV-2a), and of no step on the Mac, where the set runs in the development VM (DEV-3). The command then names the step and the reason, runs the other steps, and names every step that was not run once more in its last line. It exits with 0 when no step failed, so what was not run is told apart from what passed in the text that the command prints and not in its exit code. In every other place a step that cannot run fails by name, as a step that cannot be started does now. A hosted runner that has lost its user namespaces therefore does not pass. The command knows a worker's sandbox by the variable `SF_SANDBOX` with the value `process` or `hardened` (AG-11a), and by nothing that it tries: an attempt to create a user namespace cannot tell a worker's sandbox from such a runner. How it knows a hosted runner, for the step at level `hardened`, is left to the change that adds that step (DEV-2a). The third outcome is built with the first step that needs it.
- **DEV-12.** The release build (D-39). A workflow of its own builds a release when a tag `v*` is pushed, and on no other event. `cargo-dist` generates it (19.2), and it is edited by hand where the tool has no setting for a rule of this item, as for the environment below. How the edited file is kept from being generated over is left to the change that adds it.
  - **What it builds.** `sf` for `x86_64-unknown-linux-gnu` alone, the architecture of the one host (4.4), with `cargo-auditable`, on the runner label `ubuntu-24.04` that DEV-5 pins. The development VM has the Mac's architecture (DEV-3) and runs development builds (DEV-4), which are no release, and CI checks arm64 on every pull request (DEV-5). An arm64 target is added when a host needs it. The operator's Mac does not build a release: the attestation is signed with the identity of the workflow, and a build there would have none.
  - **What it publishes.** The archive of the build and the file with its SHA-256, as a GitHub Release of the factory repository, with an artifact attestation for the archive.
  - **Permissions.** The top-level permissions are `contents: read` and every action is pinned to a full commit SHA, as VAL-6 has it for the CI workflow. `contents: write`, `id-token: write` and `attestations: write` are held by the jobs that run in the environment `release`, and by no other. The workflow uses no cache (VAL-7).
  - **The environment.** `release` is the protected environment of VAL-7, and the operator creates it. It admits tags `v*` alone and names the operator as its required reviewer, so nothing is published that the operator did not approve after the tag was pushed.
  - **The tags.** A ruleset restricts the creation, the update and the deletion of tags `v*`, with the repository-admin role as its only bypass actor. The operator creates it with the workflow, since `sf` holds no credential that can write a ruleset (20.1). The factory's App holds Contents: write (5.7) and is on no bypass list (GH-11), so from M1b on GitHub refuses it such a tag. Without the ruleset the App could start a release run, and the environment alone would stand between that run and a release.
  - **What nobody observed.** That GitHub does each of these on a public repository of a personal account is probe P-35, answered on the scratch repository before the workflow is merged.
  - **Protected paths.** The workflow lies under `.github/**`, which the built-in list of 11.3 protects. BOOT-4 says when the configuration of `cargo-dist` joins its list.
- **DEV-13.** How a release is verified and installed, through M1 (D-39).
  - **Verified on the Mac.** The operator downloads the archive there and verifies its attestation with `gh attestation verify`, against the factory repository, with the signing workflow held to the release workflow (`--signer-workflow`), the source ref held to the tag (`--source-ref`) and self-hosted runners refused (`--deny-self-hosted-runners`). The Mac holds the operator's `gh` login.
  - **Installed on the server.** The operator copies the archive to the server over SSH and unpacks it there as root (D-16), into `/opt/sf/<version>/`, where `<version>` is the tag without its `v`. The binary is `/opt/sf/<version>/sf`. The link `/opt/sf/current` names the version in use, and the operator switches it.
  - **Whose directory.** `/opt/sf` belongs to root. The factory's user reads it and writes nothing in it, so a control plane that was subverted cannot replace its own binary. It lies under no home directory (CFG-4).
  - **Rollback.** An earlier version stays beside the new one. A rollback points the link back and restores the store backup (18.1). Through M1 the operator copies the store by hand before switching the link: `sf self-update`, which makes that copy, arrives in M2, and M0 has no store.
  - **What the server needs.** Neither `gh` nor a GitHub credential. Verifying on the server was not taken: the manual of `gh` does not say whether the command works without a login for a public repository, and its offline form needs the attestation and a trusted root that another machine fetched first.
  - **Later.** `sf self-update` (M2) says how it verifies when it is built.
- **DEV-14.** `cargo xtask fetch` fetches what the check reads from the network, and `cargo xtask check` fetches nothing (D-41). The task runs `cargo fetch --locked` and `cargo deny fetch db`. The second clones the advisory database with the `git` of the machine, or fetches into the clone, under `$CARGO_HOME/advisory-dbs` (P-38). `cargo deny` reads the time of the last fetch from the file `FETCH_HEAD` of that clone, and a fetch that fails leaves the file empty, with the time of the attempt: the next check then passed on a database that was too old. The task therefore notes the time of the file before it fetches. Where the fetch fails it sets the time back, or removes the file where there was none, and exits with the failure. With the time put back, the check failed again (P-38).
  - **On a hosted runner** the job runs the task before it enters the network namespace (DEV-6). A task that fails there fails the job.
  - **In a Claude Code session** a `SessionStart` hook of `.claude/settings.json` runs it at the start of every session, as one sets `core.hooksPath` (DEV-9). The hook starts the task in the background, with its output in `target/xtask-fetch.log`, so that the session does not wait for the VM, and exits with 0. A task that fails there stops no session, and the age of the database stays what it was. On macOS the task fetches the dependencies twice, outside the development VM for the clippy and the tests that run there and inside it for the set, and the database inside it, where the step reads (DEV-3). The start of a session on the Mac therefore starts the VM. In the VM the task runs on a copy of the work tree of its own, at `~/sf/fetch/tree` with its build output at `~/sf/fetch/target`, each below the path of the work tree on the Mac as the check's are (DEV-3), so that a check started while the fetch runs keeps its copy.
  - **In a clone used in any other way**, and on the server before the check of DEV-7, whoever runs the check runs the task first.
  - **In a worker's sandbox** nobody runs it: a worker reaches no address that holds the database (SEC-11). The host shows it a clone of its own (VAL-1a), and its dependencies are those of the setup command (WS-4).

  A check that finds the database missing or too old fails at the `cargo deny` step (DEV-2a) and is run again after the task. A check command that fetches before its steps was not taken: it needs a second form for the places without a network, which DEV-1 rules out, and a commit without a network would fail.

---

## 20. Onboarding a repository

### 20.1 `sf repo add`

1. Check that the App is installed; read the capability probe and enforcement mode.
2. Produce a readiness report. **Blocking:** a single-command setup and test entry point that passes on the default branch under the sandbox; an `AGENTS.md` that the instruction set can start from (AG-2b); a `rust-toolchain.toml` that the host can resolve, with its toolchain, components and targets installed (WS-7), and every check tool that the policy of step 3 will name (WS-7a); no submodules, no layout of a git repository at the root of the tree (WS-13), no `filter=` attributes and a clean round-trip checkout at the default-branch head; no branch named `factory`, which would make every work branch impossible to create (8.2); no rule that GitHub lists for the name of a work branch (GH-22); no Cargo git dependency outside the egress list; `cargo deny`, if used in verify, passes without fetching against the host-fetched advisory database (VAL-1a); the workflow audit (VAL-6a). **Advisory:** check speed, flake rate, documentation layout, architecture rules, the repository's auto-close setting, other installed Apps that can write pull requests, past approving reviews by bot accounts, every symbolic link of the tree at the default-branch head with its target, since a change to the file that a link points at is flagged only where a pattern protects that file (VAL-5f), a tree at the default-branch head that holds a `Cargo.toml` and no `Cargo.lock`, since no diff of such a repository shows a package that no manifest names (VAL-5g), every pair of paths of that tree that have the same fold, since a checkout on a file system that is not case-sensitive holds one of the two (WS-12), and every directory below the root of that tree that has the layout of a git repository, since git reads its configuration when it is started there and no run changes a path below it (WS-13), and that classic branch protection rules are not read, so that the operator looks at them by hand (GH-22).
3. Open one onboarding pull request containing `.factory/policy.toml`, default prompts, the issue forms and the `CODEOWNERS` entries for the protected paths (VAL-3a). It holds no `AGENTS.md`: a repository without one is not onboarded (step 2), since a skeleton would make the policy valid while every session ran on next to no instructions (D-34). That policy is valid by 16.3, and the body of the pull request lists the warnings of VAL-5c for its path patterns and that of AG-2b for a comment that nothing closes, and the instruction set with the instruction references whose file was left out (AG-2b). The CI workflow template is not pushed by the App (GH-11): `sf repo add` writes it to a local file and prints the commit command, and the operator adds it to the onboarding branch with their own credentials.
4. Do nothing else in that repository until the pull request is merged and a workflow satisfying VAL-6a exists on the default branch. Closing the pull request opts the repository out.
5. After the merge: create the label set through the API, read it back and compare it with 5.2 (GH-3a), and write the workflow's check names into `required_checks`. Only then is the ruleset of 20.3 activated, by the operator: the App has no Administration permission (GH-11) and `sf` holds no credential that can write rulesets. `sf repo add` prints the ruleset as importable JSON; `sf repo check` then re-detects the mode (4.1) and verifies the bypass list of each contributing ruleset (DEP-4, DEP-5).

Until M5 the same steps are done by hand from this checklist, the entry point of step 2 with `sf sandbox exec` (CLI-6), the rules read of step 2 with the operator's own token (GH-22), and the App that step 1 looks for is created by hand as well (GH-11b). The factory repository's own onboarding is BOOT-6.

### 20.2 Modes after onboarding

A repository advances by operator command: `observe` (A0: readiness lint and plans only) → `propose` (A2) → named A3 classes.

### 20.3 Recommended ruleset

For the default branch, in `enforced` mode: require a pull request; one approval; dismiss stale approvals on new commits; require approval of the most recent push by someone other than the pusher; require code-owner review; require the status checks `factory/integrity`, `factory/verify`, `factory/review` (source: the factory App) and the CI checks of VAL-8; do not require branches to be up to date; block force pushes and deletions. Signed commits are not required in v1 (5.6). The ruleset targets the default branch alone, and so reaches no work branch (GH-22).

Required checks apply to every pull request, and the factory checks exist only on factory pull requests. The repository-admin role is therefore a bypass actor in pull-request-only mode: without it the operator could not merge a pull request they wrote themselves, since an author cannot approve their own pull request. Human pull requests are merged through that bypass and are outside the factory's guarantee. The factory App is never a bypass actor. The mode detection of 4.1 accepts exactly this bypass configuration, and beside it a ruleset with no bypass actor (DEP-5).

The bypass covers the CI checks too: under this ruleset alone a pull request that the operator wrote merges with no check green (P-38). An operator who wants such pull requests held to CI keeps a second ruleset for the default branch, with the CI checks and no bypass actor, which the bypass does not reach (DEP-5). The second ruleset is an option and no part of this recommendation (D-41). The factory repository has it (DEV-8).

### 20.4 Repository conventions the factory expects

- A root `AGENTS.md` of about 100 lines that maps into `docs/`; details live in versioned documents, not in the instruction file. A document that every session should be given is named there in a code span or a link, and a pattern of policy admits it (AG-2b). It is written for the sessions that work on the repository interactively, and no part of it has to be written for a worker (AG-21).
- Fast, deterministic checks runnable with one command.
- Architecture rules enforced mechanically (crate boundaries, lints with remediation text in their messages).

---

## 21. Bootstrapping and evaluating the factory

### 21.1 Self-hosting

- **BOOT-1.** Humans (with interactive agent assistance) build M0 and M1 (D-16). Claude Code sessions on the operator's Mac write the code, one issue, branch and pull request at a time, and the operator reviews and merges every pull request. That work is tracked as issues on the factory repository, grouped under one GitHub milestone per roadmap milestone; the operator never makes them `state:ready`, so the factory never claims them. BOOT-1a says what such an issue is and whose. M1a runs on the scratch repository (BOOT-2). From M1b the factory is run attended on its own repository from an installed, tagged build, once that repository is onboarded (BOOT-6); from M2 unattended.
- **BOOT-1a. The issues of a milestone's build (D-52).**
  - **What they are cut from.** The row of the milestone in 22 and the items that it cites. An issue cites the ids of what it builds and restates no rule. This specification holds no list of the issues: the tracker does.
  - **What one issue is.** A slice that ends in something that can be run: a step of `cargo xtask check` (DEV-2), a layer of 19.4 or a command of `sf`. A crate of 19.1 is no issue. Each issue is one branch and one pull request (BOOT-1).
  - **Whose it is.** An issue that a session takes carries the label `ready-for-agent`. A step that the operator alone can take is an issue of its own, with `ready-for-human`: every step on the server (D-16), a ruleset or an environment of the repository (DEV-8, DEV-12), and a probe that needs the operator's own login. A session takes no step of such an issue. It writes what the operator runs, and where the step yields something to record it writes the pull request from what the operator brings back; that pull request closes the issue. The two labels are those of `docs/agents/triage-labels.md`. Neither is a label of 5.2, so no issue of the build is a work item (`GLOSSARY.md`).
  - **What blocks what.** An issue that waits for another names it through GitHub's issue dependencies, so the tracker shows which can be taken. The issues have no parent issue.
  - **When a milestone is cut.** When the one before it has met its exit criterion (22): M0 with D-52, M1a at M0's exit and M1b at M1a's. What a milestone builds changes how the next is cut. An issue of a later milestone that needs nothing of an earlier one is opened when it can be taken, under the GitHub milestone of its own.
  - **A probe that the build answers.** The issue whose work observes the answer records it in its own pull request, with the change that 23.2 asks of this specification.
  - **A change to this specification.** One that the build finds is made in the pull request of the issue that found it, with a row in 23.1 where it is a decision. No map of decisions is opened again for it.
- **BOOT-2.** The binary that operates on the factory repository is always an installed, pinned release, never a build of the tree it is changing. A development build runs only against the scratch repository (`sapientpants/sf-scratch`), never against the factory repository. The factory version is recorded in every marker.
- **BOOT-3.** From M2, upgrading the operating binary is a human action (`sf self-update`), allowed only after the eval gate passes on the candidate: the smoke eval until M3, the eval suite afterwards. The first tag, `v0.1.0`, is cut when the rest of M0 passes in CI and in the development VM; the operator installs it on the server to run M0's exit check (22). During M1 the operator installs a newer tagged build by hand; the gate is the harness tests of 19.4 passing on the tagged commit, in CI and on the server (DEV-7). From the first build that holds the Claude driver there is a second gate (D-46): the operator runs `sf contract` on the server from the installed build, as the factory's user, and starts no agent session with that build but the test's own before the test has passed there (CLI-8). Previous binaries and store backups are kept for rollback (18.1). A build is released as DEV-12 says and verified and installed as DEV-13 says.

  M0's exit check is these steps, in this order (D-39). Every step on the server is the operator's (D-16).
  1. The operator pushes the tag `v0.1.0` and approves the job of the release workflow that waits for it. The workflow publishes the archive with its attestation (DEV-12).
  2. On the server, under the operator's own account, `cargo xtask check` passes on a checkout of the tag (DEV-7). It comes before the install, so that no build is installed on a host where the sandbox tests fail.
  3. The operator verifies the attestation on the Mac and installs the build on the server (DEV-13).
  4. As the factory's user, `sf doctor` passes, with the checks that CLI-1 gives M0.
  5. The cold build. As the factory's user: `sf sandbox exec --dir <clone> --target <directory> -- cargo build --workspace --all-targets --locked` (CLI-6). The clone is one of the factory repository at the tag, which the operator made from GitHub without a credential and which the factory's user can write, and the target directory is new and empty. The registry is reached through the proxy and nothing else is reached (SEC-11). The step passes when the command exits with 0 and the proxy's denied-request log is empty. The time and the size that the command reports are the cold half of probe P-13.
  6. The seeded build. The same command in a second clone at the tag, with `--target` naming the directory that step 5 left. It passes as step 5 does, and what it reports is the other half of P-13.
  7. The agent session. `sf sandbox exec --dir <directory> --model <id> --agent <prompt>`, in an empty directory, with a prompt that asks for one shell command to be run and its output reported. It passes when `sf sandbox exec` exits with 0, which says that the stream ended in a `result` line of subtype `success`, when its report shows at least one use of the Bash tool, and when the denied-request log is empty.

  The verify commands of BOOT-6 were not taken for step 5: they need `cargo-nextest` in the tools directory, which the server holds from the onboarding on (4.4). Step 7 is a session and not one turn, which is one model call (`GLOSSARY.md`): a session that calls a tool makes at least two. The Bash tool had run under `--disable-userns` with a stand-in only (P-6), and never under the factory's own sandbox with its seccomp filter.
- **BOOT-4.** The factory's own gate code, prompts, policy and this specification are protected paths in its repository and are never auto-merged. The built-in list of 11.3 names the prompts and the policy (`.factory/**`), the workflows and the settings that wire the session hooks (`.github/**`, `.claude/**`), and the configuration of clippy, rustfmt, `cargo nextest`, `cargo deny` and the toolchain (`clippy.toml`, `deny.toml`, `rust-toolchain.toml`, `.cargo/**` and the other names of VAL-5j). It does not name the paths below, so the factory repository's policy MUST list each of them in `[paths] protected_extra` (16.2):

  | Paths | What they hold |
  |---|---|
  | `SPEC.md`, `GLOSSARY.md`, `docs/research/**` | The specification (D-18), its terms (D-21) and the research records behind it |
  | `crates/**` | Every crate of 19.1 except `xtask`: the code of `sf` and its tests |
  | `xtask/**` | The check command (DEV-1), the tasks the hooks of DEV-9 and DEV-10 run, and the structural test (19.1) |
  | `.githooks/**` | The pre-commit hook (DEV-9) |
  | `Cargo.toml`, `mise.toml` | The workspace lints of 19.3, the second pin of the toolchain (19.1) and the pins of the check tools (DEV-2) |
  | `docs/agents/**` | The documents `AGENTS.md` sends an interactive session to: how a session that holds the operator's `gh` login works the issue tracker and its labels |
  | `lima.yaml` | The configuration of the development VM, in which the check set runs on the Mac (DEV-3) |
  | `/.*`, `/.*/**` | Every dot file and every dot directory at the root: `.config/**`, where `cargo nextest`, `cargo deny` and mise look; `.mise.toml`, `.miserc.toml` and `.tool-versions`; and whatever a later tool reads there |
  | `mise.*.toml`, `mise/**`, `typos.toml`, `_typos.toml`, `pyproject.toml`, `.ignore` | The names of mise and of typos that no dot at the root covers (P-43) |

  The second to the fifth row are the gate code, and the list as far as that row was decided as D-24. The sixth row was added by D-26, the seventh by D-53, and the last two by D-49. The policy that carries the list is written at the repository's onboarding, and this MUST binds from that pull request on (BOOT-6); until then this table is the list.
  - `crates/**` is listed whole, not as named crates or modules. Nearly every crate of 19.1 holds part of a gate or of a control. The three that hold least are `factory-store`, `factory-cli` and `factory-testkit`, and they hold the journal a flag lives in (VAL-3), the binary, and the fakes the tests of 19.4 trust. A list of modules cannot be written before the code exists, and a file that is renamed or added falls outside such a list without anyone noticing.
  - A change to the code of `sf` under `crates/**` changes no gate of the factory until the operator installs a release built from it (BOOT-2, BOOT-3). The checks of the factory repository act sooner. The workflow runs on the pull request (VAL-6, DEV-5), so a change to a test under `crates/**`, or to anything under `xtask/**`, changes what CI checks on the pull request that makes it. A change to `.githooks/**` runs at the next commit on a clone that has it (DEV-9).
  - `.githooks/**` is in this repository's policy and not in the list of 11.3. The directory's name is this repository's choice (DEV-9), and another repository's `core.hooksPath` can name any directory.
  - No pattern of the list is anchored but the two for the dot files of the root (VAL-5b, D-25). A match here only adds a flag, and under `crates/**` nearly every pull request carries one already. One consequence waits for D-19: `SPEC.md` also matches every `spec.md`, so in the layout of 6.3 it would flag `docs/specs/<capability>/spec.md` inside a `type:spec-delta` issue too, where the built-in list does not. The pattern is looked at again when D-19 is decided.
  - The pattern `Cargo.toml` has to stay unanchored: it matches the manifest of every crate (VAL-5b). `Cargo.lock` is not listed: the dependency rule of 11.3 already flags a new dependency and a changed source (VAL-5g), and a dependency bump that changes `Cargo.lock` alone stays open to a class (13.6). A bump that also changes a manifest is flagged, and every bump of an exactly pinned crate (19.2) is one.
  - `docs/agents/**` is listed because the repository's policy declares no instruction directory that holds it (BOOT-6). Under the default of AG-2a it would have been protected as one. A worker that changed these files would change what a session with a GitHub credential is told to do. One of them holds the rule that no triage label is mapped onto `state:ready`.
  - A file that configures a check of DEV-2, the development VM (DEV-3) or the release build (19.2) joins the list in the change that adds the file.
  - **The last two rows are for the files that a tool reads beside its own** (D-49). mise read nineteen names in a directory, eight of which outrank `mise.toml`, and a `.miserc.toml` there put another file in the place of `mise.toml`. typos read five names and every `.ignore` of the tree (P-43). The rule above does not reach such a file: whoever adds one to change a check does not add it to the list.
  - The two dot patterns are anchored to the root, where the tools look for these files when the check command runs. Below the root, `crates/**` and `xtask/**` cover what a tool reads from the directory of a crate. `.ignore` is unanchored, since typos read one in any directory.
  - In the listing of a flag each of these patterns stands near the top of the summary, as one that matched few paths (VAL-3b). A file under a second name also fails the structural test (DEV-2b).
  - Every file at the root, `/*`, was not taken. It would protect `Cargo.lock`, which D-24 left out so that a dependency bump stays open to a class.
  - The structural test checks that the policy lists every path of this item, from the change that adds the policy (DEV-2).
  - Every pull request in which the factory changes code on its own repository therefore carries a flag, and a head with a flag does not merge without review (13.6). No class of work that changes code can merge at A3 there, and the factory repository does not reach lights-out while this list stands.
- **BOOT-5.** From M2 the factory also runs on a second, unrelated repository, so defects that self-hosting would hide become visible.
- **BOOT-6.** The factory repository is onboarded by hand, from the checklist of 20.1, at the start of M1b (D-26). It is one issue and one pull request (BOOT-1): a Claude Code session writes the onboarding pull request and the operator merges it. The policy is not written before that, and no early cut of `sf repo add` writes it (M5). No build of `sf` runs on this repository before M1b, so nothing would read the file, and until M1a has built the code that parses a policy nothing can tell whether it is valid (CFG-2). The one real issue of M1a runs on the scratch repository. For that run the scratch repository is given a policy of its own, the first that `sf` reads, an `AGENTS.md`, without which that policy is invalid (AG-2b), and the labels of 5.2 (GH-3a), and the development VM that the build runs in (DEV-4) holds the check tools that policy names (WS-7a); preparing it is work of M1a and not of this item. Of step 5 of 20.1, the labels are created and read back once the pull request is merged, on the operator's instruction (D-21), and the ruleset of 20.3 is created beside that of DEV-8 when DEV-8 says, which is later in M1b (D-41). Before step 1 the operator creates the factory's App and installs it on this repository (GH-11b). The entry point of step 2 is run from the installed build with `sf sandbox exec` (CLI-6), in a clone at the default-branch head: the setup command and the verify commands of the table below as one command line, since each call is a new instance with an empty `CARGO_HOME` (WS-4), and with `--tool` for each check tool that those commands run.

  The pull request holds what step 3 of 20.1 names. Its `CODEOWNERS` entries are the file `.github/CODEOWNERS`, since the repository has no such file (VAL-3a). The session reads the errors endpoint of VAL-3a for the head of the pull request, and the pull request is not merged while that endpoint lists an error (D-37). With `.factory/policy.toml` it adds the parts of the structural test that read the policy (DEV-2), and two tests of `factory-core` that read files of the repository itself (19.1). One parses the policy with the policy types of that crate. The other makes the comparison of VAL-3a between `CODEOWNERS` and the three lists, of which the policy holds two and the crate the third. The policy holds every setting that the pipeline of M1b reads, and nothing for a stage that does not exist yet:

  | Key | Value | Why |
  |---|---|---|
  | `schema_version`, `ecosystem` | `1`, `"rust"` | CFG-2, 9.5 |
  | `[models] implement` | `claude-opus-5-5`, effort `medium` | The model that the $20 of D-22 is priced for (BUD-6). A dearer model here is also a change to that limit |
  | `[setup] commands` | `cargo fetch --locked` | WS-4 |
  | `[verify] commands` | The four commands of 11.2: formatting, clippy, nextest and the doc tests | Below |
  | `[tools]` | Every check tool that `cargo xtask check` runs, at the version `mise.toml` pins | Below |
  | `[merge] strategy` | `"human"` | The strategy of M1b (22) |
  | `[instructions] directories` | `["/GLOSSARY.md"]` | Below |
  | `[paths] protected_extra` | The patterns of BOOT-4 | BOOT-4 |
  | `[paths] lockfiles` | `["/Cargo.lock"]` | Anchored, because a match exempts a file from the size budget (VAL-5b) |

  - **What it leaves out.** It has no `[limits]`: the round caps and the size budget keep their defaults. The session limits of D-22 are keys of operator configuration, and a policy of version 1 has no key for them (CFG-5). It has no `[stages]`: `plan` and `review` arrive in M2 and `author_tests` in M3, and each key is added in the change that turns its stage on. It adds no required check, since the two of DEV-5 are operator configuration (VAL-8), and it names no `generated` pattern. Each later key is a change under `.factory/**`, made in a pull request that the operator merges (CFG-3).
  - **Verify.** The commands are listed in the policy, and `cargo xtask check` is not the verify command. The policy is read from the default branch (INV-10), so a candidate cannot change the list. `cargo xtask check` is defined by the candidate's own `xtask`, and CI already runs it that way (DEV-5), so as the verify command it would add no second definition of the gate (G3). Parts of it also have no place on an export: `cargo mutants --in-diff` has no diff there, since an export holds no repository (AG-11a), and the sandbox tests of 19.4 do not run in a worker's sandbox (SEC-6b). The `cargo nextest run` of the list runs none of those tests (DEV-2a). Verify therefore does not test the sandbox on this repository, and CI does, at level `process` (DEV-7). The structural test holds the list to the steps of `cargo xtask check` (DEV-2), so the list holds no check that the repository does not run. The cost is that a failure of a check outside the list, `typos` or `cargo deny` for example, shows first in CI, as a CI round and not a verify round (7.3), unless the implementer ran that check itself.
  - **Tools.** The policy names every check tool that `cargo xtask check` runs and not `cargo-nextest` alone, which is all the verify list needs (D-27).
