Naming every key of 16.1 now was not taken. The price table is a table of operator configuration, one row for each model id with five prices. The policy was not taken, and neither was a first milestone without a table. The capture limits and the session limits are keys of operator configuration alone, and a policy of version 1 lowers none. A key in both files was not taken, and neither were constants of the build. `sf init` arrives in M5. M2 was not taken, and neither was dropping the command. Not asked of the operator, of the toolchain: that the file is `rust-toolchain.toml` at the root alone and that a tree without it names no toolchain; which keys the host reads, that `profile` is tested for nothing, and that any other key or table makes the file one that the host cannot resolve; the form of a component's and a target's name; that the host triple is the GNU triple of the architecture that `sf` was built for; that a component is found under its name or its name with `-preview`, with the triple or without, and not through the renames of the channel manifest; that `rust-std-<host triple>` is asked for with the two programs; that the sandbox shows one toolchain's directory and not the toolchains directory; that a file the host cannot resolve counts as a missing toolchain, and that the host says what is wrong with it; that the host follows a symbolic link in the toolchains directory; that every function of `Ecosystem` is pure, and not that of the toolchain alone; that M0 builds the function and the lookup; and the words that the `factory-core` row of 19.1 gains, with no change to a dependency. Not asked, of the configuration: the name of every key and table but `[ecosystem.rust] toolchains_dir`, and that the tools directory is the key `tools_dir` where the draft that the operator saw had `[tools] dir`, a table name that the policy uses for another thing; which key may be left out and what then holds, and from which milestone each is read; that `none` is no value of `[sandbox] level`; that `subscription` is the one value of `[credentials] mode`, and the default; that `[github]` may be absent and that the installation is no key; that a path is absolute and nothing is expanded, what a limit and a price may be, and that a command which reads an invalid file does nothing else (CFG-6); that a build refuses a `schema_version` it does not know, and that an older build rejects a newer key; that a number is a key only where 16.2 or 16.4 names it; that M0 builds the types of the whole of version 1; that `sf sandbox exec` creates its directories below the data directory, under `sandbox-exec/`; that `sf sandbox exec` creates no instance where a toolchain or a check tool is missing; that step 1 of the claim looks up the price row of each stage that the build runs a session of, and that `sf doctor` reports a missing one; that a call on a model without a row is priced by the session's row; that a registry mirror has no key; the two terms that `GLOSSARY.md` gains; and the ids CFG-5, CFG-6, CLI-7 and P-39. D-46 has since decided that the tested range is no key (AG-5). |
| D-43 | What capture does with two paths that have one fold and with the layout of a repository under another name, what an export belongs to, and whether a diff of the host detects a rename (WS-12 to WS-15, VAL-5a). The review of draft 4 found four points that the text left open. The hostile-tree suite of WS-9 lists case collisions, and 9.4 had no rule for them (finding E3). A directory with the layout of a bare repository passed capture and the integrity check without a flag (S2). The export was named by the candidate's SHA, and nothing said that a verify which starts again gets a new one (S5). VAL-5a did not switch rename detection off (S6). Probe P-7 had left the first two open. What git does in each of the four was observed first, as probe P-40, on the operator's Mac and in a Debian container on it. The operator then answered six questions, each as recommended. The host rejects two paths that have the same fold, unless the tree the run started from holds both. A rule over the whole tree, with a claim and an onboarding that refuse a repository which holds such a pair, was not taken, and neither was admitting what git admits. The host rejects every path that a run changes below a directory with the layout of a repository, by git's test for one as it was observed: `HEAD` with `objects` and `refs`, or `HEAD` with `commondir`. A `flag` in 11.3 was not taken, and neither was admitting the layout. The operator sets `safe.bareRepository = explicit` for their own account on the Mac and on the server, and the development VM has it in its system configuration. Nothing checks the setting: a step of `cargo xtask check` that tests it was not taken, and neither was leaving it out of this specification. An export belongs to one stage execution, lies in `exec/<seq>/export/` and stays until 9.3 removes it. Removing it with its stage execution was not taken, and neither was the name by SHA with a rule that the host deletes the directory before each use. Rename detection and copy detection are off in every diff the host computes. A second form of diff with detection, for the size budget and the reviewer's input, was not taken, so a file that moved counts in the size budget as a deletion and an addition. M0 builds the fold of VAL-5d, which the two rules of capture read, and M1a the matcher over it. A prototype that folds the letters A to Z alone until M1a was not taken. Three points differ from an answer, and each came out of the review of this change. The first answer named the previous tree as what a pair is measured against, and WS-12 names the base tree: after a base update that conflicted, the previous tree holds what the merge put together. The second answer spoke of the paths of a round and of a layout in the new tree. WS-13 reads the paths in which a tree differs from the base tree, and a layout in either, so that a run cannot take a layout of the base tree apart and write its `config`. And that answer had every layout of a base tree listed as an advisory item, where one at the root is a blocking item and stops the claim: no run could change a path of such a repository. Not asked of the operator: that the host makes the two tests on the tree of every test-merge and of every base update, and that a rejection there is handled as a conflict (12.3, 12.4); that WS-12 compares whole paths, of entries of every kind, and not the names inside one directory; that a pair is admitted by its two paths in the base tree, whatever the run did to either; that a name which is not valid UTF-8 is folded as VAL-5d folds a path, at the cost of a rejection too many; that the layout is read from names alone, and neither from the content of `HEAD` nor from a mode; that the entries of two directories count as those of one where their paths have the same fold; that the root of a tree counts as any directory does; that a path which a run removes below a layout is rejected like one it adds or changes, and that a run may remove a layout whole and no part of it; that a file outside its directory which a layout of the base tree reads its configuration from is not covered, and that `protected_extra` is the operator's means for it; that the host reads the paths in which two trees differ from the trees, and not from the listing of the staging git; that onboarding lists the pairs and the layouts of a default branch as advisory items; that the feedback of a rejection names both paths of a pair; that the walk of a tree lies in `factory-git`; that the setting is an item of its own, WS-15, and that the provisioning of the server in 4.4 names it; the sentence that INV-11 gains; that a stage which starts again is a new stage execution with a new `<seq>`; that `verify` and `review` never share an export; that every git command of the host names its repository (WS-8); that the option against renames is given although `git diff-tree` detected none without it; the cases that WS-9 gains for the suite of M0, and the tests of 19.4; that the case of 19.4 for a file that moved stands in the Git row and not, as the review proposed, in the row of the path patterns, since it is a case of the diff; that a link under a new name is a row of VAL-5f; the row that the threat table of 13.1 gains and the item of 23.3; the term that `GLOSSARY.md` gains; and the ids WS-12 to WS-15 and P-40. P-40 ran git 2.56.1 on macOS and git 2.47.3 in a Debian container on the same machine. Nothing ran in the factory's sandbox or on the server, and the trees with two paths of one fold were made with `git mktree`. Two reviewers read the change, one against the rules for editing this specification and one against the ticket, the answers and the kept output, and what they found is in the text: the second found the base update and the merge of a branch that is behind, the file added to one of two directories that the base tree holds, the layout taken apart, the layout at the root of a base tree, and what a `config` reads from outside its directory, and that the record said of every layout what the script had run on some, after which the script ran again. Left open: what a program does that reads a repository without the `git` CLI, with the setting or without it; a directory that becomes a layout when something creates an entry that git does not track; a merge that is made between two test-merges, whose tree nothing tests (23.3); the spellings of HFS+ and NTFS, which VAL-5d does not fold; an issue whose tests need such a layout as a fixture, which a round cannot commit; and the export of `review`, which M2 builds. |
| D-44 | What M1 records, and what `sf run` does at its start, at a failure and at a wait, before the outbox, the recovery, the rounds, the escalation and the reconcile loop of M2 exist (8.8, ORCH-6 to ORCH-12). The review of draft 4 found four points. The roadmap gave M1a's store two tables, and a run that survives an interruption needs the activity record of AG-15, the journalled candidate and the spend of each model call, which neither holds; M1a also makes external writes with no outbox, and nothing said what `sf run` does with an activity that a killed one left (finding B7). What `sf run` does when a stage fails was said for a session limit alone (B8). M1b adds the CI wait and the wait for a review to a foreground command, while the loop that serves waits is M2, and both outcomes of those waits that are not the happy path lead to rounds (B9). And 23.2 filled a table `probes` in M1a that the roadmap did not give it (C8), of which D-39 had settled the table and left the spend. Nothing was observed for this decision: every point is a choice about the factory's own code, and no probe goes with it. The operator answered eleven questions on 2026-11-26, in three rounds, each as recommended. **The store.** M1a creates `runs`, `events`, `activities` and `spend`, each in the form of 18.1, and `outbox`, `etags` and `probes` arrive with M2 (ORCH-6). Two tables with a wider `runs` row were not taken, and neither were all seven. **The writes.** Through M1 `sf run` makes each external write itself, under its key of 8.5, and no write is stored first (ORCH-7). A small outbox in M1a was not taken. **A live activity.** At its start `sf run` reaps every activity that the store records as live and starts the stage again as a new stage execution, which does not count toward Poison (ORCH-8). Reaping and then exiting was not taken, and neither was refusing to start. **A failure.** Where the full pipeline has a round or a restart of the stage, `sf run` writes the feedback, records the return to `implement`, reports and exits with a failure, and the operator's next `sf run` starts `implement` with that feedback; the factory starts no second session by itself (ORCH-10). Verify rounds in M1a were not taken. The round counter of a failed gate moves and no cap is enforced, as D-22 has M1 record the limits that it does not enforce. No counter moving, which the review proposed, was not taken. **A stop.** Where the full pipeline escalates, resets, freezes or takes a new snapshot, `sf run` reports and starts nothing, the run keeps its stage, and the operator removes the cause and runs the command again, or cancels the run and approves the issue again (ORCH-11). It is the rule that 12.3 had for a merge conflict, now for every such case. The label and the comment of an escalation from M1b were not taken. After a result with `needs_human` the operator answers by editing the issue, cancels and approves again: a run of M1 keeps the snapshot of its claim, as D-36 has it. A new snapshot at every start of `sf run` was not taken. **The rows of 7.7.** `sf run` evaluates all of them when it starts on a run that exists and, in M1b, on every poll of the `ci` wait. It carries out those that M1 has an action for, and every other one stops the run (ORCH-9). Three rows alone were not taken. The operator cancels a run by `state:triage` or by closing the issue, and `sf cancel` arrives with the control API in M2; a `sf cancel` of M1a was not taken. **The waits of M1b.** `sf run` stays in the foreground through the `ci` wait, which a deadline bounds, and exits when the run enters `human_review`. Run again, it looks once: it finishes a run whose pull request is merged, returns to `implement` for a review that requests changes, stops for a conflict, and otherwise reports that the run waits (ORCH-12). The store lock is then free during a review, and a second issue can run. Staying until the merge was not taken, and neither was ending at every wait. **A red check, and a review that requests changes.** Each returns to `implement` by the rule of ORCH-10, with the tail of the failing step or the review as the feedback, and the next candidate goes to the same branch and pull request. The rule of a merge conflict was not taken for them: on the factory repository a red check on a head that passed verify is the expected case (BOOT-6), and each would cost a run from a new branch. One point differs from an answer. The answer on the rows of 7.7 put an edit of the issue under the stop, and ORCH-9 stops for one only before the `ready` stage: after it 7.7 has the factory note the edit and do nothing, and a run that stopped there could no longer return to `implement` for a review. That edit is noted whoever made it, where 7.7 escalates for an edit by anyone but an authorised actor, because through M1 `sf run` does not ask who edited. Two reviewers then read the change, one against the rules for editing this specification and one against the ticket and the answers, and what they found is in the text. The second traced sequences through the items and found six that did not hold: a review that requests changes started a round at every later `sf run`; a push that was cut off before its result was recorded showed at the next start as a foreign commit, and so did a ref at `base_sha`; a claim that met a foreign ref would have adopted it at the next start; one candidate on the `runs` row could pass the candidate of a failed round to the next; the label and the status comment of a return were lost where `sf run` was cut off after the transaction; and nothing said what a cancel does with a `state:ready` beside `state:triage`. It read the fixed text again and found that a session which changes nothing then left its round without a candidate. The first found eight places where an item and a section that it cites said two things, the three rows of 7.7 on an edited issue among them. Not asked of the operator, of the store and the writes: that the `runs` row holds the snapshot, the candidate of the current round, the tree that the workspace was last captured from and the last pushed head, and the event the rest; that the row holds no candidate from a return to `implement` until the next capture; that such a value is on the row before its write is sent; that the spend of an issue is the sum over its runs; that what a batch carries of the plan's window stays in its `events` row; that a stage which writes is left only when each of its writes has a result; that a create of the ref is a replay when the attempt was on the row before that `sf run` started, which 8.2 now says; that the pull request and the status comment are looked up before a second create; and that M1 has a row of its own in 19.4. Not asked, of the start and the read: that the activities of every run of the store are reaped, and not those of the named run alone; that `sf run` prints what it reaped; that a run in `claim` completes the claim before GitHub is read for it; the note in the handoff for a session that was cut off; that Poison and Systemic have no case before M2; what an interrupt records; that the evaluation ends at the first row that ends the run or stops it; which heads of the work branch are the factory's own; that `factory:hold` and `factory:restart` stop the run whoever applied them, that the run goes on whoever removed them, and that the second label is left as it is; that a `sf run` which finishes or cancels a run claims nothing in the same invocation; that the cancel takes from the issue the state label that the run had set, and leaves a `state:ready` that stands beside `state:triage`, by which the next `sf run` claims; and that `sf retry` arrives in M2 with `sf cancel`. Not asked, of a failure: the steps of ORCH-10 and their order, with the writes of a return before its transaction; the feedback of each row; that an outcome of a session moves no counter; that the `runs` row holds the id of the review that last started a round, and that a review starts one round; that the row names the feedback file of a return; that a session which changed nothing returns to `implement` with no candidate made, a first session included; each row of the table of ORCH-11 with the way on that it names, among them a setup command that fails, a capture that runs into its timeout, a pull request that the operator may reopen, and the workflow change of a `type:ci` issue, for which no patch is attached before M2; that the claim which follows a cancel for a foreign commit takes that commit off the branch, and that the report says so; that a claim which meets a ref it did not make closes its row as `cancelled` and sets no `state:needs-human`; and that `sf run` exits with a failure after a `needs_human`. Not asked, of the waits: the 30 s between two polls; that the `ci` wait ends at the later of two times, so that a `sf run` which starts on a run in `ci` waits once more; that the run of an M1a build rests at the stage `ci`; that a run which waits for its review ends `sf run` with 0; that the test-merge is made once for each `sf run`; that an approval makes `sf run` do nothing; that `sf run` names the other non-terminal runs of the store at its start; and that the nudge of PR-3 waits for M2. Not asked, of the rest: what the exit status tells apart; section 8.8 as the place of these items, and the ids ORCH-6 to ORCH-12; the words that the rows of M1a, M1b and M2 gain in section 22; and that no term joins `GLOSSARY.md`. Left open: which pid and which group the `activities` row holds at level `process`, which D-40 left open; the columns of each table, and the bytes of the two hashes of a snapshot (D-35), with the change that builds the store; what the report of `sf run` holds as data under `--json`; a required check that turns red after the pull request is ready, which 7.2 names and no row of 7.7 holds; whether a pull request goes back to draft when its run returns to `implement`; the label `state:in-progress` that a return sets for a review and that nothing sets back, where `sf run` was cut off before the transaction and the operator dismisses the review before the next one; and whether a verify command that runs into its timeout is a failed gate or an abandoned activity, which 8.2 and 11.2 do not say. |
| D-45 | What the claim checks of an issue beyond what 5.3 and 8.2 listed, how it reads an issue that holds more than one page of events or of comments, and how an acceptance criterion is written (5.3 rule 7, GH-21, WI-3, WI-4, SEC-2). The review of draft 4 found four points. SEC-2 is the control that 13.1 names against a stranger's text, and no step performed it: readiness had six rules, none of which read the author or an editor, and the scratch repository and the factory repository are public (finding S4). Rule 6 of 5.3 said that one GraphQL read is enough, while probe P-8 had found that the first page of the events holds the oldest and had read no second page (E2). 9.4 left a skip token in a title to the readiness lint, which nothing enforces before M3 (C3). And 6.2 gave the written form of a criterion in one table cell, while no row of M0, M1a or M1b built the parser that the result check of M1a and the manifest of M1b need (B10). The paging was observed first, on 2026-11-26, on one issue of the scratch repository that had been given 105 body edits, 111 rename and label events and 105 comments (P-8). The events and the comments come oldest first, `last: 100` returns the newest, and the page before a cursor was the same after the issue had gained an item of each kind at its newest end and after the comment that the cursor names was deleted. The edit history ended at 100 entries, the text as created and the newest 99 edits, and an edit by the scratch App had left the list with nothing in the response to say so. The operator answered eight questions on 2026-11-26, in two rounds, each as recommended, the two on the reads with those observations before them. **SEC-2.** It is rule 7 of 5.3, checked by the claim on the author, the edit history and the actors of the rename events, with one check of the permission for each distinct account. Through M1 a failure ends the claim with no run row and nothing written, and `sf run` names the account and where it stands; from M2 the factory does what it does for rule 6. `state:needs-human` from M2 was not taken, since a new label cannot make such an issue pass, and neither was a label that M1 writes, where no other refusal of the claim writes one. **The reads.** One query asks for the newest 100 of each list, and the claim pages back through the events to their end and through the comments for at most 10 pages, with the comments before them counted as left out (GH-21). An issue on which GitHub does not name an author, an editor or an actor is not dispatched. One page, with no claim of an issue that has more, was not taken: the cancels of M1 fill a page by themselves. **A full edit history.** An issue whose edit history holds 100 entries is not dispatched, since the list cannot be known to be whole. `sf run` says so, and the operator restates the text in a new issue (SEC-2). Checking the editors that GitHub still returns was not taken. **A skip token in a title.** The claim ends on it and `sf run` names the token, as D-35 has the claim end on a body over its cap (9.4, WI-4). Removing the token, as 9.4 removes a reference, was not taken: the pull request title would differ from the issue's without a sign, the removal has to be repeated because what is left can form a token again, and from M3 the lint refuses such a title in any case. **A criterion.** It is one line that begins at the first character of its line: a bullet that may be left out, the id, the method in parentheses and in small letters, a colon and the statement (WI-3). A numbered item, a task box and an indented line are no criteria, and a criterion over several lines was not taken. Every other line of the section that is not blank fails the issue, and what is reported names the line. Passing over such a line was not taken, and neither was failing only a line that looks like a criterion. The id is `AC-` and a number from 1 to 9999 without a leading zero. The ids are unique, and they may have gaps and stand in any order; `AC-1` to `AC-n` in order was not taken. **The parser.** M1a builds the section parser and the criterion parser, and the claim ends on a body that the pipeline of M1 cannot work from: a repeated section name, an empty Outcome, no criterion, or a line that fails WI-3. `sf run` names every rule that failed (WI-4). The rest of the lint stays in M3: the sections that a tier requires, the spec refs, the clarification marker, the comment on the issue and `sf issue lint`. `sf issue lint` in M1a was not taken, nor the claim of a body that the parsers cannot read, nor a roadmap with no parser before M3. One point of the text differs from an answer. The answer on the reads had rule 6 rest on the first read alone. The first read gives the title, the body and the time of the last body edit. The last rename event and the `labeled` event are the most recent of their kind over all pages of the events, since either lies on an older page once 100 later events follow it. Two reviewers then read the change, one against the rules for editing this specification and one against the ticket and the answers, and what they found is in the text. The first found the rule for a title stated in three places in three wordings, which 9.4 now holds alone; rule 7 restating SEC-2 with a term that left the scratch App out; and what fails that rule spread over GH-21 and SEC-2. The second traced cases through the items. It found that a rename older than the newest 100 events passed rule 6 as it was worded, which is the point above; that the count of the comments left out could not be computed from what the claim reads, so it is now a count of comments whoever wrote them, taken from the first read; that a read of a further page of the edit history changed no outcome, and it is gone; that nothing had `sf run` name the token; that nothing made a carriage return part of a line ending; that the later reads of `sf run` asked for 300 entries that no row uses; that this row listed several answers as not asked; and that two sentences said more of the probe than its record does. It read the fixed text again and found that the count had no case for a walk that reaches the oldest comment, and that this row still listed two answers as not asked. Not asked of the operator, of rule 7 and the reads: that the App is admitted on an issue whose author it is and on no other, by the id that the commit identity of 5.6 holds, that for a development build it is the scratch App, and that M1a builds that part with the rest; that the 10 pages count the first read; that the count of the comments left out is taken from the `totalCount` of the first read and counts comments whoever wrote them (SEC-4); that an item of an older page is taken as it is when its page is read; that `totalCount` is no test for a whole list of events; that a page which cannot be fetched is a failure of the request and none of the rule; that the later reads of `sf run` take none of the lists (ORCH-9); and that the resume of 7.5.1 does not evaluate rule 7 again. Not asked, of the title: that the rule reads the title as the host generates it for a pull request, after the sanitisation and without its references, and that one left with nothing but white space ends the claim too. Not asked, of a criterion: that `AC` is in capitals; where spaces and tabs may stand; that the statement holds at least one character that is no white space, and is not held to the form of 6.2; and what the parser makes of the four bodies of P-4. Not asked, of the parser and the claim: that the claim asks for the Outcome and the Acceptance criteria sections whatever the tier label; that the operator removes `state:ready` and applies it again after the edit; that the host takes the ids and the methods from the snapshot and never from a result, and parses them anew where it needs them; that a result with `success` names each id of the issue once and no other, that one which does not is treated as one that the schema rejects, and that with another status the list is not held to the ids (10.5); the row of tests in 19.4; that the term "Acceptance criterion" joins `GLOSSARY.md`; and the id GH-21 in 5.3, with WI-3 and WI-4. The first reviewer asked for the mark of a tunable number on the 9999. It carries none: the number is the operator's answer, and no measurement is expected to move it. Left open: whether GitHub keeps an edit that the list no longer names, and whether its page shows one; a cursor of the edit history after an edit; a list of more than two pages; an item of an older page that changes between two reads of one claim; what a deleted label or a deleted account leaves in the lists; who edited a comment, which SEC-3 does not read; how many permission checks the distinct authors of a long list of comments may cost; how far back the resume of 7.5.1 and the reconciler of 7.7 read, and what a resume does with an edit history that has become full, both with M2; what a run does when a later snapshot of it fails a rule of WI-4, and what the daemon tells the operator of an issue that it does not claim under one, both of which D-36 holds; whether the `criteria` of a reviewer's result (11.5) are held to the ids of the issue as an implementer's are, with that stage in M2; and which fields the issue form requires, which P-4 left open and M3 decides. |
| D-46 | Which version of the agent CLI M1 runs, which tools a session has, and which tests exist before the first real sessions with the subscription token (AG-5, AG-22, CLI-8, 19.4, 22). The review of draft 4 found six points. AG-5 refused dispatch outside a tested range that had no value, the lock-down was observed on one version, on macOS and outside a sandbox, and the record of probe P-21 handed AG-5 a decision that nobody took: a floor of 2.1.314, or accepting the leak that version fixed (finding E1). The roadmap first named the contract test in M2, while BUD-10 and DEV-7 leaned on it from M1a (C7), and neither that test nor the escape tests at level `process` were required before a session held the token (S8). 19.4, 21.2 and BOOT-3 gated every pull request and every install of M1 on tests against `fake-github`, which no row built (B11). The encoder rests on behaviour that GitHub does not document, and 19.4 tested it against the recorded answers alone (E9). And AG-1 passed a tool allowlist that no rule listed (S12). Nothing was probed for this decision. Three facts were read from what was already there: the `system/init` of a session of 2.1.311 without a tool list names 23 tools, with the Agent tool as `Task` and without `Glob` and `Grep` (the output of P-21 on the branch `prototype/claude-code-lockdown`, now in its record); the operator's Mac holds 2.1.326, the version of P-36; and the rows "Issue" and "One foreground run (M1)" of 19.4, which D-45 and D-44 wrote, already hold M1a's code against `fake-github`. The operator answered eleven questions on 2026-11-26, in two rounds, each as recommended. **The range.** A list of exact versions that is a constant of the build, with one version through M1 (AG-5). A key of the operator configuration was not taken. **The floor and the version.** No version below 2.1.314 joins the range. The version to try first is 2.1.326, which the operator installs on the server before M0's exit check. A version joins the list only in the change that records the contract test passing on it, so AG-5 names no number before M1a. Staying on 2.1.311, with the leak named in 23.3, was not taken. **The contract test.** The whole of it is M1a work, with the driver, the lock-down included. M1a's exit asks that it pass in the development VM, inside the sandbox, and it passes on the server before the first session of M1b. Through M1 a person starts it, and M2 adds the schedule. Leaving the lock-down part in M2 was not taken: M1 would have rested on the macOS observation of P-21 alone, on a repository whose settings file holds real hooks (DEV-9). **Its form.** A command of the build, `sf contract`, which the operator starts on the server as the factory's user (CLI-8). A test of the repository, run through `cargo`, was not taken: the factory's user reads the token and has no toolchain, the operator's own account would vouch for the sessions of another, and a schedule would need a checkout and a build on the server for each run. **The escape tests.** At level `process` they are an exit criterion of M0: in CI, in the development VM and on the server. M0 and not M1a, because M0 ends with the first session that holds the token inside the factory's sandbox. **`fake-github`.** It is in the M1a row for the calls of claim and publish and in the M1b row for the calls that M1b adds, and a call joins the adapter and the fake in one change. 19.4 has a column that says since which milestone a layer exists, and BOOT-3 says what its harness tests are during M1. `wiremock` and the scratch repository alone through M1 were not taken. **The encoder.** A test writes what the encoder makes of a mention, of each form of a reference and of a character reference to the scratch repository and reads it back as `bodyHTML` (19.4). It is M1a work and an exit criterion of M1a, started in the development VM, and GH-5a says that GitHub documents none of what the encoder rests on. **The scheduled run.** The scheduled end-to-end workflow against the scratch repository is M2 work, and the encoder's test runs in it from then. It puts the first secret into the Actions of the factory repository (13.5). **The tools.** 10.2 lists them for each role (AG-22): the implementer has `Read`, `Edit`, `Write`, `Glob`, `Grep`, `Bash` and the Agent tool, the planner and the reviewer `Read`, `Glob` and `Grep`, and the test author the implementer's. No role has a tool that fetches or searches the web, schedules work, starts a remote session, enters a worktree or loads a skill. The contract test asserts that a session has its role's list and `StructuredOutput`, and no other tool. A shell for the planner and the reviewer was not taken. **Web search.** It is not probed. 23.3 says that the model host is a way out which the proxy cannot see into, and that nobody observed whether the provider searches the web for a subscription token. A probe now, one session behind a proxy that allows the model host alone, was not taken: no session has the tool, so the answer would change no rule. Two reviewers then read the change, one against the rules for editing this specification and one against the ticket and the answers, and what they found is in the text. The first found the gate that holds a build on the server stated in five places and in three wordings, which BOOT-3 now holds alone; what the harness tests are stated twice with two contents, which 19.4 now holds; five older sentences that the change had made false, in 5.7, 13.5, SEC-7, 16.1 and DEV-7, two of which the second found as well, and the row of D-42, which still left the key open; the word for the version to try first, which was the glossary's word for a commit; a row of 19.4 named a contract test that the glossary says is none; and a general rule on when a test is written, which no answer asked for and which is gone. The second traced cases through the items. It found that 4.4 asked M0 for a version of a range that is empty until M1a; that CLI-8 denied the factory's user a toolchain, which 4.4 gives it, and the server a build, which DEV-7 has the operator make there; that the assertion on the tools let a tool of an MCP server pass, where the answer had the role's list and `StructuredOutput` alone; that nothing said which role's tools a session of the test has; that AG-5 called 2.1.326 the version of the development VM, which pins none; that the rule on the tools named a workflow, which no answer did; and that this row listed four answers as not asked. The second read the fixed text again. The fix had made a probe of two facts that the reviewers found resting on no observation, and one of them had been observed: the record of P-26 says that the allowlist takes the Agent tool as `Agent`, which AG-22 now states. The other is probe P-41: whether a subagent has a tool that its session lacks. The sessions of P-26 started subagents under a list, and nobody compared. That reading also found the new record of the tool lists saying more of one kept output than it holds. Not taken from the reviews: another name for `sf contract`, a 19.4 without the column, which repeats the roadmap, and a table without the tools of the roles that M2 and M3 build. Each is an answer of the operator's. Not asked of the operator, of the range: that `sf run` and the agent form of `sf sandbox exec` start no session outside it; and that the record of a pass is what `sf contract` reported, in the description of the change. Not asked, of the contract test: that the gate on the server begins with the first build that holds the driver (BOOT-3); that the host records no pass; that the command takes no store lock and writes nothing to GitHub, so a development build may run it; that each session of a case has a sandbox instance and a proxy of its own, and a working directory below the data directory; that through M1 its sessions have the implementer's tools; that it exits with 0 only where every case passed; that `--model` names its model; that SEC-7 and 13.5 admit the command as they admit `sf sandbox exec`; and that its cases are data of `factory-agent`, which `factory-daemon` runs. Not asked, of the tools: that the names are those of 2.1.311; that eight more tools are in no list because no stage needs them, `Workflow` among them; that the host's MCP file names no server (19.2); that a tool of a later CLI version is in no session until the table names it; and that each tool of the list is used without a prompt. Not asked, of the rest: that the fake answers what a probe saw GitHub answer, wherever one did; that the "Path patterns" row is M0 for the fold of VAL-5d, which the M0 row builds; that the encoder's test writes a comment and no other surface; that the run of the sandbox tests on the server is the run that DEV-7 already gave the operator before an install; the row that DEV-7 gains for the encoder's test, and its name in 19.4, "Encoder against GitHub"; probe P-41, and the tool list of P-21 in its record; the terms "Tested range" and "Contract test" in `GLOSSARY.md`; and the id AG-22 in 10.2. Left open: which version passes first, since 2.1.326 is the first to be tried and no more; whether the leak that 2.1.314 fixed reaches a session of AG-3a; whether the cases of the contract test gain what P-21 did not plant, which its record lists; the question of P-41; whether the provider carries out a web search for a subscription token; how many sessions a run of `sf contract` takes, under which caps, and what it costs of the plan; how the daemon schedules the test and learns of a pass, with M2; how the encoder's test is started, and whether it writes to a pull request body, a commit message or the output of a check run; what the fake answers where no probe saw GitHub; what, beside the refusal of AG-5, keeps the CLI on the server at one version; and whether three tools are enough for a planner and a reviewer, which M2 finds out when it builds them. |
| D-47 | What the secret rule of 11.3 is, what the literal-token scan reads, when it is tested, and which milestone builds three rows of the 11.3 table (SEC-13, 11.3, 19.4, 22). The review of draft 4 found two points. The secret rule was one row of a table, "Secret pattern in added content", and nothing said what a secret pattern is, what added content is for a binary file or for a file that moved, how a hit is reported without writing the secret to GitHub, or which crate matches; and the attribute rule, the binary rule and the size budget were placed in no milestone (finding B12). The literal-token scan, one of the four mitigations that 23.3 counts on for `subscription` mode, was half a sentence in a cell of 13.5, with no id and no statement of what it reads, and its test was an exit criterion of M2, after M1a and M1b had published with the real token (S7). Nothing was probed for this decision. One fact was read from what was already there: `Cargo.lock` holds `memchr` 2.8.3 (`cargo tree`, 2026-11-26). Two were looked for in the records of the probes and not found: which characters a subscription token holds, and what issuing a token again does to the one before it. The operator answered thirteen questions on 2026-11-26, in two rounds, each as recommended. **The pattern rule.** It leaves the design, and the literal-token scan is the only secret rule (SEC-13). A worker's environment holds one secret, and the scan finds it by its value. Deferring the pattern set to M5 was not taken, and neither was a stated set in M1a with a crate for regular expressions. The "secret-scan hits" of 15.2 and 15.3 are hits of the literal-token scan. **What is searched for.** The exact bytes of every credential that a worker's environment holds, which through M1 is the subscription token alone. No encoded form is searched for, and 23.3 says so. **A candidate.** The integrity stage reads every blob in which the candidate's tree differs from the tree at `base_sha`, whole and whatever it holds, every path of that diff, and the whole commit message. "Added content" is so defined nowhere, because no line is counted as added. **A text for GitHub.** `factory-github` searches the text of every write, the parts that the host wrote included, and each part that passes through the encoder before the encoder changes it and before a cap cuts it. It makes no write with a hit. **A hit.** It stops the run and returns nowhere, so it is no `fail` (VAL-4). The run never goes on: the `runs` row records the hit, every later `sf run` reports it again unless it finds the run cancelled, and the operator re-issues the token, cancels the run and approves the issue again (ORCH-11). Removing the cause and running again was not taken, since the scan would then search for the new value and pass the old one. From M2 a hit is an escalation with `policy-denial` whose only resume is a reset; a category of its own was not taken. **The report.** It names the place of a hit and never the value or a byte beside it. **The test.** A planted copy that is caught before it is written to GitHub is an exit criterion of M1a, and M2's exit no longer holds one. The test plants a made-up value, and M1b adds the surfaces that it builds (19.4). **The three rows.** The attribute rule, the binary rule and the size budget are M1b work, beside the dependency rule: all three serve the human review that decides a merge, and M1b has the first. **The probe.** P-42 asks what issuing a token again does to the one before it, and the operator answers it. **The name.** The rule is one item, SEC-13, under the one term "literal-token scan", which `GLOSSARY.md` defines; "credential scan" was not taken. **The small points,** which the operator took as one answer: the search is a pure function of `factory-core` on `memchr`; the value is the content of the token file without the white space at its end, read at the start of a command, and one under 32 bytes is refused; the journal, the logs, the feedback files and the terminal are not searched; the size budget follows its row through M1b, with a feedback that asks for a smaller change; the test plants the value in a text blob, a binary blob, the target of a link, a path, the commit subject and the body of a pull request; and the tripwire of 15.3 is left open. Not asked of the operator: the marker `[credential]`; that a command with a value that is too short exits before it does anything else, and that `sf doctor` reports it from M1a (CLI-1); that a command reads the token file once, so a token issued again is used and searched for from the next start; that a hit in a text for GitHub stops the stage that makes the write; that a value in the issue leaves a pushed candidate behind, and that the operator takes it out of the issue; that the run directory of a stopped run keeps the value until the retention of 18.2 removes it; that the `runs` row holds `size_strikes` (ORCH-6, ORCH-10); the rows of 19.2 and 19.4; the wording of the rows of 11.1 and 7.3, and of the M1a row of 22, which now gives the structural rules to capture as 11.3 does; the sentence of 7.6 on a `state:ready` that is handled as a reset, which that section had lacked for the workflow change of a `type:ci` issue too; and that the first line of this section names D-46 too, which it had left out. Two reviewers then read the change, one against the rules for editing this specification and one against the ticket and the answers, and what they found is in the text. Both found that a run which a hit stopped could not be cancelled: the cancel is made by the `sf run` that reads GitHub (ORCH-9), and the text had every later `sf run` start nothing, while ORCH-8 still had the run go on. SEC-13 and ORCH-8 now say that the read comes first. Both found two scopes for the search of a text. The answer had it stand where the encoder is called, and the encoder is called on the parts of a text that an agent, an issue, a review or a log wrote, never on a title or on what the host wrote. SEC-13 now has two searches, of each such part before the encoder and of the whole text of every write, and that is the one place where the text says more than the answer did. Both found the run directory given to 9.3, which removes target directories and exports. The first found the re-issue stated in 13.5 and in SEC-13, a paragraph in 11.3 that placed the rows a third time and held a rule without an id, which is gone, with its rule in ORCH-10, the exception for a hit stated three times in 11.3, a table row of ORCH-11 that stood outside its table, and the row of 7.3 and section 7.6, which knew nothing of the new outcome. The second traced cases, and read the fixed text again. It found that a value in the issue is not found again once the token is re-issued, which the text had claimed; that after a re-issue the scan is blind to the old value in the workspace of every other run, which is left open below; that nothing said whether a session gets the value that the command read at its start; and that the exit criterion of M1a asked for more than the test planted, which had no title, and called "before publish" a case that is caught after the push of a candidate without a hit. Not taken from the reviews: "with probe P-42" in the status row, since the probe is not answered and addresses no finding; and a row for a hit in the table of 8.6, which has none for the workflow change either. Left open: the answer of P-42; what a re-issue leaves behind, which is the old value in the workspace of another run that is not terminal, where the scan no longer finds it, and which matters only if P-42 finds that the old token still works; what a reset does for a hit when the cap of attempts is reached (7.6), with M2; the tripwire of 15.3, "any hit of the literal-token scan in a published commit", which cannot fire while the scan runs before every publish, and which M4 builds; whether a token can hold `GH-` and a digit; what a daemon does with a token that is issued again while it runs, with M2; the rest of the escalation of a hit, with M2; and what the attribute rule and the binary rule flag beyond their rows, which finding S10 of the same review asks. |
| D-48 | What the flags and the listing show the operator of a protected change: the file `CLAUDE.local.md`, the cap of the listing, a binary file, and the evidence of a criterion (VAL-3b, VAL-5h, VAL-5i, VAL-14b). It is the first part of an answer to five findings of the review of draft 4. `CLAUDE.local.md` was in no protected list, and probe P-21 had found that a session without the lock-down which finds one no longer reads `AGENTS.md` (finding S1). VAL-3 named the first 50 paths of a flagged rule in byte order, and all protected-path patterns are one rule, so that fifty files under `crates/` pushed `mise.toml` and `xtask/…` out of the listing (S9). The binary rule read a path that was text at the base alone, so a new binary file drew nothing (S10, in part). Item 3 of 11.6 put the implementer's evidence in a table cell, which cannot hold the fence of GH-5a (S11). Nothing was probed for this part. One fact was read from what was already there: the record of P-21 names what a session without the flags loaded from its working directory, and outside `.claude/` and `.mcp.json` those are `AGENTS.md`, `CLAUDE.md` and `CLAUDE.local.md`. The operator answered seven questions on 2026-11-26, in one round, each as recommended. **The name.** `CLAUDE.local.md` joins the built-in protected-path list, unanchored like every pattern there. **The rule behind the list.** The list holds every name that a session of Claude Code without the lock-down was found to load unasked from its working directory, and a test of the build holds the lock-down fixture of the contract test to it, so that a name cannot join the fixture alone (VAL-5h). It covers Claude Code and no other tool; a file of another tool is a pattern of `protected_extra`. **A candidate that adds one fails**, through the built-in deny list beside `target/**`, and a change to one that the base tree holds is flagged. A flag alone was not taken: on the factory repository nearly every pull request carries one. **The summary.** The listing of the protected-path rule begins with every pattern that matched and its count, and that part is never cut (VAL-3b). **The cap** of 50 paths holds for each pattern. The groups stand by rising count and then in the byte order of the patterns, and a path that two patterns match is named under both. **Every binary blob.** The binary rule flags a path whose blob in the candidate is binary, added or changed, whatever the page of a pull request renders for it; a removed one draws nothing (VAL-5i). The cost, a flag on every pull request that adds an image, is accepted. **The evidence.** Item 3 of 11.6 is a table of cells that the host wrote, the id, the result and the method, and below it one heading for each criterion with its statement and its evidence in fenced blocks (VAL-14b). The sections without the table were not taken. The operator was not asked about twelve things: the ids VAL-3b, VAL-5h, VAL-5i and VAL-14b; that the summary and the paths are two fenced blocks under captions of the host; that the host says once, after the fence, how many lines it left out, and not once for each pattern, since a pattern is no text of the host's and the summary holds each count; that the files of the computed instruction set are one group under the marker `[instruction-set]`, which no pattern can be; that the other rules keep one cap for the rule; that a symbolic link at `CLAUDE.local.md` fails as a file does, and so does one that a candidate moves; that the test of the fixture is a test of the build and no case of `sf contract`; that a criterion's statement stands in a fenced block of its own, as text of an issue; that the reviewer's `satisfied` and `confidence` are written by the host beside the caption of its evidence, from M2; that the cap of length is the encoder's for each block; that a blob which was binary and is text draws nothing from the binary rule; and the rows of section 22 that name these items. No reviewer read this text before it was merged: the operator asked for the merge when the first round was answered, and the review is made with the second part. Left open, with the second part: which file names the tools of DEV-2 read unasked, and what follows for the lists (finding S3, probe P-43); what GitHub shows no content of with no attribute set, and what the listing of item 5 of 11.6 names (the rest of S10, probe P-44); and the gap that the cap leaves inside a pattern that names a whole tree, which 23.3 records. |
| D-49 | Under which names the configuration of the toolchain and of a check tool is protected, and what the pull request body names of a change that GitHub's page may not show (VAL-5i, VAL-5j, VAL-14c, DEV-2b, BOOT-4). It is the second part of the answer that D-48 began, to two findings of the review of draft 4. The lists protected one file name for each tool of the checks, and the review named seven more that the tools read, from its reviewer's knowledge (finding S3). The threat table promised flags on hidden diffs, the rules followed git attributes, and what GitHub hides without one was the reviewer's knowledge too (S10). Both were observed before the operator was asked. Probe P-43 ran eleven tools in trees of their own on the operator's Mac: all seven names are read, `.clippy.toml`, `rust-toolchain`, `.mise.toml` and `mise.local.toml` win over the protected name beside them, `.tool-versions` and `.config/mise.toml` are read below `mise.toml`, and `.config/nextest.toml` is the one file that nextest reads; rustfmt, `cargo deny`, typos and mise read more names than the review listed; and an argument that names the configuration leaves a second file in effect for clippy and for the exceptions file of `cargo deny`. Probe P-44 read four pull requests of the scratch repository without a login: the page showed no content for a binary file on either side of a change, collapsed every file that GitHub takes for generated, Rust source under such a path among them, collapsed a file from 400 lines of diff where the diff held eight files or more and from 16,000 bytes where it held 25, and collapsed every file after 5,700 to 6,000 lines or 200,000 to 220,000 bytes of rendered diff. The operator answered nine questions on 2026-11-26, in one round of two halves, each as recommended. **The built-in list** gains `.clippy.toml`, `rust-toolchain`, `.deny.toml`, `deny.exceptions.toml`, `.deny.exceptions.toml`, `rustfmt.toml`, `.rustfmt.toml` and `nextest.toml`: every name under which the probe found clippy, rustfmt, `cargo nextest`, `cargo deny`, rustup or cargo to read its configuration (VAL-5j). Each is flagged and none fails, since a repository may keep its configuration under either name. **BOOT-4** gains every dot file and dot directory at the root of the factory repository, `/.*` and `/.*/**`, and the names of mise and typos that no dot covers: `mise.*.toml`, `mise/**`, `typos.toml`, `_typos.toml`, `pyproject.toml` and `.ignore`. Every file at the root was not taken: it would protect `Cargo.lock`, which D-24 left out. **No argument.** No command passes its configuration as an argument, and the structural test of the factory repository fails on a file under a second name (DEV-2b). An argument was not taken because it fails for the two tools that matter most here: clippy carries the spawning discipline of 19.3, and the exceptions file loosens `cargo deny`. **mise's own trust.** `mise exec` and `mise run` trusted a directory without asking. 23.3 records it and no control is added: where they run on a branch, the code of that branch runs too. **GitHub's detection of generated files** stays unmodelled, and 23.3 records what the probe found (VAL-14c). A copy of its list of names as a flag was not taken: the rules are documented nowhere, those that the page applied were behind the source of GitHub's library, and a copy goes stale without a sign. **Large files.** Item 5 of 11.6 names each file whose own diff holds at least 400 lines or 16,000 bytes, and says in one line when the whole diff holds at least 5,700 lines or 200,000 bytes that the page may collapse what follows. It is information and no flag, and the four numbers are tunable and the probe's. **One number differs from the answer.** The operator was asked about 5,000 lines for the whole diff, which is what the first report of the probe said. Its output shows 19 files of 300 lines rendered, 5,700 lines, and the review of this text found the difference; the answer was that the numbers are the probe's, so the text holds 5,700. **Binary on either side.** The binary rule flags a changed path whose blob is binary at the base or in the candidate, so a file that was binary and is text is flagged, which D-48 had left out without asking the operator (VAL-5i). **What the body calls it.** The listing names three groups that the host can compute, an attribute, a binary file and a large diff, in the place of "hidden or collapsed in the GitHub diff", and the threat table of 13.1 names the two of them that are flags. **The view of someone who is logged in** was not observed, since no browser was at hand; P-44 is recorded as answered for the page without a login, and the numbers change when the operator's view differs. ### 23.2 Probes

Facts the design depends on that research could not confirm from primary sources. A probe is answered by whatever can observe it: a script, a live session, a workflow run, or the operator on the server. The answer is recorded in `docs/research/probes.md`, and the record says how it was observed. `sf` holds no probe suite (D-39): earlier drafts had `sf doctor --probe` answer each probe against a scratch repository, and many of the answers so far are no question about a repository. A probe that writes to GitHub or reads from it is run on the scratch repository, and never on the factory repository. All are M0 unless marked: M0's exit (22) owes those that a rule of M0 or M1 depends on, however each was answered, and every other probe carries the mark of the milestone whose rules first read its answer (D-38). A probe that needs an App identity is answered with the scratch App (GH-11a). An answer observed with the operator's token stands unless it is about what an identity may do or may read; such an answer is observed again with the scratch App's installation token, and its row says so. An answered probe keeps its row, which then also states the answer.

| # | Question | Design impact |
|---|---|---|
| P-1 | What does creating an existing ref return (409 or 422, and the message)? Answered on 2026-11-20 by calls on the scratch repository, with the operator's token and not an installation token: a 422 with the message `Reference already exists`, whatever SHA the call asks for, under API versions `2026-03-10` and `2022-11-28`. Every fault in the ref name or the object that was tried got a 422 as well, told apart by its message alone, and a branch name that collides with the ref gets the same message as an object that is not a commit. No 409 was seen. Of two creates sent at once, one succeeded and the other got this 422, in each of 40 pairs; neither the answer nor the ref says who created it. Observed again on 2026-11-25 with the scratch App's installation token (D-38): every case that was tried with the operator's token got the same status and message, and of ten pairs of creates sent at once, one request succeeded and the other got this 422. With a ruleset on `factory/**` that restricts creation, the call got a 422 with the message `Reference update failed`, for a ref that exists and for one that does not, and the read of the ref returned 200 for the one and 404 for the other. With a ruleset that restricts updates, or deletions and force pushes, the answers were those without a ruleset. No 409 was seen in 83 creates. An empty repository and a ruleset of an organisation were not tried. What the other rules of a ruleset and a classic branch protection do to the create, and to the factory's other writes to the branch, is probe P-45. | 8.2, 8.5, 20.1 |
| P-2 | (M2) Does an approving review from a GitHub App, or from `github-actions[bot]`, count toward required approvals under a ruleset? | Onboarding warning; PR-2 already ignores them |
| P-3 | (Not scheduled) Can an App bot be an issue assignee? | Optional "assigned to the factory" signal |
| P-4 | How does an issue form render into the body? Answered on 2026-11-20 by four issues that the operator created on the scratch repository through the form of 6.2, read back through REST and GraphQL: one block per field in the form's order, each the line `### <label>`, a blank line and the field's text as submitted, with a blank line between blocks. An empty field holds `_No response_`; a field of spaces and line breaks holds those. Nothing is escaped, so a heading typed into a field cannot be told from the form's own, and a carriage return in a field stays in the body. A sketch of the parser of WI-1 read back every field of the two bodies made from ordinary text; in the third, written to look like the form's output, two names repeat (WI-1a); in the fourth, a fence left open in one field takes the next field's heading with it, on GitHub's page as in the sketch. Two lines typed by hand arrived with a line feed between them. | 6.2, WI-1 to WI-1b, WI-3, 6.4 |
| P-5 | Is a push that changes `.github/workflows/` rejected for an App without the Workflows permission on every push path? Is one accepted that changes a directory of that name below the root (`sub/.github/workflows/`) or in another letter case at the root (`.GitHub/workflows/`), and does GitHub take a file there for a workflow? Does it read a workflow through a symbolic link whose path is `.github` or `.github/workflows`? The three questions that need no App were answered on 2026-11-21 on the scratch repository, with the operator's token and not an installation token. GitHub took a file for a workflow only at `.github/workflows/<name>` at the root, in that letter case: a workflow under `sub/.github/workflows`, under `.GitHub/workflows` or under `.github/Workflows` started no run on a push and was not registered. With `.github/workflows` a link to a directory that held a workflow, and with `.github` such a link, no run started on a push to another branch, on a push to the default branch or for a pull request, while a regular workflow beside each of them ran. A link that was itself a file of `.github/workflows` was registered as a workflow, and its run failed before any job. The App part was answered on 2026-11-25 with the scratch App's installation token. GitHub refused the App every push that adds or changes a file under `.github/workflows/` at the root: a workflow, a file that is none, an empty file, a file one directory further down and a link that is a file of the directory, to a new branch and to an existing one. It refused the addition of a workflow through the contents endpoint and the tree endpoint of the REST API, with a 403. It accepted a push that only removes a workflow or changes its mode, and a push that leaves the workflows as they are, to a branch that the App had created at a commit with a workflow in its tree. It accepted a push of a workflow under `sub/.github/workflows`, `.GitHub/workflows` or `.github/Workflows`, and a push that makes `.github/workflows` or `.github` a link, and none of those five started a run. A push of two commits was accepted where the first adds a workflow and the second removes it, and the App could then put no ref on the first. It could put a ref on a commit of the operator's that adds a workflow once that commit was the head of a branch, and merge such a branch, and the workflow ran. The events `schedule`, `workflow_dispatch` and `pull_request_target` were not observed. | GH-11; defence in depth for 11.3; the reach of the workflow rule (VAL-5e); what a link at those names draws (VAL-5f) |
| P-6 | Does the agent CLI's inner Bash sandbox work inside the outer bubblewrap sandbox and inside the microVM, or must it be disabled? Answered on 2026-11-20 for bubblewrap, on the server as the factory's user, with a stand-in for the factory's sandbox and Claude Code 2.1.311. It works where the stand-in lets a process create a user namespace, once `socat` is installed: the CLI's sandbox nested and confined the command further. Under `--disable-userns` each command failed, or ran unsandboxed when the model asked for that. Without `socat` the CLI warned on stderr and ran every command unsandboxed. It does not have to be disabled: it is off unless a settings file turns it on, and with it off the Bash tool ran in the CLI's own namespaces, under `--disable-userns` too. `sandbox.enabled: false` in the host's `--settings` file is the setting, and it turned the sandbox off when the config directory had turned it on. The microVM was not tried (M2), and AG-8 does not wait for it. | AG-8, SEC-6b |

| P-8 | Who is reported as the editor of an issue title and body through the API? Is `lastEditedAt` set for API edits, and does a title change always produce a rename event? Answered on 2026-11-20 by edits of six issues on the scratch repository, through REST, GraphQL and the web interface, by the operator and by a workflow as `github-actions[bot]`; the factory's own App does not exist yet. The three paths report alike. A body edit sets `lastEditedAt` and `editor` and adds an entry to the edit history, which names the editor of every edit; `editor` names the last one only. A title edit moves none of these and produces a rename event that names its actor: one for each of 32 changes, those of case or of trailing spaces alone included. Sending or saving a value the issue already has leaves no trace. Every time is a whole second, and a body edit sent just before a `labeled` event, or at once with it, can carry that event's time. An App is a `Bot` with the login `github-actions` in GraphQL and `github-actions[bot]` in REST, under one numeric id. The reads were observed again on 2026-11-25 with the scratch App's installation token (D-38), on which SEC-1 and SEC-2 rest: on the six issues, and on one that the App filed, it read the same `editor`, `lastEditedAt`, edit history and events as the operator's token, in each of 21 pairs of responses, and it read the permission endpoint of SEC-1. The scratch App is named as the Actions App is: a `Bot` whose login has the suffix `[bot]` in REST and not in GraphQL, under one numeric id, which is not the App's id. What an issue holds beyond one page was observed on 2026-11-26, on one issue that had been given 105 body edits, 111 rename and label events and 105 comments, read with both tokens (D-45). The events and the comments come oldest first, and they page: `last: 100` returned the newest 100 and `before` the ones before them, and the page before a cursor was the same after the issue had changed and after the comment that the cursor names was deleted. The edit history ends at 100 entries: the API returned the text as created and the newest 99 edits, with a `totalCount` of 100 and no further page, and an edit by the scratch App was in no response. The two tokens read the same in each of 25 pairs. | 5.3 rules 6 and 7, GH-21, WI-3, 7.5, 7.7, SEC-1, SEC-2 |

| P-15 | Which encodings suppress mentions, issue references and closing keywords in comments, pull request bodies and commit messages? Answered on 2026-11-21 by text written to the scratch repository in seven encodings, by the operator and by a workflow as `github-actions[bot]`, and read back as rendered HTML, timeline events and the operator's notifications; the factory's own App does not exist yet. In a comment, an issue body and a pull request body a backslash escape and a character reference suppress nothing: the mention notified, the references were recorded and the pull request closed its issue. A fenced block and a code span suppress all three in a comment, an issue body and a pull request body, as long as the text does not end the fence. A fence suppresses nothing in a commit message, where the commit closed its issue. A zero-width joiner or space after the `@`, the `#` or the hyphen of `GH-` suppresses all three on every surface. An issue URL is matched by its path, `owner/repo/issues/<n>`, with or without a scheme and a host, and a zero-width space inside the path stops it. A closing keyword never acted without a live reference directly after it. Text set by an edit acts as text written new. A mention and a `#<n>` in an issue title did nothing. In a commit message `&#<n>;` is a reference to issue `<n>`. No notification from a commit message was seen, and a push with the workflow's token left no reference events. | GH-5a, PLAN-5, 9.4 |

| P-21 | In non-bare `claude -p` with `--setting-sources user` and `--strict-mcp-config`, which of these still load from the working directory: `CLAUDE.md`, `CLAUDE.local.md`, skills, commands, subagents, plugins, hooks? Is there a flag or setting that disables each? Answered on 2026-11-19 by live sessions of Claude Code 2.1.311 on macOS, outside the factory's sandbox: none of them, and neither `AGENTS.md`, `.claude/rules/`, the `env` blocks of the two settings files, the permission rules of `.claude/settings.local.json`, nor anything found in a subdirectory or written mid-session. `--setting-sources user` alone does this, `.mcp.json` included. | P-22 | Under subscription authentication: do `--max-turns` and `--max-budget-usd` work, what usage does the stream report, and what does the CLI emit when a plan limit is reached (event shape and reset time)? Which hosts does the CLI contact? Answered on 2026-11-19 by the same sessions, on a `claude setup-token` token, except for the plan limit, which was not reached on purpose. Both caps work. With the prompt on the command line, as in the sessions that reached one, each ends the process with `error_max_turns` or `error_max_budget_usd` and exit code 1; with stream-JSON input, which the driver uses (AG-1), the process stays alive after either result (P-26). The final `result` holds tokens and API-equivalent dollars per model, as running totals for the session; per-call output tokens are in `message_delta` events only. A session that completes emits one `rate_limit_event`, after its first response, with the window, its utilisation, `resetsAt` in Unix seconds and the account's usage-credit state; the event of a rejected request is known from the documentation only. `api.anthropic.com` is the one host a session needs; without `CLAUDE_CODE_DISABLE_NONESSENTIAL_TRAFFIC=1` the CLI also asks for a Datadog log host. | Section 14, 8.3, SEC-11, 13.5 |
