| P-25 | Does adding a label that does not exist fail, or create the label? Answered on 2026-11-21 by calls on the scratch repository, with the operator's token, which has admin permission there, and not an installation token: the REST API creates the label, grey and without a description, and applies it. Each of the four calls that take label names did so (add labels, replace the label set, update an issue, create an issue), under API versions `2026-03-10` and `2022-11-28`, and each answered with its ordinary success. `gh issue edit --add-label` refuses with `'<name>' not found`. In the one request that tried it, to the add-label endpoint, a name that differs from an existing label only in letter case applied the existing label and created nothing. Observed again on 2026-11-25 with the scratch App's installation token (D-38), which holds the Issues permission and no admin permission: each of the four calls created the label and applied it, under both API versions, and each applied the existing label when it was sent a name in another letter case, as did a read of a label by its name. With that token the App also listed the labels, created one with a colour and a description, changed it, removed one from an issue and deleted labels. | GH-1, GH-3a, 20.1, D-21 |
| P-26 | (M1a) What a session limit meets on the stream of a headless session. With stream-JSON input, does the CLI's `--max-turns` count start over with each message while its budget total runs on, as the documentation says? What does the stream show of a subagent's model calls: the lines, their token counts, and whether `--max-turns` and `--max-budget-usd` count them? Which fields of a `rate_limit_event` show usage credits switched on, and in use? The first two were answered on 2026-11-21 by live sessions of Claude Code 2.1.323 on macOS, on a `claude setup-token` token and outside the factory's sandbox. The turn count starts over with each message and the budget total runs on over the session; neither cap ends the process. A subagent's calls are `assistant` lines with a `parent_tool_use_id`, whose input and cache counts are real and whose output count is a placeholder, with no `message_delta`. `total_cost_usd` and `modelUsage` include them. The budget cap counts them; the turn cap does not, and a subagent runs on when the main loop reaches it. Left to itself the CLI runs a subagent in the background, which gives one message two results and can let a budget cap pass with no `result` and an exit code of 0; with `CLAUDE_CODE_DISABLE_BACKGROUND_TASKS=1` every subagent ran in the foreground. A foreground subagent's text-only calls are in the stream only with `--forward-subagent-text`. The third is not provoked: it is recorded when a session meets it (BUD-8). | BUD-6, BUD-8, BUD-10, AG-1, AG-11, AG-14 |
| P-27 | Which command lines make git skip its hooks, what does git hand a hook, and does a `PreToolUse` hook of Claude Code refuse such a command in a live session? Answered on 2026-11-21 on git 2.56.1 and Claude Code 2.1.323 on macOS, by a script in throwaway repositories and by hand in the session that wrote the hook. On a commit, `--no-verify`, `--no-verif`, `--no-veri` and `-n`, alone or among other short options, skip the hook; a shorter abbreviation is refused as ambiguous, the last of `--verify` and `--no-verify` wins, and a word that is the value of another option, `-n` after `-m` or `--` after `--mess`, is no flag and does not end the options. `git push --no-verify` skips the pre-push hook, and `-n` there is a dry run. A `core.hooksPath` given through `-c`, `--config-env` or the `GIT_CONFIG_*` variables skips every hook. A hook that is not executable is ignored, with a hint, and the commit goes through. A hook runs at the top of the work tree with `GIT_INDEX_FILE` set, and in a linked worktree `GIT_DIR` too. A relative `core.hooksPath` holds in a linked worktree. The `PreToolUse` hook refused each skip it was given, alone and in a compound command, and let a commit through whose message names the flags. With `"if": "Bash(git *)"` it was not run for `time git …`, `env … git …` or git named by its path; with a filter of its own it refused those. A command inside `bash -c` went through both, and a flag that comes out of a variable skips the hook as one written out does. | DEV-9, DEV-10 |
| P-28 | (M1b) How does GitHub read a `CODEOWNERS` pattern? Does an entry written by the rule of VAL-3a own what its pattern protects: `**/crates/**` a file under `crates/` at the root and under a nested `crates/`, `**/Cargo.toml` the manifest at the root and one in a crate, an entry with a leading `/` the root alone? Is a path that differs from an entry only in letter case owned? Does the file read `?` as one character within a component and `a/**/b` as VAL-5c does, how is an entry written whose pattern holds a space or begins with `#`, and does an entry for a name without a wildcard, such as `**/Cargo.toml`, also own what lies below a directory of that name (D-29)? By GitHub's documentation, read on 2026-11-21, the paths are case-sensitive and the file follows most of the rules of `.gitignore`, without negation and without character ranges. Its sample file shows `**/logs` and `apps/` matching in any directory and `/docs/` at the root only, and has no pattern that ends in `/**` and no bare file name. No API names the owner of a path. Answered on 2026-11-22 on the scratch repository, with 67 variants of the file passed over its default branch one at a time, in 68 commits, each read through the endpoint that lists the file's errors (`GET /repos/{o}/{r}/codeowners/errors`) and through the owner that the page of a file takes from an endpoint of the web interface, on 64 or 65 test files. In each form that was asked about, an entry of VAL-3a owned the files that its pattern matches by VAL-5c: under `crates/` at the root and below it, the manifest at the root and in a crate, the root alone for a leading `/`, and `a/**/b` with none or more components between. It owned more where its last component matches the name of a directory: every file below that directory, so `**/Cargo.toml` owned what lies in a directory named `Cargo.toml`. It owned no path in another letter case or normal form. `?` stood for one byte and so not for `é`. A space ended the pattern and a `#` began a comment wherever it stood, so that `**/#hash.md` became the entry `**/` without an owner, which matched every file, left it without an owner and drew no error; with a backslash before it a space, a tab or a `#` was part of the pattern, which then owned the one name. A pattern copied without the prefix was read from the root alone where it holds a `/` between two components and does not begin with `**/`. A later line decided, and a line without an owner left its files with none. GitHub used the file under `.github/`, then the one at the root, then the one under `docs/`, and found `.GitHub/CODEOWNERS` and `codeowners` too. The errors endpoint named a character class, an unknown owner and a word that is no owner, and nothing for a `#` in a pattern or an entry without an owner; it gave a 404 where no file exists. Not observed: which file a pull request is held to, and whether a required code-owner review reads an entry as the page does (P-18); and whether an installation token may read the errors endpoint (P-33). | VAL-3a, VAL-5c, 20.1, BOOT-6 |
| P-29 | Which spellings of a name does a case-insensitive file system take for one name, which does git, and how does the crate `globset` read a pattern? Answered on 2026-11-21 on the operator's Mac (macOS 27.0.1, git 2.56.1), by scripts in throwaway directories. APFS took two names for one in each of 21 pairs exactly where they are a canonical caseless match by the Unicode Standard: full case folding (`straße` and `STRASSE`, the long s, the Kelvin sign, the ligature `ﬁ`) and canonical normalisation, and neither compatibility forms nor the Turkic `i`, a zero-width code point or a trailing dot. git folds the letters A to Z in a case-insensitive pathspec and compares every other byte as it is. It refuses a path component that is a spelling of `.git`: in any ASCII letter case always, with a code point that HFS+ ignores under `core.protectHFS`, and in a spelling of NTFS under `core.protectNTFS`; it accepted the same spellings of other names. `globset` 0.4.21 matches `?` against one byte, lets `[!x]` match a `/`, and folds ASCII letters only. A sketch of the matcher of VAL-5c and VAL-5d on ICU4X 2.4.0 gave the expected answer for each of 55 pairs and 32 patterns. A case-insensitive file system other than APFS (ext4 with `casefold`, NTFS, HFS+) was not observed. The definition of a canonical caseless match, D145, was read in a summary then and word for word on 2026-11-26, in version 18.0.0 of the standard. | VAL-5c, VAL-5d, 9.4, 19.2 |
| P-30 | Does cargo read its configuration through a symbolic link, and how does a diff show a symbolic link? Answered on 2026-11-21 on the operator's Mac (macOS 27.0.1, cargo 1.100.0, git 2.56.1), by a script in a throwaway directory. With `.cargo` a link to a directory that holds a `config.toml`, and with `.cargo/config.toml` a link to such a file, cargo ran an alias defined there; with no `.cargo`, and with a link to a path that does not exist, it did not, and it gave no warning for that link, nor did a build. `git diff-tree -r --raw --no-renames` lists a link as one entry with mode `120000` on the side that holds it: status `A` for a new link, `M` for one pointed elsewhere, `T` for a file that became a link or a link that became a file, and `D` for a removed one. A tracked directory that became a link is the link's `A` and a `D` for each file that was in it, and a link that became a directory the reverse. A change to the file that a link points at is listed under that file's path alone. Linux, and clippy, rustup and `cargo deny` on a link, were not observed. | VAL-5f, 20.1 |
| P-31 | What does `pulldown-cmark` report for a code span, a link and an HTML comment? Answered on 2026-11-21 on the operator's Mac (macOS 27.0.1, cargo 1.100.0), by a throwaway package on `pulldown-cmark` 0.13.6 without its default features, on two sample texts and on the factory repository's `AGENTS.md`. A code span came back with its text, also where a run of two backticks held a backtick and where it stood in a table cell. An inline link and the three kinds of reference-style link came back as links, each with its destination as written: with a fragment, a query and percent-escapes, with `..`, and with a leading `/`. An image and an autolink were told apart from a link, and a bare path in the prose was reported as nothing. Nothing inside a fenced or an indented code block was reported as either form, and nothing inside an HTML comment, inline or as a block. A comment block ran through the whole line that held its `-->`, and to the end of the text where nothing closed it. A `<!--` in a code span came back as a code span, and one in the middle of a paragraph that nothing closed as text. In `AGENTS.md` it found 44 code spans, 35 of them different, and no link. Without its default features the crate resolved with `bitflags`, `memchr` and `unicase` alone. Linux, the tables and other extensions of GitHub's dialect, and a text that is not valid UTF-8 were not tried. | AG-2b, 19.2 |
| P-32 | What does GitHub's page hide of an issue body and of a comment, and does a CommonMark reading of an HTML comment hide the same? Answered on 2026-11-22: 71 sample texts were rendered by GitHub's Markdown API in the context of the scratch repository, 23 of them were also read back as the `bodyHTML` of four issues there, and 20 of those as that of a comment, written with the operator's token, all 71 were read back as files of a branch there, and all were read by a throwaway package on `pulldown-cmark` 0.13.6. The API, the issues and the files agreed on every text. The page showed a `<!--` in a code span, in a code block, after a backslash and inside a paragraph that nothing closed, as CommonMark reads them. It hid more than an HTML block that begins with `<!--`: a comment on a line inside a `<div>`, a `<pre>` or a `<span>` block; everything after a `<!--` in such a block that nothing closed, through the next `-->` in raw HTML or to the end; and `<?`, `<!` and `</` before a space, each through the next `>`. It showed the text after a `-->` on its line, and it closed a comment at `--!>`. A `<!-- x -->` after a vertical bar inside a code span of a table cell was hidden, and the crate reported it as inline HTML only with its table extension on. A link reference definition was hidden, a link title and the alternative text of an image were in attributes alone, and a table cell beyond the columns was dropped. A `<!--` in a title was shown as text. ESC, backspace, DEL and U+0085 came back as U+FFFD, and a zero-width space, a direction override, a tag character, a variation selector and a soft hyphen came back as they were. A zero-width space inside `<!--` made it text. The reading of AG-2b before D-35 kept text that the page hid in 25 of the 71 texts, and the rule of SEC-4, as a sketch, in 5, none of them a comment. A comment that its author minimised came back with `isMinimized` true and its body whole, for each of six reasons. An App's token, the page in a browser, a comment that another account minimised and Linux were not tried. The answer stands without an App's token (D-52): it is about what the page hides of a text, and not about what an identity may do or may read (D-38). | SEC-3, SEC-4, AG-2b |
| P-33 | (M5) May an installation token read `GET /repos/{o}/{r}/codeowners/errors`, and under which permission of the App? The operator's token read it (P-28). If the App's token may not, `sf repo check` reads it with the operator's own credential, as it reads the bypass list (DEP-4). | VAL-3a, 20.1 |
| P-34 | What does cargo read as a dependency in a manifest, and what does a lockfile hold for a package? Answered on 2026-11-22 on the operator's Mac (macOS 27.0.1, cargo 1.100.0, git 2.56.1), by a script in a throwaway directory, after the Cargo reference was read. cargo read a dependency from `dev_dependencies` and `build_dependencies`, at the top level and under `target`, in a package of edition 2021, and refused those tables in one of edition 2024; the reference names neither. It followed the key `registry-index` of a dependency, which the reference does not name either, to a registry of its own. An entry of `workspace.dependencies` that no member inherits was not resolved. A dependency that a manifest holds as optional is in the lockfile whether or not a feature turns it on, and a feature added to an entry brought a package into the lockfile that no manifest names. A renamed dependency is in the lockfile under the name of `package`. A lockfile of `version = 4` holds one `[[package]]` table for each package: a registry package with a `source` and a `checksum`, a git package with a `source` that ends in `#` and the commit and with no `checksum`, and a package of the tree, or one that a `[patch]` by `path` put in the place of another, with neither. `[replace]` leaves two tables, and an unused patch one under `[[patch.unused]]`. A `[patch]` or a `[replace]` in a member that is not the workspace root was ignored with a warning. With no manifest changed, cargo built the version and the git commit that an edited lockfile named. It dropped, or under `--locked` refused, a package that no manifest asked for and a package from another place than its manifest asked for, and it refused a changed checksum. Where a manifest asked for more than its lockfile held, it stopped under `--locked` and without it fetched and built the missing package. It did not open a `Cargo.lock` in a member's directory. A manifest whose dependency table or entry has another shape did not load. Not run: another registry, a vendored source, the configuration file, a lockfile of an older format, and anything on the server. | VAL-5e, VAL-5g, 20.1; decision D-32 |
| P-35 | Does GitHub hold a release workflow as DEV-12 needs, on a public repository of a personal account? Does an environment that admits tags `v*` alone and names a required reviewer hold a job of a workflow that a tag push started until that reviewer approves, and may the reviewer be the account that pushed the tag? Does a ruleset that restricts the creation, the update and the deletion of tags `v*`, with the repository-admin role as its only bypass actor, refuse such a tag to an App that holds Contents: write, and take it from the operator? Does `gh attestation verify` with `--signer-workflow`, `--source-ref` and `--deny-self-hosted-runners` accept the archive of such a run and refuse one that a workflow built from another ref? To be answered on the scratch repository before the release workflow is merged, the question of the ruleset with the scratch App's installation token. | DEV-12, DEV-13, VAL-7 |
| P-36 | Three facts about Linux that M0 builds on. Does `bwrap --disable-userns` do on the hosted runners of DEV-5, with the bubblewrap that apt installs there and inside the network namespace of DEV-6, what it did on the server (P-6)? In a Lima VM as DEV-3 describes the development VM: do the five tests of P-16 and that case pass, does a session of the agent CLI complete under the stand-in of P-6, and does `/dev/kvm` answer `KVM_CREATE_VM`? Where does the agent CLI read and write on Linux outside what SEC-6 gives it: its temporary files, its socket and the managed settings (P-26, P-21)? Answered on 2026-11-25 by workflow runs on the scratch repository and in a Lima VM on the operator's Mac, with stand-in commands and Claude Code 2.1.326. The option does the same with bubblewrap 0.9.0 on both runner labels, in the network namespace too, in each of 8 jobs; it needs `--unshare-user` by name. The VM passes the tests and the case with bubblewrap 0.12.0 and no setup step, the fifteen sessions of P-6 gave there what they gave on the server, and `/dev/kvm` answers `KVM_CREATE_VM` and `KVM_CREATE_VCPU` for a member of group `kvm`; no microVM was booted in it. On Linux the CLI keeps a subagent's task output and its socket under `TMPDIR`, the socket only where every directory on the way belongs to the user or to root and none can be written by the group or by others without the sticky bit, and a session with a subagent completed with `/tmp` read-only. The CLI looks for managed settings under `/etc/claude-code/`: a settings file planted there took effect under `--setting-sources user`, and not once the stand-in hid the directory. With the login the CLI also fetches the account's managed settings, which were empty, and its policy limits, which were not read. | SEC-6, SEC-6b, SEC-6c, SEC-6d, AG-3a, DEV-3, 19.4 |
| P-37 | Does the bubblewrap backend of D-40 do on a kernel what its sources say? Nothing of SEC-6e, SEC-10a and SEC-10b was run: bubblewrap was read in its manual and source at 0.9.0 and 0.12.0, and the filter in Flatpak's source (`docs/research/19-bubblewrap-backend-sources.md`). The questions are answered in the development VM, with bubblewrap 0.12.0. The first three need neither the network nor the operator's login and are answered on the hosted runners of DEV-5 too, with 0.9.0. The first process: does a program that bubblewrap starts with `--as-pid-1` in a new network namespace bind `127.0.0.1`, and does a connection to that port, passed on to a Unix socket which the sandbox shows, reach a listener on the host? Signals: does a SIGTERM sent to the pid of `--json-status-fd` reach that program, and through it the group of the command it started, while `bwrap` lives on, and does a SIGKILL to it leave no process of the sandbox? The filter: does bubblewrap take one program for each error code through `--add-seccomp-fd`, and does each row of the table of SEC-6e do what it says, the x32 row on x86-64 included? Under the filter: do a cold build of the factory repository and one session of the agent CLI run, and which refused call, if any, does each make? The proxy: does `cargo` fetch from the registry, and does a session of the agent CLI complete, through the variables of SEC-10b where no name resolves inside the sandbox? The temporary directory: does the agent CLI place its socket and a subagent's task output under `/tmp` where `TMPDIR` holds `/tmp` and the directory has mode 0700 (SEC-6c)? Answered by M0's prototypes of the sandbox and of the proxy (22), in parts: each issue that builds one of them records in its own pull request what its tests observed (BOOT-1a, D-52). A session of the agent CLI needs the operator's login, and starting it is the operator's step, which is an issue of its own. | SEC-6b, SEC-6c, SEC-6e, SEC-10a, SEC-10b, AG-12; the exit check of M0 (22) |
| P-38 | Three facts that the check command and the ruleset of DEV-8 rest on. What does `cargo deny` do without the advisory database and without a network, and where does it look for the database? What does `cargo mutants` do on macOS with code that the build leaves out? Does a ruleset's bypass cover every rule of that ruleset, and a rule of a second one? Answered on 2026-11-26, on the operator's Mac and on the scratch repository. `cargo deny` 0.22.1 refuses `check --disable-fetch` as an unknown argument, and with `--offline` it fetches nothing. Without that option it clones the database from `github.com` with `git`, or fetches into its clone, and exits with 1 where it cannot. With `--offline` and no database it exits with 1, and `check bans licenses sources` passes. The database is `advisory-dbs/advisory-db-3157b0e258782691` under `CARGO_HOME`, or under a `db-path` that may name a variable, and a clone that `git clone --depth=1` made under that name was taken. With the root `advisory-dbs` on a file system mounted read-only the run fails at the lock file `db.lock`, and with the database mounted read-only below a writable root it passes. A last fetch older than `maximum-db-staleness` fails the run, and a fetch that fails renews that time unless the time of `FETCH_HEAD` is put back. The 0.18.5 that the Mac holds took the same options and then failed to parse the database of the day. `cargo mutants` 27.1.0 reported the five mutants of a function under `cfg(target_os = "linux")` as missed and exited with 2. On the scratch repository, with the operator's token, a pull request of the bypass actor's merged with no approval and neither required check, under a ruleset that names the repository admin with the mode `pull_request`; a direct update of the branch was refused. With a second ruleset that names no bypass actor the merge was refused, with 405 over REST and with an error of type `UNPROCESSABLE` over GraphQL, until that ruleset's check was green. `GET /rules/branches/{branch}` listed each rule once for each ruleset. Not observed: any of the `cargo` cases on Linux, and an account without a bypass. GitHub's documentation of rulesets was not read: the two pages asked for returned 404. | DEV-2a, DEV-3, DEV-8, DEV-14, VAL-1a, DEP-5 |
| P-39 | Where does rustup put a toolchain, what records its components, and does a toolchain's directory work without rustup? Answered on 2026-11-26 on the operator's Mac (macOS 27.0.1, rustup 1.29.3), with a rustup home of its own in a scratch directory. For the channels `1.100.0`, `1.100` and `stable` rustup made the directories `1.100.0-aarch64-apple-darwin`, `1.100-aarch64-apple-darwin` and `stable-aarch64-apple-darwin` below the directory `toolchains` of its home, and each held rustc 1.100.0. The file `lib/rustlib/components` of a toolchain held one line for each installed component: `cargo`, `rustc` and `rust-std` with the Mac's triple after the name, `clippy` and `rustfmt` as `clippy-preview` and `rustfmt-preview` with it, a second target as `rust-std-x86_64-unknown-linux-gnu`, and `rust-src` with no triple. Removing a component or a target took its line away with its files. With the `bin` of the toolchain first on a cleared `PATH` and no rustup, a crate was built, tested, linted and checked by rustfmt in each of four cases: in place, from a copy under another path, from that copy with no write permission on any entry, and through a symbolic link. A `rust-toolchain.toml` beside the crate that named another version changed nothing, and `cargo +1.97.1` was refused as an unknown command. On the toolchain of the minimal profile `cargo clippy` and `cargo fmt` were unknown commands, and a check for a target without its `rust-std` failed. Run in a directory whose `rust-toolchain.toml` named a toolchain that its home lacked, `rustup --version` downloaded and installed it. The six pages of the rustup book that were read name the file's five keys and the grammar of a channel, and say neither where a toolchain lies nor that the `components` file exists. Not observed: Linux, a sandbox, a dated channel, another version of rustup or of the toolchain. | WS-7, 9.5, 16.4 |
| P-40 | Four facts about git that capture, the export and the host's diff rest on. Which directories of a checkout does git take for a repository when it is started in them, what does it run from their `config`, and what does `safe.bareRepository = explicit` change? What does a diff of two trees show for a file that moved? What do the export commands of 9.4 leave when they run a second time on a directory that was written to? What does git make of a tree with two paths that the fold of VAL-5d takes for one, and what does a file system that is not case-sensitive hold of it? Answered on 2026-11-26 by scripts in a scratch directory, on the operator's Mac (macOS 27.0.1, APFS that is not case-sensitive, git 2.56.1) and in a Debian 13.8 container on it (git 2.47.3). git took a directory under a name other than `.git` for a repository where it held `HEAD` with `objects` and `refs`, also where those two were symbolic links or regular files of mode `100755`, and where it held `HEAD` with a file `commondir`; on the Mac also under the names `head`, `Objects` and `REFS`. Started there or two directories below, in the work tree or in a clone, it ran an alias of that directory's `config`, and in every layout but the one with `commondir` its fsmonitor on `git status`. It did the same where the `config` was a symbolic link to a file outside the directory or named such a file in `include.path`. At the root of a checkout it took the `.git` instead, and in a copy of that tree without its `.git` it took the root and ran the alias. With `safe.bareRepository = explicit` in the global configuration, in the system configuration or on the command line it refused each layout that it had taken, also where the directory's own `config` set the value back; set in the surrounding repository alone, the setting did nothing. Under it an ordinary repository, a linked worktree, the inside of a `.git` and a bare repository as a remote by path worked as before, and git started inside a bare repository needed `--git-dir` or `GIT_DIR`. `git diff-tree` detected no rename without an option, also with `diff.renames` set, and `git diff` detected them; with detection a moved `clippy.toml`, a moved workflow and a moved symbolic link were one entry each, and without it a deletion and an addition. The export commands, run again on a directory that had been written to, left the changed files and exited with 1, and with `-f` they left a planted file. `fsck --strict` and `index-pack --strict` accepted seven trees with two paths of one fold. On Linux the export held both paths of each. On the Mac it held one file of each pair of files, merged two directories into one with exit status 0, failed on a symbolic link beside a directory, and a clone was dirty from the start; the entries of a layout that lay in two directories, `fix` and `FIX`, became one directory there, whose alias git ran. The factory's sandbox, the server, and a program that reads a repository without the `git` CLI were not run. | WS-8, WS-9, WS-12, WS-13, WS-14, WS-15, VAL-5a, VAL-5d, VAL-5f, 12.4, 19.4, 20.1 |
| P-41 | (M1a) Does a subagent have a tool that its session lacks, where the session is given a tool list (AG-22)? The sessions of probe P-26 started subagents under the list `Read,Glob,Grep,Agent`, and nobody compared the tools that a subagent had with that list. It is observed by the first run of the contract test in M1a (19.4), on the version that it runs on. | AG-22, 19.4 |
| P-42 | (M1a) Does a new `claude setup-token` end the token that was made before it? Where it does not, how is a token ended, and how long does one that nobody ended stay valid? SEC-13 has the operator re-issue the token after a hit of the literal-token scan, and nobody observed what that does to the old one. The operator answers it, since it takes their own login and a browser: with a second token made, a session that is given the first one either completes or is refused. No session of the factory is needed for it. | 13.5, SEC-13, 23.3 |
| P-43 | (M1b) Which file names does each tool of the DEV-2 set read unasked, the toolchain and mise among them, from the root of a workspace and from the directory of a crate, and which wins where two lie side by side? Does an argument or a variable that names a tool's configuration leave the other files without effect? Answered on 2026-11-26 by scripts on the operator's Mac, which ran rustfmt, clippy, cargo, rustup, mise, `cargo nextest`, `cargo deny`, typos, `cargo mutants`, `cargo llvm-cov` and `cargo doc` in trees of their own: each of the seven names that the review of draft 4 gave is read, four of them win over the protected name beside them, `.tool-versions` and `.config/mise.toml` are read below `mise.toml`, and `.config/nextest.toml` is the one file that nextest reads; rustfmt reads two names, `cargo deny` a second name and an exceptions file under three, typos five names and every `.ignore`, mise nineteen names and a `.miserc.toml`; an argument leaves the other files without effect for rustfmt, nextest, `cargo mutants` and rustup, for the configuration of typos though not for its ignore files, and not for clippy or for the exceptions file of `cargo deny`; `mise exec` and `mise run` trusted a directory of untrusted configuration without asking. Not observed: Linux, a case-sensitive file system, and the versions that `mise.toml` will pin. | 11.3, VAL-5j, BOOT-4, DEV-2b, 23.3 |
| P-44 | (M1b) Which changed files of a pull request does GitHub's page show no content of when no attribute asks for it, and at what size? Answered in part on 2026-11-26 by four pull requests on the scratch repository, read without a login: no content for a binary file that is new, changed, removed or text again; `Cargo.lock` and every file whose path holds a name of GitHub's list for generated files collapsed behind "Load diff", Rust source among them, and no `.rs` file collapsed by a header; a file collapsed from 400 lines of diff where the diff held eight files or more, and from 16,000 bytes where it held 25; every file collapsed after 5,700 to 6,000 lines or 200,000 to 220,000 bytes of rendered diff; a removed text file collapsed. Not observed: the view of someone who is logged in, which `.gitattributes` the page obeys, and the limits of a whole pull request. | 11.3, VAL-5i, VAL-14c, 13.1, 23.3 |
| P-45 | What does a rule on `factory/**` do to the writes that the factory makes to a work branch, for an App on no bypass list, and what does the App read of the rule before the branch exists? Answered on 2026-11-26 by two scripts on the scratch repository, and on 2026-11-27 by a third, with the scratch App's installation token; the operator's token made and removed each rule. Under a ruleset, one rule at a time: `creation` refused the create of a ref; `update`, `pull_request`, `required_signatures` and `required_status_checks` refused every push; `non_fast_forward` refused a force-update, by a push with a lease and through the REST API; `deletion` refused the delete; `required_linear_history` refused the push of a merge commit, and the create of a ref at the head of `main`, which has 14 merge commits behind it, while it accepted a create at a commit with none; `required_status_checks` refused the create too, unless the rule set `do_not_enforce_on_create`. Every refused create got `422 Reference update failed` and no rule was named. Every refused push, update and delete named its rule. `GET /rules/branches/{branch}`, under `metadata=read`, listed each rule for a name that no ref had, with its ruleset, also for a ruleset that names every branch and for one with a bypass actor, and nothing for a disabled ruleset. A classic branch protection rule on `factory/*` was in no response of that read. The App was refused `branchProtectionRules` over GraphQL and the protection endpoint of the REST API, and read `refUpdateRule` and `protected` of a branch that existed. A classic rule did not stop the create. With nothing chosen it stopped a force-update and the delete, and with required reviews or a lock every push. GitHub refused to create a rule for required deployments without the environment, a rule for a commit message pattern or a branch name pattern, a ruleset in evaluate mode and a classic rule that restricts pushes. A ruleset for every branch that excludes `refs/heads/factory/**` listed nothing for a work branch, and the App's create, force-update and delete of one went through. The operator's token read the same list of rules as the App's, also on a ruleset that the operator's role may bypass. Not tried: a ruleset of an organisation, the 14 other rule types of GitHub's reference, the merge queue, code scanning and those of a push ruleset among them, and a create of an existing ref under a rule other than `creation`. | GH-22, 8.2, 8.6, 20.1, 23.3 |

### 23.3 Known weak points

- Human review is the main defence against a plausible but wrong change, and review quality falls as volume rises. The review-queue limit and the size budget are blunt tools.
- There is one human, so the person who approves the issue is also the person who approves the code, and their own pull requests bypass the ruleset of 20.3. On the factory repository a second ruleset still holds those to CI (DEV-8).
- Reviewer agents are lenient by default and unevaluated until M3.
- The agent CLI's protocol and flags change often. The tested range and the contract test contain this but do not remove it. Through M1 the range holds one version, and the test runs when a person starts it (AG-5, CLI-8).
- In `subscription` mode a token valid for a year sits in the environment of a process that may be subverted, and the CLI runs without bare mode. The mitigations (model-requests-only scope, egress allowlist, literal-token scan, contract test with its lock-down cases) reduce this but do not remove it. The scan finds the exact bytes of the token and no encoded form of it (SEC-13). Nobody observed whether issuing the token again ends the one before it (P-42); until someone has, a token that a hit named may stay valid for the rest of its year, and after a re-issue the scan no longer searches for it, in the workspace of another run either. Bare mode is announced to become the default for `-p`, which may force a change to this mode. The model host is also a way out that the proxy cannot see into (SEC-11). A worker that holds the token may be able to have the provider search or fetch the web for it: through the CLI's own tool, which no session has (AG-22), or by a request that it writes itself. Nobody observed whether the provider does that for a subscription token, and no probe asks (D-46).
- The plan's limits are shared with the operator's own interactive use and assume ordinary individual usage; a busy daemon may exhaust them and stall both.
- Lights-out rests on class predicates and automated evidence. For user interfaces and service behaviour this specification does not yet define that evidence, so those kinds of work stay reviewed until it does.
- The seccomp filter of level `process` is a deny-list (SEC-6e). A system call that the kernel gains later is allowed until someone adds it to the table, and at that level the host's kernel is all that stands between a worker and the host (SEC-6b).
- A directory with the layout of a git repository runs the commands of its `config` wherever git is started in it. Capture rejects one that a run brings in, and every change below one that the base tree holds (WS-13). One that a human commits is left to a git setting on the operator's machines that nothing checks (WS-15). So is a file outside the directory that a layout of the base tree reads its configuration from, until the operator protects it, and a directory that becomes a layout only when something creates an entry that git does not track. A pair of paths with one fold, or a layout, that a merge puts together from a branch and a default branch that moved is seen by the test-merge of 12.3 and by nothing after it: a merge that the operator makes between two of them is not tested. No program that reads a repository without the `git` CLI was tried, with that setting or without it (P-40).
- The listing of a flag names at most 50 paths for one protected-path pattern (VAL-3b). Where a pattern names a whole tree, as `crates/**` and `xtask/**` do on the factory repository (BOOT-4), a candidate that adds fifty files which sort before a path pushes that path out of its group. The summary still counts it, and the journal and the diff hold it.
- GitHub's page collapses changes that no rule of the host names. It collapsed every file that its own detection takes for generated, by a part of its path or by a line of its content: a worker that names a file `Cargo.lock.rs`, or puts it below a directory `__generated__`, has ordinary source collapsed behind "Load diff" (P-44). The host holds no copy of those rules (VAL-14c). The page also took a text file for binary by its extension, collapsed a removed text file, and showed a change of mode in the header alone. What stands against this is the button that the page puts in the place of each such file, the listing of a protected path, and from M2 a reviewer that reads the host's diff.
- The limits at which the host calls a file large are those of the page as someone who is not logged in gets it, found in part on compare views (P-44). The two limits for a whole diff are known only between two experiments each, and the two figures in bytes agree only if a collapsed file counts towards the limit. The operator's own view, which `.gitattributes` the page obeys, and the limits of a whole pull request were not observed.
- The lists name the files that each tool of the checks read unasked in the versions that probe P-43 ran, on macOS. A later version of a tool can read another name, as `cargo deny` gained two between 0.18.5 and 0.22.1, and nothing notices it: the flag and the structural test of DEV-2b both go by the names of the probe. The same holds for a tool that DEV-2 has not chosen yet.
- `mise exec` and `mise run` trusted a directory of untrusted configuration without asking, and then ran what its files name (P-43). Wherever either runs on a branch, the build scripts and the tests of that branch run too, so this adds no reach that the code did not have. The server and a worker's sandbox hold no mise.
- A classic branch protection rule that matches a work branch is read by nothing before the branch exists (GH-22). The readiness report says so and the operator looks by hand. Where one is missed, or added later, a run finds it at the write that GitHub refuses: with required reviews or a lock that is the first push of publish, after a whole implementer session. A ruleset that is added after a claim's read is met in the same way.
- Every verify is a cold build. On a large repository this may dominate cycle time until a safe seeded cache exists.
- Most published throughput and quality figures for such systems are self-reported. No target in this specification is derived from them.

---

## Appendix A. Mapping to prior art

| This spec | OpenAI Symphony | StrongDM Attractor |
|---|---|---|
| Poll / dispatch / reconcile tick (8.3) | Same loop | — |
| `state:*` labels (5.2) | Tracker states Todo / In Progress / Human Review / Merging / Rework / Done | — |
| Status comment (5.4) | Workpad comment | — |
| Typed stages with result contract (7.3, 10.5) | — | Graph nodes with outcomes |
| Plan gate, human review (7.4, 12.2) | Human Review state | Human gate nodes |
| Holdout scenarios (11.7) | — | Scenarios as holdout tests |
| `.factory/policy.toml` (16) | `WORKFLOW.md` | DOT pipeline file |

Symphony's Elixir reference now includes a minimal GitHub Issues adapter (open/closed only, no dependencies); it is prior art for the adapter boundary, not for a label-driven workflow. Fabro (MIT, Rust, Attractor lineage) is worth reading as implementation prior art before M1.

## Appendix B. Research and review

The reports in `docs/research/` hold the evidence and source URLs behind this specification:

- `01-state-of-the-art`, `02-orchestration-architecture`, `03-specs-and-validation`, `04-github-integration`, `05-rust-implementation`, `06-security-governance-ops`: the six research angles.
- `07-gaps`: contradictions between the reports and how they were resolved. Where this specification departs from one of its resolutions, 23.1 or 13.6 records why.
- `08-fact-check`: 66 load-bearing claims checked against primary sources (56 confirmed, 9 partly, 1 wrong; corrections are applied here).
- `09-spec-review`: the 59 findings of the adversarial review of draft 1, each with the reviewer's proposed fix and the sceptic's verdict. This draft addresses each one, not always with the proposed fix.
- `10-spec-recheck`: the 47 problems a recheck found in draft 2 (unresolved findings and contradictions the rewrite introduced), addressed in draft 3.
- `14-server-facts`: what the operator's server is, observed on 2026-11-20 and set against the host requirements of 4.4. Reports 11 to 13 are on `research/*` branches and are cited from `probes.md`.
- `15-session-sizes`: how large the operator's interactive sessions on the factory repository were, in model calls, minutes and API-equivalent dollars, measured on 2026-11-21 for the session limits of section 14 (D-22).
- `16-spec-draft-4-review`: the 51 findings of a review of draft 4 as it stood on 2026-11-22, in four lenses (consistency, evidence, security, and whether M0 and M1 can be built from the text), each with the reviewer's proposed fix. No second reader judged them. B1, B5 and B15 are addressed and E4 in part (D-39), E5, E7 and E8 by probe P-36, B2 by D-40, B6, C1 and C2 by D-41 with probe P-38, B3 and B4 by D-42 with probe P-39, E3, S2, S5 and S6 by D-43 with probe P-40, B7, B8, B9 and C8 by D-44, S4, E2, C3 and B10 by D-45 with probe P-8, E1, C7, S8, B11, E9 and S12 by D-46, B12 and S7 by D-47, S1, S9 and S11 by D-48, S3 and S10 by D-49 with probes P-43 and P-44, and C4, C5, C6, C9, C10, E6, E10 to E14, B13 and B14 by D-50. With that each of the 51 is addressed, E4 in part.
- `17-scratch-app`: the scratch App's id, slug and installation id and where its key lies, with the checklist of its creation set against what GitHub returned on 2026-11-25 (GH-11a, GH-11b).
- `18-release-tooling`: what the book of `cargo-dist` and the manual of `gh` say about the release workflow and the verification of an attestation, read on 2026-11-25 and not observed (DEV-12, DEV-13, D-39).
- `19-bubblewrap-backend-sources`: what the manual and the source of bubblewrap, Flatpak's seccomp filter, Moby's default seccomp profile and the crate `seccompiler` say on the points that the bubblewrap backend rests on, read on 2026-11-26 and not run (SEC-6e, SEC-10a, SEC-10b, D-40).
- `20-stack-resolution`: the throwaway package that names every crate of the table of 19.2, and the versions that `cargo generate-lockfile` resolved for it on 2026-11-27 (19.2, 22).
